6 Antique Trends Designers Are Watching for 2027

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Above: The kitchen in designer Phillip Thomas’s Bellport retreat.

Do the words “antique” and “trend” really belong in the same sentence? In many ways, a great antique shop feels like the antidote to trend fatigue: a space where the true must-haves are hidden in dusty corners, with part of the fun in the thrill of the hunt. Yet, as we round out 2026, there’s no denying the current design cycle is casting off perfection for patina. Antiques are en vogue, and top designers are keeping a close eye on the periods, pieces, and yes, trends, that are poised to find new life in 2027.

“The return of interest in antiques reflects a trend towards more personal, unique interiors, and away from strict modern rooms which can often be cool and generic,” says ELLE Decor A-List designer Jamie Drake. This shift paves the way for more playful interactions between pieces, unexpected layering, and unabashed personality.

Other experts second the motion for more character. “I’m seeing clients really drawn to antiques with more to them than meets the eye: pieces that have a bit of humor or whimsy, or pieces with great provenance,” says ELLE Decor Rising Star William Cullum. (He lists a “ridiculously giant butterfly light fixture” from New Orleans, rattan chairs from Bing Crosby’s estate, and a disco ball from Cyndi Lauper’s Bonham’s sale in his list of recent acquisitions—professional and personal.)

Luckily for the amateur antiquer, not all trending vintage styles require a substantial space commitment or glamorous provenance—although an interesting backstory helps. “Antiques are always about a story: where you found them, who owned them, and how that story will continue on after us,” Cullum says.

Ahead, we asked top designers and experts to share the most inspiring antique trends of the moment. From neoclassic mirrors to hand-painted chests paired with modern art, their takes are sure to inform your next estate sale rummage, or inspire you to restyle an old heirloom.

We called mirrored surfaces a major trend to watch for fall 2026, and antique trends have followed suit. Unexpected (or better yet, artfully manipulated) reflections can add rich dimension and movement to any space, but in this category, antiques create a window to the past that’s tough to beat.

“One of the easiest ways to add a sense of history to a room is to include an antique mirror,” says Drake. “Whether you choose a sober and strict neo-classic piece or a more flamboyant baroque one, reflections of the past add depth and sparkle.”

“One trend I’m observing is an uptick in antique dowry chests,” antiques dealer Ali Mahon says. From Pennsylvania Dutch to Swedish Folk and Spanish carved, she’s noticed clients gravitating toward more delicately adorned storage over spare. With thoughtfully mixed furniture (from style to period) becoming more desirable than matching sets, Mahon sees more hand-painted antique surfaces in our future: “With their decorative tulips, leaves, birds, and hearts, dowry chests create a colorful splash in any room.”

ELLE Decor Rising Star Reagan Gail Struble agrees—although delicate designs are not a requirement of the trend, in her book: “Something about a playful wash of color on an awesome old piece of furniture is very alluring, especially when you can see the age and texture of the wood peeking through,” she says. “Hand-painted case pieces add a little punch of charm to any room.”

Luckily, these antiques can be just as functional as they are fashionable. Mahon recommends using a painted chest as a coffee table, at the foot of the bed, or in a space that could benefit from a bit more romance (and a few extra drawers). When styling your find in a modern space, Struble says that contrast is key: try a “chippy Swedish chest of drawers” with an unexpected photograph or painting above it.

According to ELLE Decor A-List designer Nick Olsen, Art Deco pieces are setting the tone for 2027 antique trends—although, if you ask him, they were never out. “I'm particularly inspired by the [art deco influence in the] Gujarat palace in India,” he says. “I'm fascinated by how every element seems to have a wide border: marble floors, carpets, walls of veneer and mohair velvet upholstery.”

According to Olsen, using Art Deco design compositions can do as much for a room as actual pieces from the period. “I like the idea of contrasting borders: solid textures punctuated by smaller graphic prints on furniture and more delicate lighting,” he explains. And while borders are easy to create with paint, paneling, and carpet seaming, Olsen challenges clients to push the Art Deco agenda further by bordering with luxe materials.

Whether you’re new to antiquing or simply trying to add more character to a space, lighting is an excellent place to start. “Lighting is the jewelry of the home, so it’s one of my favorite places to bring in something vintage,” says A-Lister Corey Damen Jenkins. “An Art Deco sconce or an antique brass lantern has a warmth to it that’s hard to find in something brand new.”

Adding to an existing space? Jenkins advises starting small: “Swap one overhead fixture or put a pair of vintage lamps on a console, and you’ll be surprised how much the whole room shifts,” he says. “It’s an easy way to add history without starting over.”

“In light of America’s 250th birthday, Americana in general is in vogue,” says Mahon. Among her clients, the trend has manifested in quilts and textiles, portraits of sea captains, trade signs, and ironstone—culminating in an aesthetic she describes as “elevated country—not corny.”

If you’ve noticed that no particular design period seems to reign supreme among designers’ top antique trend picks, it’s not an accident. Heading into 2027, there’s a strong argument to be made that mixed-period design is more influential than any singular reference.

“I love a room that doesn’t belong to any one decade,” Jenkins says. “A nineteenth-century chest with a 1970s lamp on top, or a Lucite chair pulled up to an antique desk. Those are the pairings that make people stop and look twice.” Drake adds that seemingly out-of-place seating is an easy way to add intrigue. “I love including antique chairs as sculptural notes, contrasted against modern upholstery and tables,” he says.

But when it comes to antiquing, there’s really only so much one can plan for. Letting a great find lead the way often turns out to be a better strategy than arriving to an estate sale with a rigid checklist. The best way to keep up with top designers when it comes to antiquing this year? Find a piece that has something to say, place it where it seems to belong, and then try it where it doesn’t.

Grace McCarty is a freelance Associate Digital Editor at ELLE Decor, where she covers design trends, shopping, and culture. She previously held a staff position at SELF Magazine, where she focused on beauty, style, and wellness. Her work has also appeared in Glamour and Parade.

7-Minute Yoga to Practice While Sitting at Your Desk

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Yoga while sitting at your desk can be a simple way to soothe physical tension during the work day. This practice was created by instructor Laia Bové, who has taught students of all levels for more than a decade.

If you spend a lot of time hunched over your desk, you already know that it’s important to break the stagnation of long hours in front of your computer screen. But sometimes the time you think you’ll have to release the tension in your neck, shoulders, and upper back—likely before or after work—disappears due to other responsibilities, distractions, or just plain exhaustion.

This 7-minute yoga practice was made with the idea that you can sneak in a little yoga during work. These stretches target the most common areas where tension and aches arise when sitting at a desk and act as a quick reset. By the end of the seven minutes, you’ll feel a lot looser throughout your upper back and maybe even a little more ready to take on the rest of your day.

You can do this sequence between tasks, meetings, or calls, or after you power off your laptop and before you move on to other activities. You don’t need any special clothes or equipment—in fact, you don’t even need to get up from your chair.

Sit in your desk chair with your feet planted on the floor. Exhale and lower your chin toward your chest. Inhale and lift your chin toward the ceiling. Repeat that a few times.

Next time you exhale and lower your chin toward your chest, keep your chin lowered as you turn your head toward the right, as if you were drawing your nose toward your right shoulder. Take a breath here, then return to center with your chin still lowered. Then draw your nose toward your left shoulder. Return to center, then lift your chin to neutral.

Clasp your hands and rest them lightly behind your head. Point your elbows toward the sides of the room to prevent your hands from placing too much pressure on your head. Inhale as you lift your chest and draw your elbows slightly behind your chest, if possible, and slightly arch your back in a Cow Pose variation.

Keeping your hands where they are, exhale as you round your back, bring your elbows toward each other, and draw your navel toward your spine in a seated variation of Cat Pose. Repeat another round of this seated Cat-Cow variation.

Keeping your hands clasped behind your head, exhale as you lean toward the left, feeling a stretch along your right side. Inhale and return to center. Exhale as you bend toward the right, feeling a stretch along your left side. Inhale and return to center, then release your arms.

Place your fingertips on your shoulders and point your elbows toward the sides of the room. Draw your elbows toward each other in front of your chest as if you want them to touch, then lift them to point toward the ceiling, then point them toward the back wall, and then point them at the floor, moving in a large circular motion. Continue drawing elbow circles as many times as you like. Then switch directions.

Exhale and tilt your head toward the right, drawing your right ear toward your right shoulder. Inhale and return to center. Exhale and tilt your head toward the left, drawing your left ear toward your left shoulder. Repeat a few times.

Reach your arms out to your sides in a T shape, then cross your arms and wrap them around your chest as if you’re giving yourself a big hug. Take a few breaths here in Eagle Arms variation. Then repeat with the opposite arm on top. Release your arms.

With your arms relaxed at your sides, draw your shoulders up toward your ears and squeeze your muscles as tightly as you can—maybe you even scrunch the muscles in your face. Take a big breath in, then release your shoulders down and relax the muscles you were tightening. Repeat one more Shoulder Squeeze.

Take a big breath in through your nose and out through your mouth. Repeat as many times as needed until you’re ready to resume your day.

59-Room Andaz Turks & Caicos At Grace Bay Opening April 2027, Now Bookable

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

The much delayed Andaz Turks & Caicos is getting closer to opening. The hotel has now moved forward the date as of which it’s accepting reservations, and we’ve also learned more details about the on-property amenities. Let’s cover all the details, as we’re potentially under five months from the opening…

The Andaz Turks & Caicos at Grace Bay is accepting reservations for stays as of April 1, 2027. This will mark Hyatt’s first hotel in Turks & Caicos, and the first Andaz property in the Caribbean. For that matter, it’s one of the only Andaz properties that’s currently in the pipeline outside of Asia, as the brand has really lost steam.

The 5.5-acre resort will offer four restaurants and bars, three outdoor pools, a spa, a kids club, a fitness center, tennis and pickleball courts, and more. The Andaz will be comprised of 59 rooms and 73 branded residences, so at least the hotel portion of the property will be boutique. It’ll be located directly on Grace Bay, known for offering one of the most beautiful stretches of beach in the Caribbean.

As far as the culinary concepts go, the Andaz Turks & Caicos will feature the following:

Runa Spa & Wellness, the spa at the Andaz, will be around 7,000 square feet. It will have five treatment rooms, along with a sauna, cold plunge, relaxation area, and more.

The opening of this resort has been majorly delayed. It was initially announced in 2018 with a 2021 opening, but the timeline slipped by six years, presumably due to a combination of the pandemic, plus… well, how hotel construction projects often work out. Hopefully there are no additional delays beyond the current planned spring 2027 opening.

With the Andaz Turks & Caicos at Grace Bay bookable, what are rates like? For those redeeming points, this is a Category 8 World of Hyatt property, meaning a free night redemption in a standard room costs 35,000-75,000 points per night.

World of Hyatt’s new award chart sure makes top end redemptions expensive, unfortunately! As of now the hotel is primarily bookable between April and November of 2027, so this doesn’t really cover peak season. For most dates, I currently see rates of either 35,000 points, 45,000 points, or 55,000 points per night. So value here is highly dependent on which of those rates you can find.

When it comes to cash rates, those currently start at around $900-1,300 per night, and that depends on when you’re staying. That’s inclusive of the service charge, but exclusive of the taxes.

If you are looking to book a cash stay here, I’d recommend doing so through the Hyatt Privé program, which offers extra perks like room upgrades, complimentary breakfast, and a hotel credit.

I suppose good options on Grace Bay are limited, and this hotel is boutique. But at these rates, they better offer something significantly better than the typical Andaz experience, in my opinion.

Turks & Caicos is absolutely beautiful, and it’s an easy getaway from the United States, given how close it is to Florida. Historically, the country hasn’t had that many points hotels, though that’s slowly starting to change.

Several years back, we saw the opening of the Ritz-Carlton Turks & Caicos. It has an amazing location on Grace Bay and the grounds of the property are beautiful, but it otherwise doesn’t feel very luxurious, and lacks personality.

So it’ll be nice to have another points property, and I look forward to learning more about the Andaz. Maybe I’m overly optimistic, but I think the Andaz might just end up offering a better experience than the Ritz-Carlton, at least based on the design renderings, and also the more boutique scale of the property. Andaz really is quite an inconsistent brand.

I of course should acknowledge that many people are understandably frustrated by the way that Grace Bay has developed over the years, given all the huge concrete towers that have taken shape along the beach. It has definitely lost its charm a bit, though the beach is still gorgeous.

Even beyond Grace Bay, we’re seeing some other great new options in Turks & Caicos. In 2025, we saw the opening of Salterra Turks & Caicos, a Marriott Luxury Collection property. This is great for those who want a bit more secluded of a trip, since South Caicos Island is a bit less crowded than the area around Providenciales. This is also where Sailrock Resort is located, which is an independent hotel affiliated with Small Luxury Hotels of the World (SLH).

There’s also a Waldorf Astoria Turks & Caicos planned for a 2028 opening, located on Dellis Cay, a nearby island that’s not as developed, which can be reached by a 25-minute boat transfer from Providenciales. That should also be a great option, especially with how the Waldorf Astoria portfolio is developing. However, don’t count on that timeline sticking either.

The Andaz Turks & Caicos is accepting reservations for stays as of April 2027, and it will be located on the famous Grace Bay. The property will have just 59 hotel rooms, in addition to residences, so I like how it’s on a smaller scale than some other properties.

This should be a very nice addition to the Caribbean points hotel market. Hopefully we don’t see any additional delays with the opening…

What do you make of the upcoming Andaz Turks & Caicos?

Rivian spinout Also was working on an e-moto project in Kenya — until it walked away

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

Also, the micromobility startup spun out of Rivian in 2025, quietly started and then walked away from an electric two-wheeler pilot project in Kenya earlier this year, as it sought to focus its resources on its consumer e-bike and autonomous vehicle ambitions, TechCrunch has learned.

The project received grant funding from the Shell Foundation, an independent charity established by the British oil and gas giant Shell plc. A section of the Shell Foundation’s website that has since been removed said the project’s goal was to “enable and de-risk global OEM entry into Africa’s EV market by piloting a locally integrated electric two-wheeler platform in Kenya, with potential to scale across East Africa.”

According to the deleted section, Also was expected to work on modular battery designs and distributed EV charging, and planned on “developing a purpose-built electric motorcycle for African riders.”

The opportunity for electric motorcycles seems ripe on the continent, which has a massive two-wheeler market. African mobility company Spiro recently said it has more than 130,000 electric motorcycles operating across seven countries, along with 2,500 battery-swapping stations. This boom has drawn interest from Chinese mobility companies and investors, which are pouring resources into the space.

In a September email, Also told TechCrunch that it had “explored this project” with an unnamed partner, but ultimately “decided to focus on its core roadmap” after completing only “some early-stage work.” Also said the grant funding it received from the Shell Foundation “will follow the project to support its ongoing development,” but declined to offer specifics.

The Shell Foundation told TechCrunch in an email that the project was “intended to generate evidence on driver income uplift potential, operational performance and commercial viability to deepen the company’s understanding of the market opportunity.” But when TechCrunch asked for more specifics, the Shell Foundation declined, citing a nondisclosure agreement with Also. Days later, the project disappeared from the Shell Foundation’s website.

During an interview last month at TechCrunch Disrupt, Also co-founder and president Chris Yu said the Kenya pilot project initially made sense given Also’s mission to build small, purpose-built vehicles that are “first and foremost cost-optimized to do the job that needs to be done in a particular market.”

But other projects took precedence, Yu said, especially the development of small autonomous vehicles — which Also is working on as part of a partnership with DoorDash.

“We piloted and ultimately decided, largely because of just urgency and pace around autonomy, frankly, that our attention would be best, in the near term, served fully focused” on that, he said, adding that he thinks there’s a “high chance that autonomy will show up first, and at a broader scale in these types of purpose-built smaller vehicles than at scale in passenger vehicles.”

Also walked away from the Africa project during a somewhat rocky first year of independence.

Supply chain problems forced the company to repeatedly delay deliveries of its first product, a premium e-bike called the TM-B, frustrating customers who had reserved one. What made matters worse was Also’s relative silence. The company said little for much of 2026 about when the bikes would arrive — a communication failure its leadership now acknowledges was a mistake.

“I think we did a pretty poor job communicating to our reservation holders through the summer when they were all waiting, and we got called out for that,” Yu said in the interview. “But I think it’s actually great. It’s really hard to earn the opportunity for people to care, and to tell us what they think, and to help us improve.”

Getting its first product launch back on track is crucial for Also, which has ambitions far beyond e-bikes. The company plans to sell commercial cargo bikes to companies like Amazon, including four-wheeled models, and has the partnership with DoorDash. These products are next in the company’s pipeline, Yu said, and represent bigger revenue opportunities.

But the TM-B comes first. It’s Also’s most visible product to consumers, and its striking design has drawn plenty of curiosity.

Yu said no single problem was responsible for delaying the bikes, which finally started shipping to customers in early October. Instead, Also spent much of the year playing supply chain Whac-A-Mole, a particularly difficult challenge given the TM-B’s complexity. Yu described the bike as “kind of a computer on wheels.”

At one point, Also found itself competing for the same raw materials used to make printed circuit boards for AI data centers. At other times, the company struggled to navigate certification requirements with different government agencies.

Yu said the scope of these problems, and the pace at which new ones popped up — almost weekly — made it difficult to give customers reliable updates about why their bikes were delayed.

“The entire team is like running at a million miles an hour to get these things produced and shipped to customers, and then it’s also really dynamic as well,” he said. “I think what we could have done better is just [say], straight up: ‘We’re chasing down these things, we’ll have an update shortly.’”

That didn’t happen at first, but Also has steadily improved its communication, earning some goodwill from customers on Reddit. The company will need it as it works through the inevitable kinks that come with a first product. Early customers have already reported problems, including delivery damage and noticeable battery drain.

Former High Museum of Art executive sentenced to 12 months in prison for embezzlement

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Auguste Rodin's The Shade at the High Museum of Art, Atlanta Photo: Marc Merlin, via Flickr

Brady Lum, a former chief operating officer at Atlanta's High Museum of Art, was sentenced to 12 months in federal prison on Monday (2 November) for embezzling more than $600,000 from the institution. The sentencing comes as the High celebrates its centennial and its parent organisation, the Woodruff Arts Center, conducts a major corporate fundraising campaign.

Federal prosecutors said Lum diverted museum funds to pay for personal purchases, including guitars, music lessons and woodworking equipment. He concealed the expenditures through falsified invoices, reimbursement requests and accounting adjustments. The scheme began in September 2019, less than nine months after Lum joined the museum, and continued until its discovery in November 2025.

Lum paid full restitution of $607,688.18 to the High and voluntarily paid approximately $500,000 more to cover costs the museum incurred while uncovering and investigating his criminal activity.

During Monday's hearing, US District judge Michael L. Brown focused extensively on the deterrent purpose of sentencing. Although Brown said he did not believe Lum was likely to reoffend, he emphasised the court's responsibility to discourage similar financial crimes by others in positions of trust.

The judge acknowledged similarities between his own professional and socioeconomic background and Lum's, discussing how sentencing structures have historically been shaped by educated, professionally successful individuals whose circumstances resemble those of many white-collar defendants.

Brown also considered Lum's prompt restitution after the misconduct was discovered, distinguishing between voluntarily returning stolen funds before detection and repaying them after being caught. The latter, he indicated, carried less weight in assessing accountability.

Lum addressed the court and apologised for his conduct, expressing regret for disappointing his family and community. The gallery appeared to consist largely of relatives, friends and supporters of Lum.

Contacted for comment following Monday's sentencing, a spokesperson for the Woodruff provided the following statement: "We respect the court’s decision and remain focused on fulfilling our mission, responsibly managing our resources and connecting deeply with the audience we serve."

Both the Woodruff and the High declined to provide substantive information about financial safeguards introduced following Lum's misconduct, or whether they intend to publish a remediation plan addressing financial controls, governance reforms and oversight procedures.

Wendy Halsted Beard was arrested last year after an FBI investigation

Michael Maurello may face up to 20 years in prison for his financial misdeeds at the museum

"He did his job, but his job turned out to be assisting a criminal fraud," a New York district court judge said

Michael Maurello is also expected to pay back what he he stole from the museum

Toyota Confirms New GR Celica By Announcing 2027 World Rally Championship Car

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

The heralded sports car nameplate will finally return as part of Toyota’s racing outfit—and then, presumably, on a production car too.

On Tuesday, Toyota officially confirmed that the Celica will return to the Japanese automaker’s family, but its first appearance seemingly won’t be as a production car. Instead, Toyota’s motorsport outfits—Toyota Racing and Gazoo Racing Toyota—will enter two models in the 2027 World Rally Championship ... and one will be a Toyota GR Celica.

The Celica's return has been rumored for years, but this is the first time Toyota has used the official name. Late last year, Cooper Ericksen, Toyota North America’s senior vice president of product planning and strategy, said a model that could be the Celica was in “pretty advanced development.” Now, Toyota says it will have a Celica WRC car ready by late January, when the Rally Monte Carlo will kick off the season.

Welsh rally driver and 2026 WRC title winner Elfyn Evans will head up Toyota Racing’s WRC effort alongside former Gazoo Racing driver Oliver Solberg. Solberg was the runner-up in the 2026 WRC season, narrowly missing the crown after a crash on the final power stage at the Rally Italia Sardegna. Finnish WRC driver Sami Pajari will also join the team.

“Before now, there was a specific regulation that said that for Rally1 homologation, there’s only one model allowed per manufacturer. That regulation has been changed to say, as long as the main mechanical components remain identical, you can do a variant model of your Rally1,” Tom Fowler, technical director of Toyota Gazoo Racing, said to DirtFish. “We had this opportunity to use this as a marketing tool and as a tool to increase the participation and to make things more interesting. There’s not really a big change; in the past we have run four, five or six cars on an event—but they’ve all been the same. This time, instead of running identical cars, we’re going to run two cars.”

Mechanically, this means the current GR Yaris Rally1 and the incoming GR Celica Rally1 will be largely similar. 2027 WRC Rally1 cars will continue to use a spaceframe chassis with turbocharged 1.6-liter inline-four engines and homologated suspension, running gear, and brakes from the current Rally2 category.

Toyota has yet to provide any other details about the Celica rally car beyond its name, but homologation regulations for the 2027 WRC season require production of at least 10 units within 24 months of the homologation date. Additionally, Toyota must be capable of supplying at least 10 race-ready WRC27-type cars per calendar year to customers.

Both of those regulations pertain to rally-specific models, not road-going models. Hence, the announcement that a Celica is coming to WRC doesn’t necessarily confirm the imminent arrival of a road car. Toyota executives previously confirmed the Celica would likely have an internal combustion engine, but declined to provide specific details, and the Celica’s shape, power figures, drivetrain layout, and number of seats have yet to be announced.

The Celica has a long history in WRC, first competing in 1972. Toyota entered the Celica in WRC through the 1980s and 1990s, with Carlos Sainz Sr. taking the drivers' titles in 1990 and 1992. Juha Kankkunen and Didier Auriol continued the Celica winning streak in 1993 and 1994. Toyota’s factory-supported Celica WRC run ended after the automaker earned a year-long WRC ban in 1995 over an illegal turbo restrictor, though privateers continued racing the model later in the 1990s.

A New York transplant hailing from the Pacific Northwest, Emmet White has a passion for anything that goes: cars, bicycles, planes, and motorcycles. After learning to ride at 17, Emmet worked in the motorcycle industry before joining Autoweek in 2022 and Road & Track in 2024. The woes of alternate side parking have kept his fleet moderate, with a 2014 Volkswagen Jetta GLI and a BMW 318i E30 street parked in his Queens community.

Delta Plans Another Austin Route

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Delta is expanding its Austin, Texas, network with a new daily route set to launch next spring.

The carrier will begin flights between Austin and Northwest Arkansas National Airport, which serves Fayetteville and the surrounding region, on March 7, 2027. SkyWest will operate the route under the Delta Connection brand using an Embraer E175.

Service from Northwest Arkansas to Austin begins March 8. Tickets are now on sale.

Flights will depart Austin at 8:59 p.m. and arrive in Northwest Arkansas at 10:49 p.m. The return flight will leave at 6 a.m. and arrive in Austin at 7:50 a.m. All times are local.

“Connecting Central Texas and Northwest Arkansas with new nonstop service brings two dynamic, growing regions closer together while giving our customers even more choice and convenience,” Luis Guitart, Delta’s Austin-based regional sales manager, said in a statement.

Guitart described the addition as another example of the airline’s investment in Austin.

Delta has been building its Austin operation, with plans to offer more than 70 peak-day departures to approximately 30 destinations by summer 2027. That expansion includes new service to Paris and San Diego, a second daily flight to San Jose, California, and year-round service to Cancun, Mexico.

First Lite Uncompahgre Foundry Jacket, Tested and Reviewed

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 3, 2026 4:36 PM EST

We may earn revenue from the products available on this page and participate in affiliate programs. Learn More ›

Get the hottest outdoor news from Outdoor Life.

It’s not often that you get to hunt in an area that is the namesake for the jacket you’re wearing, but this fall, I did just that. The Uncompahgre National Forest covers almost a million acres of high-elevation backcountry in Colorado. At, and over, 10,000 feet, the wind and weather can be unpredictable. So your clothing needs to be versatile and ready for whatever Mother Nature might throw your way.

After wearing the Uncompahgre Foundry Jacket during my first-rifle elk hunt with fluctuating temperatures, driving rain, and even a healthy dose of snowfall, I will be the first to tell you that this jacket earned its name. It’s surprisingly packable, offers zippered ventilation around the triceps, and is incredibly warm. Since my elk hunt, it’s earned a spot in my treestand layering system as well. Here’s everything you need to know about this must-have outer layer.

As I mentioned above, the weather on my elk hunt was unpredictable at best. From driving rain for multiple days on end, to rapidly dropping temperatures bringing snow into the high country, I got to experience the full spectrum of temperatures and conditions. The Uncompahgre Foundry jacket was one piece of my kit that I wouldn’t have wanted to live without.

It remained durable after hiking through oak brush, deadfall, and large conifer stands, but more importantly, it kept me dry. Since I was living in a tent, my biggest fear was getting wet, and thus, staying wet with no way to really dry off given the conditions. Thankfully, this jacket both figuratively and literally had my back.

Upon returning home, I tested the Uncompahgre Foundry jacket throughout an October cold front here in Pennsylvania as well. While the conditions were drastically improved compared to the backcountry in Colorado, this jacket still fit into my kit wonderfully. Rather than wear it the entire walk to the stand, I simply packed it up into a ball and tossed it in my pack. After climbing in, I’d throw it on and enjoy my sit. It keeps me warm and surprisingly doesn’t make a ton of noise when you’re moving to grab your bow and draw back.

While I do really like this jacket, there’s always room for improvement. The first thing I noticed was the pocket design and layout. On the front of the jacket are four pockets. Two magnetic dump pockets, and just above those, two zippered hand warmer pockets featuring a warm tricot lining. Both are quite handy for different things, but I often found myself digging around in the wrong pocket due to their layout and close proximity to each other. If it were up to me, I’d leave the dump pockets where they are, and move the hand warmer pockets to a more traditional, hip-centric location.

I also had an issue, that was certainly in part user error, where the hand warmer pockets got wet during a popup rain shower. I had accidentally left the zipper open, but due to the design, the rain ran down the front of the jacket and right into those pockets. When the zippers were closed, no water intrusion occurred. User error aside, I’d argue it’s another reason to relocate the hand warmer pockets.

Where the Uncompahgre Foundry jacket shines truly is in its versatility. I was able to wear it in temperatures ranging from 20 degrees, all the way up to 50 degrees. Admittedly, 50 degrees might have been right on the edge of too hot for this jacket, it really did a nice job of ventilating when I opened up the tricep vents.

It’s incredibly windproof, waterproof, durable, and warm. Honestly, it’s an outer layer I didn’t even know I needed until I had it. And now that I have it, it’ll be accompanying me on every hunt for the remainder of the fall and winter.

As an avid hunter, I came to the realization years ago that you get what you pay for when purchasing hunting clothing. I hate being cold, and I hate being wet. Thankfully, the Uncompahgre Foundry jacket does a wonderful job of eliminating both of those concerns. If you’re hunting for a new outer layer to get you through the rut, rifle, and late season, look no further.

Derek Horner is the manager of audience development for Outdoor Life where he manages OL’s social accounts and email campaigns, and shares our stories with the world. He was born and raised in central Pennsylvania where his father and grandfather taught him to hunt and fish. He graduated from Pennsylvania State University in 2018 and still lives in central Pennsylvania.

I Feel, Therefore I Am: Neuroscientist Antonio Damasio the Consciousness of the Body

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Every month, I spend hundreds of hours and thousands of dollars keeping The Marginalian going. For two decades, it has remained free, ad-free, AI-free, fully human and alive thanks to patronage from readers. I have no staff, no interns, not even an assistant — a thoroughly one-woman labor of love that is also my life and my livelihood. If it makes your own life more livable in any way, please consider aiding its sustenance with a one-time or loyal donation. Your support makes all the difference.

You can also become a spontaneous supporter with a one-time donation in any amount:

Partial to Bitcoin? You can beam some bit-love my way: 197usDS6AsL9wDKxtGM6xaWjmR5ejgqem7

Need to cancel a recurring donation? Go here.

For an act of resistance to the tyranny of algorithms, try the Marginalian newsletter—undistracted notes on the search for meaning, free, ad-free, AI-free, fully human since 2006. (Here is an example.)

“A purely disembodied emotion is a nonentity,” William James wrote in his revolutionary theory of how our bodies affect our feelings just before the birth of neuroscience — a science still young, which has already revolutionized our understanding of the cosmos inside the cranium as much as the first century of telescopic astronomy revolutionized our understanding of our place in the universe.

Meanwhile, ninety miles inland from William James, while Walt Whitman was redoubling his metaphysical insistence that “the body includes and is the meaning, the main concern… and is the soul,” Emily Dickinson was writing in one of her science-prescient poems:

The Brain — is wider than the Sky — For — put them side by side — The one the other will contain With ease — and you — beside —

The Brain is deeper than the sea — For — hold them — Blue to Blue — The one the other will absorb — As sponges — Buckets — do —

It is the task, the destiny of science to concretize with evidence what the poets have always intuited and imagized in abstraction: that we are infinitely more miraculous and infinitely less important than we thought. The universe without, which made us and every star-dusted atom of our consciousness, is ever-vaster and more complex than we suppose it to be; the universe within, which makes the universe without and renders our entire experience of reality through the telescopic lens of our consciousness, is ever-denser and more complex than we suppose it to be.

A century and a half after James, neuroscientist Antonio Damasio picks up an empirical baton where Dickinson had left a torch of intuition. In his revelatory book Feeling & Knowing: Making Minds Conscious (public library), he makes the bold case that consciousness — that ultimate lens of being, which shapes our entire experience of life and makes blue appear blue and gives poems their air of wonder — is not a mental activity confined to the brain but a complex embodied phenomenon governed by the nervous-system activity we call feeling.

Decades after Toni Morrison celebrated the body as the supreme instrument of sanity and self-regard, neuroscience affirms the body as the instrument of feeling that makes the symphony of consciousness possible: feelings, which arise from the dialogue between the body and the nervous system, are not a byproduct of consciousness but made consciousness emerge. (Twenty years earlier — an epoch in the hitherto lifespan of neuroscience — the uncommonly penetrating Martha Nussbaum had anticipated this physiological reality through the lens of philosophy, writing in her superb inquiry into the intelligence of emotions that “emotions are not just the fuel that powers the psychological mechanism of a reasoning creature, they are parts, highly complex and messy parts, of this creature’s reasoning itself.”)

Damasio’s premise rises from the flatland of earlier mind-based theories by a conceptual fulcrum both simple and profound:

Feelings gave birth to consciousness and gifted it generously to the rest of the mind.

This view defies both extremes that dominate our present models of consciousness, each unimaginative and intellectually unambitious in its own way, as all extremes invariably are: materialism, which confines it to the neural activity of the brain, and mysticism, which places it entirely outside the contours of the body and beyond the reach of scientific investigation. Damasio writes:

Any theory that bypasses the nervous system in order to account for the existence of minds and consciousness is destined to failure. The nervous system is the critical contributor to the realization of minds, consciousness, and the creative reasoning that they allow. But any theory that relies exclusively on the nervous system to account for minds and consciousness is also bound to fail. Unfortunately, that is the case with most theories today. The hopeless attempts to explain consciousness exclusively in terms of nervous activity are partly responsible for the idea that consciousness is an inexplicable mystery. While it is true that consciousness, as we know it, only fully emerges in organisms endowed with nervous systems, it is also true that consciousness requires abundant interactions between the central part of those systems — the brain proper — and varied non-nervous parts of the body.

Drawing on the native poetics of our physiology, Damasio offers a definition of consciousness:

Consciousness… is a particular state of mind resulting from a biological process toward which multiple mental events make a contribution… These contributions converge, in a regimented way, to produce something quite complex and yet perfectly natural: the encompassing mental experience of a living organism caught, moment after moment, in the act of apprehending the world within itself and, wonder of wonders, the world around itself.

A century and a half after Darwin scribbled a note to himself in the margin of one of his manuscripts — “Never say higher or lower in referring to organisms… Say more complicated.” — Damasio details the levels of complexity by which various organisms manage the living wonder of themselves. All life-forms, from bacteria to Bach, share a basic machinery of stimulus-detection called sensing. Organisms with nervous systems are capable of minding — the neurobiological process of mapping information into patterns and translating it into mental images.

These images furnish representations of the world, making it comprehensible and therefore survivable as the organism navigates that world by a sort of native biological intelligence that powers the basic self-care necessary for maintaining homeostasis — maintenance that eventually comes aglow with feeling. More complex organisms can manipulate those images, integrating them into a system of reference we call knowledge, which the nervous system makes explicit by creating patterns and committing them to memory, so that the organism can plan, reason, and reflect.

Ultimately, feeling conspires with minding and knowing to give rise to the system-level phenomenon of consciousness from the infrastructure of the nervous system and the body: Our perceptual senses — sight, touch, hearing, smell, taste — render the external world in mental images; our feelings render the internal world, representing in our own minds the state of our bodies — those roiling inner worlds in which all sense of wellbeing is won or lost. From this sense of ownership of ourselves arises the phenomenon of consciousness — the functions that makes possible the novel responses we call adaptation, or art.

Consciousness gathers together the bits of sapience that reveal, by dint of their coincident presence, the mystery of belonging. They tell me — or you — sometimes in the subtle language of feeling, sometimes in ordinary images or even in words translated for the occasion, that yes, lo and behold, it is me — or you — thinking these things, seeing these sights, hearing these sounds, and feeling these feelings. The “me” and “you” are identified by mental components and body components.

The most crucial of these bits of sapience manifest as mental images formed by body-mind interactions:

Turn a mind inside out and spill its contents. What do you find? Images and more images, the sorts of images that complicated creatures, such as we are, manage to generate and combine in a forward-flowing stream. This is the very “stream” that immortalized William James and gave fame to the word “consciousness” because the two words were so often paired in the phrase “stream of consciousness.” But… the stream… is simply made of images whose near-seamless flow constitutes a mind.

When we relate and combine images in our minds and transform them within our creative imaginations, we produce new images that signify ideas, concrete as well as abstract; we produce symbols; and we commit to memory a good part of all the imagetic produce. As we do so, we enlarge the archive from which we will draw plenty of future mental contents.

But it is the feeling coloring these mental images that makes our consciousness what it is — every perceived and stored scene or song, landscape or idea, is already infused with affect in the jar of memory, and that is what makes it shimmer with meaning.

Insisting that “we should celebrate the wealth and the messiness we have been gifted by affect,” Damasio writes:

What you perceive or remember, what you try to figure out by reasoning, what you invent or wish to communicate, the actions you undertake, the things you learn and recall, the mental universe made up by objects, actions, and abstractions thereof, all of these different processes can generate affective responses as they unfold. We can think of affect as the universe of our ideas transmuted in feeling, and it is also helpful to think of feelings in music terms. Feelings perform the equivalent of a musical score that accompanies our thoughts and actions.

Feelings are commingled with the things and events we feel thanks to the exceptional and intimate cross talk between body structures and nervous system.

Mapping the four-billion-year history of living organisms along its branching streams, Damasio envisions the distributary that led to us as a cascade of three evolutionary stages: being, feeling, and knowing, which continue to coexist in each of us modern sapiens, coursing through the various anatomical and functional systems that give us life. No invention of nature, Damasio argues, powered a greater leap than the emergence of nervous systems, which made minds possible — but their inception, like so many great inventions, was an unbidden byproduct of solving pressing necessities:

Complex, multicellular organisms with differentiated systems — endocrine, respiratory, digestive, immune, reproductive — were saved by nervous systems, and organisms with nervous systems came to be saved by the things nervous systems invented — mental images, feelings, consciousness, creativity, cultures.

Nervous systems are splendid “afterthoughts” of a non-minded, non-thinking, but pioneeringly prescient nature.

These astonishing afterthoughts of evolution became the stage on which the theater of consciousness plays out. Damasio explains:

Nervous systems enable both complex movements and, eventually, the beginning of a real novelty: minds. Feelings are among the first examples of mind phenomena, and it is difficult to exaggerate their significance. Feelings allow creatures to represent in their respective minds the state of their own bodies preoccupied with regulating the internal organ functions required by the necessities of life… Feelings provide organisms with experiences of their own life.

He considers how this transformative afterthought might have evolved and how it gave us the capacity for feeling that forever changed the course of life on Earth:

Feeling probably began its evolutionary history as a timid conversation between the chemistry of life and the early version of a nervous system within one particular organism… Those timid beginnings provided each creature with an orientation, a subtle adviser as to what to do next or not to do or where to go. Something novel and extremely valuable had emerged in the history of life: a mental counterpart to a physical organism.

Feelings… provide the urge and the incentive to behave according to the information they carry and do what is most appropriate for the current situation, be it running for cover or hugging the person you have missed.

Through this essential feedback loop of feeling, we are able to assess how we are doing at the basic task of living — not only at the binary state of whether or not we are staying alive, but on the qualitative scale of how well our actual experience maps onto our optimal experience. Pleasure and pain, love and longing — these are all varieties of conscious experience that allow us to fine-tune our flourishing. They all arise when stimuli trigger molecular messages that travel from body tissues and organs, through nerve terminals, into the central nervous system and the brain, producing mental images that give us valuable information we experience as emotional states, which serve to steer us toward corrective action.

This might seem mechanistic and unpoetic, but out of this biological feedback loop arises our capacity for problem-solving and poetry, for beauty and transcendence, for everything we call creativity. In consonance with the consolation Lou Andreas-Salomé offered to her dispirited poet-friend Rainer Maria Rilke — “A great deal of poetic work has arisen from various despairs.” — Damasio observes:

The human experience of pain and suffering has been responsible for extraordinary creativity, focused and obsessive, responsible for inventing all kinds of instruments capable of countering the negative feelings that initiated the creative cycle.

Ultimately, we are puppets of both pain and pleasure, occasionally made free by our creativity.

This feedback loop of feeling is unavailable to organisms in the less developed being stage of evolution, and yet out entire sense of being — the meta-awareness we experience as a self — is contingent upon it. Damasio writes:

Not surprisingly, feelings are important contributors to the creation of a “self,” a mental process animated by the state of the organism, and are anchored in its body frame (the frame constituted by muscular and skeletal structures), and oriented by the perspective provided by sensory channels such as vision and hearing.

Once being and feeling are structured and operational, they are ready to support and extend the sapience that constitutes the third member of the trio: knowing.

Feeling provides us with knowledge of life in the body and, without missing a beat, makes that knowledge conscious… The maps and images created on the basis of sensory information become the most abundant and diverse constituents of mind, side by side with ever present and related feelings. More often than not, they dominate the mental proceedings.

Once experiences begin to be committed to memory, feeling and conscious organisms are capable of maintaining a more or less exhaustive history of their lives, a history of their interactions with others and of their interaction with the environment, in brief, a history of each individual life as lived inside each individual organism, nothing less than the armature of personhood.

This understanding defeats a popular dictum of the self-help world — the comfort-blanket belief that one cannot cause another person’s feelings or be caused to feel a certain way by another person’s actions. No: One person can very much make choices and take actions toward another that impact and impair the other person’s homeostasis — that is, the organism’s sense of stability and safety — thus producing in that other person the negative feelings that are the organism’s feedback loop to protect homeostasis: pain, our primary signal for course-correction.

This is where our physiology and psychology converge. Offering neuoroscientific affirmation of Hannah Arendt’s searing philosophical-political indictment that “society has discovered discrimination as the great social weapon by which one may kill men without any bloodshed,” Damasio writes:

We are quite familiar with the direct way in which illness gives way to discomfort and pain or exuberant health produces pleasure. But we often overlook the fact that psychological and sociocultural situations also gain access to the machinery of homeostasis in such a way that they too result in pain or pleasure, malaise or well-being. In its unerring push for economy, nature did not bother to create new devices to handle the goodness or badness of our personal psychology or social condition. It makes do with the same mechanisms.

This is so because feelings are not purely mental phenomena but delicate interleavings of body and mind — the serpent of consciousness biting its own tail:

The power of feelings comes from the fact that they are present in the conscious mind: technically speaking, we feel because the mind is conscious, and we are conscious because there are feelings… Feelings were and are the beginning of an adventure called consciousness.

In the land of language, however, the adventure has been burdened by a great deal of cultural baggage, loaded with misconceptions and misuses. Consciousness is a young English word, not yet born when Milton wrote that “the mind is its own place, and in it self can make a Heav’n of Hell, a Hell of Heav’n.” Today, particularly in panpsychic theory, it is often used interchangeably with mind, which plunders it of the essential role of feeling. Damasio points out that even in the older Romance languages, which include his native Portuguese, one must settle for the word conscience, already blunted by its multiple meanings. My native Bulgarian might come closest to Damasio’s model — the most literal translation of our word for consciousness is self-knowledge.

This, indeed, is the crux of Damasio’s case for feeling — feelings are how we know that our experience is our own, that the bodies through which experience courses are our own, that the perspective through which images flicker on the screen of the mind is our own. I am reminded here of something I once heard Gloria Steinem say, in the midst of a twenty-first century cultural dark age for conscience: “The place where we need to go is where our bodies… are our own. This is the basis of democracy.”

With an eye to this essential parameter of ownership — the great revelation made possible by feeling — Damasio writes:

Feelings let the mind know, automatically, without any questions being asked, that mind and body are together, each belonging to the other. The classic void that has separated physical bodies from mental phenomena is naturally bridged thanks to feelings… Self-reference is not an optional feature of feeling but a defining, indispensable one.

What does it mean to say “I am conscious”? At the simplest level imaginable, it means to say that my mind, at the particular moment in which I describe myself as conscious, is in possession of knowledge that spontaneously identifies me as its proprietor… Some knowledge about the current operations of my body [and] some knowledge as retrieved from memory, about who I am at the moment and about who I have been, recently and in the long ago past… [produce] mental states imbued with feeling and a sense of personal reference.

At the heart of this idea is the loosening of the brain’s stronghold of consciousness and its diffusion through the entirety of the living organism — a reconfiguration that, as Damasio puts it, “requires the placement of that mind in the setting of its body.”

This embodied model of consciousness looms with some profound consequences.

One, which Damasio does not address directly — perhaps because it is self-evident, or perhaps because he prefers not to ruffle the feelings (that is, the consciousnesses) of those who take flight from evidence in such beliefs — is a bold debunking of certain escapist fantasies from our creaturely reality: both the fantasies haunted by our parochial past and its various religious mythologies of an immortal soul that survives the death of the body (“soul” being the conceptual placeholder for consciousness before the word was coined), and the fantasies haunting the techno-utopian future with Silicon dreams of machine consciousness and technology-assisted ways of preserving human consciousness beyond the lifespan of the body by digitizing and migrating the contents of the brain alone.

Another, which Damasio does touch on at the end of the book, is a humbling antidote to the dual hubris with which humanity regards itself and other life-forms: the hubris of human exceptionalism across species, which presumes that our superior cognitive capacity relative to other animals automatically means superior consciousness (a hubris readily deconditioned by what we have been learning, for instance, about the complex consciousness of the far more modest-brained octopus), and the within-species hubris that treats individuals with higher cognitive capacity measured by our deeply flawed IQ metrics as superior to those with other, less computationally driven and computable forms of intelligence and sensitivity.

And this is the optimistic undertone I hear in Damasio’s model: By understanding as a full-body phenomenon the consciousness that lenses our view of reality and shapes our life-experience, we can not only become better stewards of our own bodies and of the planet we share with other bodies, human and nonhuman, but we can begin to dismantle the artificial hierarchies and categories by which we have long bolstered our creaturely centrality across the various scales and spectra of existence, from Ptolemism to anthropocentrism to racism, choosing instead to be both humbled and hallowed by the evolutionary wonder of consciousness.

In the remainder of Feeling & Knowing, Damasio goes on to detail the three universes of experience from which our mental images spring, how our chemistry and our skeletal frame converge to produce our sense of belonging to ourselves, the role of affect in how we allocate attention, and much more, including how the discoveries of science in the epochs since Emily Dickinson penciled her far-seeing verse have clarified her core insight:

Dickinson was candidly committed to an organic view of mind and to a modern conception of the human spirit. And yet, in the end, what turned out to be wider than the sky was not the brain but life itself, the begetter of bodies, brains, minds, feelings, and consciousness. What is more impressive than the entire universe is life, as matter and process, life as inspirer of thinking and creation.

Every month, I spend hundreds of hours and thousands of dollars keeping The Marginalian going. For two decades, it has remained free, ad-free, AI-free, fully human and alive thanks to patronage from readers. I have no staff, no interns, not even an assistant — a thoroughly one-woman labor of love that is also my life and my livelihood. If it makes your own life more livable in any way, please consider aiding its sustenance with a one-time or loyal donation. Your support makes all the difference.

Forget These 4 Ab Workout Myths. Train Using These Principles Instead.

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Know the truth before you begin your transformation.

YOU WANT TO get in shape, and you want to look the part. So you tack on some extra time to the end of your workout to hit your abs, sure that those extra crunches will result in a six-pack sooner than later. Right?

Before we go any further, there are a handful of notions about your core training that you need to ditch immediately. If any of these sound familiar to you, don’t sweat it. You’ve likely heard about thousands of ab-building techniques, and it’s no easy feat to sift through all that information in search of the truth. What’s important is understanding what ab results are possible through consistent training. This will save you during those moments five weeks into this program when you’re questioning if you should keep going. You’ll remember what the process is, why it works, and why you really do have to log that day’s sets.

I can’t stress this enough: the abs should be treated no differently than other muscles in the body. A muscle is a muscle, and that means three things: muscles don’t work independently, muscles adapt to resistance training, and muscles need appropriate recovery to develop.

Unfortunately, when you have a topic like abs, which are arguably the most Googled and sought-after body part, you will get a large mix of opinions on how to approach training. The approach I want you to take with me as your coach is the science-based one. We aren’t going to live in the anecdotal world of “my one friend did this and got great results.” When it comes to exercise, there isn’t a one-size-fits-all solution, but there are some tried-and-true facts we have learned over the years with science and research. These are the key principles I share with my clients when it comes to breaking down the misinformation about abs.

I AM NOT sure when this myth started, but for some reason people are under the impression that abs are uniquely different from other muscles in that you can train them every day. That is absolutely not true.

Overuse of any muscle actually leads to atrophy (deterioration) of the muscle, which is the exact opposite of what you want when you are trying to build your abs. When it comes to core training and core conditioning, it’s more effective to have a mix of higher and lower intensity days, as you’ll see later in the program.

Going all out is an outdated approach to training. You simply can’t go full throttle every day and expect to stay on the ball throughout the program. There are a handful of people in the world who can do just that, and that’s amazing. But we can’t create a workout plan based on outliers. It needs to be based on general outcomes. So, in order to give yourself appropriate recovery time, challenge your muscles in different ways, and ensure continued success, it’s best to navigate between different intensity days.

THERE IS NO such thing as spot reduction.

Direct abdominal work isn’t going to burn off that layer of fat laying on top of your abs the way you might hope. Burning fat is much better attacked through nutrition, strength training, and cardiovascular exercise. The best approach is a holistic one, like I’ve designed for you in this plan.

The way you develop your core in this program will change the way you look at training moving forward. By mixing up exercises and taking a step-by-step approach to building up a strong core, you’ll see changes in everything from the way you look to the way you feel to the way you move through your day! These 90 days will build you up from the inside out into the strongest version of yourself.

BELIEVE IT OR not, they can be the same. That is the basis of this program—to blend ability with visibility.

You shouldn’t feel doomed to endless days of crunches wondering why the results just aren’t coming to you. Variety and emphasis will be large determining factors of your training. With the right mix, you will be able to get the look you want with the function you need.

HAVE YOU EVER noticed two people with differently shaped abs? Maybe one person’s abs are deeper, more symmetrical, and more defined than his buddy’s. I mean, Arnold Schwarzenegger is famous for having four visible abs no matter how shredded he got. Why is that?

Well, that is something you are born with. Thick cords of fibrous tissue stretch across the abdominal wall. The main connective tissue that runs down the center of the abdomen is called the linea alba. That line can be straight or slightly angled, depending on your genetics, and determines how symmetrical your abs appear to be. How many of the crossing tendinous fibers you have (which can be three or more) determines what kind of pack you can get, be it a six-pack, eight-pack, or even a 10-pack.

David Otey, CSCS is a fitness writer, NYC-based strength coach, and Men's Health Advisory Board member who specializes in strength and hypertrophy protocols as well as athletic performance. For more on Otey check out www.oteyfitness.com.

Brandweek 2026: Why Marketing Belongs in Product, Finance, and Tech with Petzey&#8217;s Ameetess Dira

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

In the age of AI, marketers still have to answer one big question: “Why should anyone choose you?”

Live from the Marketing Vanguard stage at Brandweek 2026, Jenny Rooney sits down with Ameetess Dira, CMO of Petzey, for a conversation about the decisions that become even more important as marketing gets easier to automate.

Ameetess breaks down why purpose alignment is a non-negotiable in her career, and why she deliberately pushes marketing into product, technology, and finance conversations.

They also discuss why some of her strongest brand partnerships looked strange on paper.

This episode is part of a special vidcast series that was recorded live during Brandweek 2026 and presented in partnership with Tatari.

Ameetess Dira is CMO at Petzey, an on-demand pet telehealth app with more than 1,000 pet professionals across the U.S.

Her path into marketing has crossed broadcasting, radio, print, agency leadership, technology, and international market expansion across Asia and the United States.

Her move into pet health was deeply personal. After losing her senior dog, Ameetess deliberately sought out work that could improve access to care for animals and the people who love them. That purpose now shapes how she thinks about everything from partnerships and crisis response to the role marketing should play inside the wider business.

Purpose Alignment Is Not a Nice-to-Have — For Ameetess, belief in the company and its leadership is a non-negotiable requirement. She wants to work with people she trusts enough to do difficult things with because the work serves something bigger than the individuals involved. She makes the distinction clear: purpose is not a layer applied to marketing after the strategy is done. It is the filter that determines whether she wants to do the work in the first place.

Look Where Everyone Else Says “That’s Too Small” — Senior dog adoption is difficult, expensive, and easy for brands to ignore; that is exactly why Ameetess pays attention to it. Her approach is to look at how culture is changing, where people remain underserved and where a brand can contribute something useful instead of competing for the same obvious audience as everyone else.

Marketing Has No Business Staying in Marketing — Ameetess deliberately inserts marketing into conversations with product, technology, and finance. Her reasoning is simple – the executive team is her primary team, and everyone around that table is responsible for the overall health of the business. The CMO who stays inside campaigns and communications is giving up influence before the real decisions are even made.

Sometimes the Right Decision Is the One the Spreadsheet Hates — During the LA fires, Ameetess opened Petzey’s platform for free wellness checks for people affected by the crisis, including first responders, teachers, businesses, and shelters. The short-term financial logic pointed toward monetizing demand, and she still chose the opposite. Her argument is that doing the right thing can matter more than hitting the immediate number, especially when the decision proves what the brand actually stands for.

Jenny Rooney is Chief Brand and Community Officer, leading strategy for the overall ADWEEK brand as well as the ways in which we serve and support our audiences with high-value content, products, partnerships and experiences, notably through our community programs such as Marketing Vanguard.

Want Year-Round Curb Appeal, Even in Winter? Try These 20 Stunning Plants

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

By Jennifer Noonan, Updated by Teresa Odle

Published Nov 3, 2026 4:30 PM EST

We may earn revenue from the products available on this page and participate in affiliate programs. Learn More ›

Tips, tricks & ideas for a better home and yard, delivered to your inbox daily.

Does your home’s landscape look barren and brown after autumn leaves fall, and doesn’t perk back up again until spring? Most homeowners think about how their beds will look when they’re in full bloom, but few imagine how that same bed will look in the dead of winter. Outdoor winter plants that add beautiful contrast to a snowy landscape aren’t just good for your curb appeal—they can also provide critical food sources and nesting sites for wildlife. As you consider updates to the plants and shrubs that surround your home, look to these appealing, year-round options.

Blue Ice bog rosemary’s unique silvery-blue foliage looks good in every season, plus this evergreen ground cover delights with a shock of pink bell-shaped blooms in early spring. Perfect for rock gardens and moist soils, it will attract birds and bring visual interest to the yard all year.

USDA plant hardiness zone: 2 to 6Size: 1 to 2 feet high and wideWhere to buy: Get a 1-gallon Blue Ice Bog Rosemary at The Tree Center for $39.50

This dwarf pine variety turns from green to gold when cold weather arrives. In fact, the colder the climate, the more intense its color becomes. This rabbit- and deer-resistant plant is easy to care for, and an evergreen shrub that will look beautiful all year long. It grows slowly, but immediately adds low-growing color and texture to a winter bed.

USDA plant hardiness zone: 2 to 7Size: nearly 2 feet high and wideWhere to buy: Get a 1-gallon potted Carstens Wintergold Mugo Pine at Mike’s Plants on Etsy for $75

Many weeping ornamentals lose their leaves in cold weather, but not the weeping Norway spruce. With its unusual shape and showy cones, this evergreen makes a great accent in the garden and provides rich green in all four seasons. It can thrive in many soil types, including rich sandy soils, and has low water needs once established.

USDA plant hardiness zone: 3 to 7Size: 4 feet high and 15 feet aroundWhere to buy: Get a 3- to 4-foot high Weeping Norway Spruce at Spring Hill Nurseries for $299

This evergreen succulent creates a lush and attractive carpet that thrives year-round in cool to warm climates. Most boast tiny flowers that attract butterflies in summer, and the foliage of Pink Form and Purple Form can take on reddish to purple hues in colder months. Easy to care for in full sun, this little wonder packs a lot of life in its compact height but excellent spread. After a season or two, it is xeric.

USDA plant hardiness zone: 5 to 9Size: 1 feet high and 1 to 1.5 feet aroundWhere to buy: Get a 4-inch pot of Sedum Hispanicum at Cristina’s Garden on Etsy for $10.99

For evergreen ground cover in warmer climates, try Pot Belly Pittosporum. The tough little shrub flowers in spring and summer, leaving its silvery green foliage with a white underside on the plant through winter.  Best of all, it keeps its rounded shape with little maintenance required. Pot Belly Pittosporum is drought tolerant once established and can grow in partial to full sun.

USDA plant hardiness zone: 8 to 11Size: 4 feet tall and wideWhere to buy: Get a 1-gallon Pot Belly Pittosporum at Cornell Farm for $28.99

This ground-hugging variety of dianthus is evergreen and cold hardy. Its gray-green foliage erupts in summer with flowers, making it a wonderful filler both in the garden and in pots, so feel free to grow it in a container and overwinter it indoors in colder zones. The flowers are also fragrant, adding a clove-like scent to the garden.

USDA plant hardiness zone: 3 to 8Size: about 1 foot high and wideWhere to buy: Get a 1-gallon Frosty Fire Dianthus at Holly Days for $11.99

If you’re looking for a distinctive shade of evergreen color, dwarf globe blue spruce delivers. This slow-growing, densely branched conifer has blue needles that become more brilliant in summer. It makes a wonderful low hedge, or stands out as a small rabbit-resistant shrub.

USDA plant hardiness zone: 4 to 8Size: 2 to 3 feet high and 3 to 4 feet wideWhere to buy: Get a 1-gallon Dwarf Globe Blue Spruce tree at Trees.com for $54.95

Pink pussytoe plants provide an evergreen mat of tiny silver-gray leaves. In late spring, the plant spikes deep pink, fuzzy flowers. It’s a slow grower and requires little water once established, so this cold-hardy beauty is a breeze to care for in any season. The native cultivar grows best in full sun.

USDA plant hardiness zone: 3 to 8Size: 0.5 feet high and 1 foot wideWhere to buy: Get a bag of 20 Pussytoes seeds at SeedCult on Etsy for $2.99

Snowdrop bulbs are happiest with cold or moderate winters, and are among favorite winter-flowering plants. Place them in full or partial where you can enjoy their delicate white flowers in the fall and throughout the cold months, even during snow. Come spring, they’ll go dormant.

USDA plant hardiness zone: 3 to 9Size: 0.5 feet high and wideWhere to buy: Get a bag of 25 Galanthus Nivalis bulbs at White Flower Farm for $20

Christmas rose, a variety of hellebore, is a pest-resistant evergreen perennial. It also provides valuable nectar for early pollinators, like the rusty patched bumblebee, who are seeking blooms in late winter or early spring. Find it a shady location with space to grow, and soon you’ll enjoy its white winter blooms in the warmer end of their growing range or early spring where colder.

USDA plant hardiness zone: 3 to 8Size: 1 foot tall and up to 1.5 feet wideWhere to buy: Get a 1-gallon potted Ivory Prince Christmas Rose at Plant Addicts for $49.99

Winter jasmine is a vining shrub that will treat you with bursts of fragrant yellow flowers between late winter and early spring; the plant’s flowers often are the first to appear as winter wraps up. But even when not in bloom, this plant’s large arching stems will provide plenty of visual interest to the garden. Hardy and easy to grow, winter jasmine is just at home in coastal areas as it is along rock walls.

USDA plant hardiness zone: 6 to 10Size: up to 10 feet tall and 6 feet wideWhere to buy: Get a 1-gallon pot of Winter Jasmine at The Home Depot for $25.58

Known for its medicinal properties, witch hazel is often used in cosmetics and lotions, since it is reported to reduce inflammation and soothe certain skin irritations. It’s also a great choice for a winter garden, thanks to its fragrant yellow-to-red flowers that can bloom even in snowy weather. This adaptable shrub or small tree thrives in full sun to partial shade.

USDA plant hardiness zone: 3 to 7Size: 3 to 6 feet tall and wideWhere to buy: Get a 1- to 2-foot high Witch Hazel at TN Nursery for $26.99

Named for its small clusters of white berries that put on a show throughout the winter and give way to little white flowers in the spring, the snowberry plant provides a valuable food source for birds during the cold winter months. This easy-care plant will spread as a dense thicket, so it’s a great choice for borders and living fences.

USDA plant hardiness zone: 3 to 9Size: more than 15 feet tall and wideWhere to buy: Get a 1-gallon potted White Snowberry at The Home Depot for $38.04

Featuring dark green glossy leaves, sweet box plants are shade-loving evergreen shrubs that boast small fragrant flowers that bloom in late winter and early spring. Once the fragrant flowers fade, little black fruits will appear in their wake. Preferring partial to full shade, sweet box plants are tough and excellent year-round plants to add interest to the garden.

USDA plant hardiness zone: 6 to 8Size: up to 2 feet high and 4 feet wideWhere to buy: Get a 1-gallon Fragrant Dwarf Sweet Box at Planting Partners on Etsy for $28.99

For a vibrant splash of color in warmer zones, plant winter heath in your yard, which will repay you with abundant tiny pink winter flowers that bloom in late winter through early spring. The colorful winter shrub is an even hardier version of E. carnea, and both are commonly used as a ground cover, forming a dense mat. Prune it right after it finishes flowering in the spring to prevent leafless stems and keep it looking great all year.

USDA plant hardiness zone: 6 to 8Size: up to 2 feet high and wideWhere to buy: Get a quart-size potted Erica Kramer’s Red Winter Heath at The Home Depot for $18.53

This deciduous shrub’s large size creates a show-stopping garden accent. In mid- to late fall, the American cranberry bush will burst with vibrant red berries that last into winter. The berries  are edible but aren’t very tasty when eaten raw. However, they provide important winter food for wildlife. Fragrant while flowers appear in spring and persist through mid summer. The shrub thrives in full or partial sun and prefers alkaline soil.

USDA plant hardiness zone: 2 to 7Size: 8 to 12 feet high and wideWhere to buy: Get a 3-gallon Bailey Compact American Cranberry Bush at Plant Addicts for $73.99

Also known as Perovskia, Russian sage is popular for its tall blue-violet flowers that bloom in the summer through the fall, attracting all sorts of pollinators to the garden. However, this shrub looks great in the winter, too, thanks to long silvery stems that offer structure and intrigue to a winter landscape. Leave the faded flower stems on until new growth appears in spring and plant Russian sage along walkways to maximize enjoyment of its fragrant flowers in summer. Once established, it is a drought-tolerant shrub.

USDA plant hardiness zone: 5 to 9Size: up to 4 feet high and wideWhere to buy: Get a 3-inch potted Lacey Blue Russian Sage at American Meadows for $7.99

Also known as Eastern teaberry, American wintergreen features glossy evergreen leaves and bright red berries that will offer a beautiful contrast to a wintery white landscape. Like many winter outdoor plants, this shrub helps small mammals and birds who love the berries. It is disease-resistant, and crushing its leaves will release a refreshing wintergreen scent. Plant the showy low grower in dappled sunlight to deep shade to keep it happy.

USDA plant hardiness zone: 3 to 7Size: less than 1 foot high and up to 1 foot wideWhere to buy: Get 6 bare root Teaberry Wintergreen plants at New Hampshire Plants on Etsy for $9.99

Yucca glauca is among the most winter-hardy yucca varieties. Its attractive evergreen foliage features long narrow stems in a rosette shape that look great all year long. In the spring, a stem rises from the rosette that features white bell-shaped flowers. These flowers attract butterflies, and the entire easy-care yucca plant provides food and nesting opportunities for small mammals, birds, and reptiles.

USDA plant hardiness zone: 4 to 8Size: 3 to 4 feet high and wideWhere to buy: Get a 5-inch pot of Soapweed Yucca at Etsy for $24.75

This low-growing shrub features glossy dark green leaves that turn bronze in the fall, persisting through the winter. During the winter you’ll also be treated to the shrub’s remaining vivid red berries, which provide a valuable food source for birds. The berries arise from tiny white flowers that form in the spring and summer, which butterflies love. Plant it in full sun to watch it thrive, though the shrub can handle partial shade.

USDA plant hardiness zone: 5 to 8Size: 1 foot high and 4 to 6 feet wideWhere to buy: Get a 1-gallon Bearberry Cotoneaster Coral Beauty at Plants Express for $17.95

The prices listed here are accurate as of publication on November 3, 2026.
