How to Mount a Riflescope Like a Pro. Follow This Step-By-Step Guide

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 3, 2026 4:24 PM EST

We may earn revenue from the products available on this page and participate in affiliate programs. Learn More ›

The latest for gun hunters and competitive shooters.

When mounting a riflescope, you’re dealing with the most fragile and failure-prone point on your gun—so it pays to do it right. The process isn’t complicated, but it does require an attention to detail and some basic equipment. So in part one of our seven-part series on mastering the use of precision riflescopes and shooting to your rifle’s full potential, I’m going to dive in to how to actually get one of these bad boys mounted properly.

To correctly mount a riflescope, these conditions must be satisfied: The reticle needs to be level with the rifle. The scope must be positioned at the correct height above the bore. The proper eye relief needs to be established. And the fasteners in the rings and bases need to be tightened the right way. Before we get into detail on each of these points, here’s a basic outline of the process.

• Clamp the barrel in the padded jaws of a bench vise. If you don’t have a bench vise, you’ll need a solid gun cradle that will hold the rifle steady.

• Use a bubble level to level the rifle action. The flat on top of the Picatinny rail works. With some actions you can remove the bolt and have enough clearance on the tang (assuming it is flat) to set a bubble level as well. As a last resort you can attach the bottom half of a scope ring to the scope base and rest the level on it.

• Set the scope in the rings and lightly tighten the fasteners so that the scope will stay put but can be rotated and moved by hand. If your scope has a flat spot on the elevation turret, place the bubble level on it to level the reticle.

• An alternate method to level the reticle is to look through the scope on your leveled rifle at a plumb bob and orient the vertical crosshair with the string.

• Once the reticle is level, remove the rifle from vise and check your eye relief. Make adjustments as needed. Do this by placing the rifle back in the vise, making sure it is level, and then moving the scope.

• Tighten the fasteners to the correct torque levels with a torque wrench.

The depth of the scope rings controls how far above the bore the scope sits. The primary concern is that both ends of the scope have adequate clearance above the rifle. Having the objective end of the scope in contact with the barrel is an obvious no-no, but you want to make sure the eyepiece isn’t cramped either. Rings that are too low can limit the horizontal travel of the scope in the rings making it more difficult to get the right eye relief.

If your scope is too high, you’ll have a hard time lining up behind the rifle when shooting prone, though a stock with an adjustable cheek piece can mitigate this.

Ultimately, this is a Goldilocks situation where the scope should neither be too high, nor too low. As a rule of thumb, a gap between the objective bell and the barrel of .25 to .5 inches should be just right—but the profile of your barreled action and stock might dictate otherwise.

With proper eye relief the shooter is able to look through the scope without any part of the image being blacked out. The way to test whether eye relief (and scope height) are correct is to get set up behind the rifle in the prone position, close your eyes, rest your check firmly on the stock by taking the tension out of your neck, then opening your eyes.

You should be looking right down the center of the riflescope and have a clear view of the reticle and image without needing to move your head.

Fortunately, many long-range rifles make fine tuning this easier by have adjustable lengths of pull and cheek piece height. Rifles equipped with Picatinny rails—which most precision rifles are—provide greater flexibility with the forward and aft placement of the scope to establish good eye relief.

Read Next: The Best Scope Rings of 2026, Tested and Reviewed

Some manufacturers provide values for the fasteners in their scope rings. Many don’t. As a rule of thumb, you can tighten the screws on scope rings to about 20 inch-pounds and be good to go.

It always makes sense to check the screws on your Pic rail or scope base as well. They tend to be beefier, so you can torque them to about 30 inch-pounds without worry.

You can use a torque wrench with preset fittings, like Fix-It Sticks or the Wheeler Fat Wrench, or one with an indicator dial like this one from Seekonk Precision Tools.

Before tightening the screws, you should consider doing one of two things. Either use a sparing amount of blue Loctite on the treads—a single small drop will do—or spray the screws with some degreaser. These will help the screws acquire a more secure hold.

Tighten the screws as you would lug nuts on a truck tire. Tighten one a bit, go to a screw on the other side of the ring and tighten that a bit, and repeat as you work your way through all the fasteners.

Tighten them gradually so that the rings are applying an even clamping force to the scope tube. When done correctly, the gap on either side of the rings should be even. That’s a good sign that whoever put the scope on the rifle knew their stuff.

John B. Snow is Shooting Editor of Outdoor Life, where he oversees the publication’s firearms and shooting coverage. This includes gear reviews, features on technical innovations, stories on shooting techniques and general hunting coverage with the occasional fishing story thrown into the mix.

Mummy in Glasgow may be repatriated to Egypt following removal from display

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

The Kelvingrove Art Gallery and Museum in Glasgow previously had mummified human remains on display in its ancient Egypt gallery

A mummy removed from display last month at Kelvingrove Art Gallery and Museum could be returned to Egypt. According to the Scottish newspaper The Sunday Post, Egyptian authorities have asked for the ­mummified remains of the priest Nekht to be repatriated.

Glasgow Life, the charitable organisation which operates the museum, confirmed to The Art Newspaper that it had received a request to send remains of Nekht back to Egypt. The organisation has not confirmed the origin of the request.

Last month, officials at the museum removed the coffin and mummified corpse of the ancient Egyptian priest from public view. A spokesperson for Glasgow Life said: “Glasgow Life Museums’ draft Human Remains Policy proposes human remains should only be displayed where there is documented and informed consent from the deceased individual or, in appropriate circumstances, their descendants. In the absence of such consent, we believe removing ancient Egyptian human remains from display is more respectful to the deceased individuals.”

The move comes after the International Council of Museums (Icom) revised its code of ethics in June, recommending the “inherent dignity” of human remains be upheld; Icom does not state however that all remains should be removed from display.

Meanwhile the Hunterian gallery in Glasgow confirmed to Museums Journal that it had covered an open sarcophagus containing the mummified remains of Lady Shep-En-Hor following an internal ethical review, although the sarcophagus itself remains on display.

The removal of the mummified remains at Kelvingrove and other museums feeds into current debate in the UK museums and heritage sector over the care and display of human remains. In response to the museum's policy on the lack of consent by the deceased, Stuart Robertson, the former director of the Charles Rennie Mackintosh Society in Glasgow, wrote on LinkedIn that “applying modern, 21st-century bureaucratic consent standards retrospectively to ancient history is an absurd and impossible standard. It completely undermines the educational mission of public museums”.

Last year, officials at the Manchester Museum asked visitors if the mummified remains of an Egyptian woman should remain on display. The mummy, known as Asru, was subsequently taken off public view.

“Asru now lies in the museum’s stores within her outer coffin, which will remain closed, but this is not intended to be her permanent resting place. We are thinking about how we better care for ancestors, in line with the wishes of the dead and of living communities,” says the museum's website.

Only fully covered Ancient Egyptian mummified remains will remain on display in the Egyptian Gallery of Sydney University’s Chau Chak Wing Museum

The restitutions are seen as reflective of the way museums are paying closer attention to the provenance of works in their collections

Guardian investigation highlighting 263,000 remains is a wake-up call for government, says Oxford professor

The Manchester Museum is running a public consultation about the future of Asru, a woman who lived in Thebes, southern Egypt

Recorded live at Brandweek 2026, the conversation examines how brands can bring greater accountability to retail media and in-store promotions through real-time data, matched-audience testing, and more rigorous measurement.

Benoit also shares insights into optimizing promotional spend across retail channels, reducing customer churn, growing household penetration, and reallocating budgets based on emerging sales signals rather than quarterly recaps.

Made In Just Dropped the Sweetest Bakeware Set—And It’s Perfect for Holiday Gifting

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

In search of the perfect gift for your favorite baker? The Made In bakeware set has arrived just in time for the holidays.

Respect Over Authenticity: Whalar Group Rewrites the Brand-Creator Partnership

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Ten years ago, brands were still asking why they should be on Instagram. Today, creators sit at the heart of strategy conversations from day one, owning stakes in companies and building businesses that look increasingly like media and entertainment companies in their own right.

That shift has changed what brands should expect from creator partnerships and what creators need to build behind the scenes.

On this episode of The Speed of Culture podcast, Neil Waller, co-founder and co-CEO of Whalar Group, joins Matt Britton to examine how experimentation, a simple thing like mutual respect, and operational rigor are shaping the next phase of the creator economy.

He also breaks down why AI could make trusted tastemakers even more valuable as content becomes increasingly abundant.

Neil Waller is the co-founder and co-CEO of Whalar Group, a creator-focused business that began as an influencer marketing agency in 2016 and has since grown into a six-company creator ecosystem.

Before Whalar, Neil and his co-founder James built their own direct-to-consumer brands on Shopify, using early creator partnerships to generate content and sales. That firsthand experience became the foundation for a business built around helping brands work more effectively with creators and, increasingly, helping creators build lasting companies around their audiences and influence.

[01:34] Proof Came Before the Pitch — Whalar’s creator thesis did not begin with an agency deck. Neil and his co-founder were already using creators to market their own Shopify brands, which gave them firsthand evidence that creator-led content could influence real purchasing behavior. For marketers, the lesson is simple: conviction becomes more credible when it comes from having tested the model yourself.

[02:57] Experimentation Beats Prediction — Neil is quick to reject the idea that Whalar’s early success came from seeing the future perfectly. Their watch brand worked because they could test quickly, learn from the market, and lean harder into what gained traction. Timing helped, but so did being willing to be wrong before becoming right. That mindset became more useful than relying on a rigid business plan in a market that was still taking shape.

[09:12] Respect Matters More Than Authenticity — “Authenticity” has long dominated creator marketing conversations, but Neil argues that mutual respect is the more useful standard. Brands need to understand why audiences trust creators, while creators need to understand what the brand represents and needs to achieve. The strongest collaborations sit between total brand control and total creative freedom.

[10:35] Creators Are The New Business Partners — Creators are no longer being added after the campaign strategy has already been written. Neil sees them increasingly contributing creative ideas, audience insight, and even business value through deeper partnerships and ownership structures. As creators become media and entertainment companies themselves, brands have an opportunity to build relationships that go far beyond sponsored content.

[13:03] Operational Rigor Is Non-Negotiable — Audience connection and great ideas can create momentum, but Neil argues that neither can compensate for weak execution forever. As creator businesses become larger and more complex, the infrastructure around them matters more. Professional teams, clear processes, and disciplined execution are what allow creative companies to turn attention into sustainable businesses.

[16:50] AI Could Make Taste More Valuable — Neil expects AI to dramatically increase the volume of personalized content while simultaneously pushing audiences toward trusted people and brands that can help them decide what is actually worth their attention. In his “barbell” view of content, abundance grows at one end while human taste and curation become more valuable at the other. The middle, generic content without a clear point of view, could become the hardest place to compete.

Matt Britton is America’s leading expert on the millennial generation having consulted for over half of the Fortune 500 over the past two decades. He is the Founder and CEO of Suzy and the host of The Speed of Culture Podcast.

Select ADWEEK as one of your favorite sources and stay up to date on our latest content.

Plus, why doing the right thing beats the short-term number.

Alaska Airlines Cuts Jobs As $700 Million Loss Looms, CEO Admits Overhead Costs Grew Too Fast

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Alaska Air Group is cutting about 200 jobs as CEO Ben Minicucci warns the company could lose as much as $700 million this year if current conditions persist. Higher fuel prices are the immediate problem, but Minicucci also acknowledged that overhead has grown too quickly. That is a sobering backdrop for an airline pursuing an ambitious international expansion.

According to Reuters, which reviewed Minicucci’s employee memo, the reductions affect non-contract positions, including 125 in Washington and 48 in Hawaii. No frontline, non-management positions are being eliminated. Affected employees will finish working November 13 but continue receiving pay through December 31, with severance and other support.

The cuts represent less than 1% of the group’s roughly 32,500 employees. That makes them relatively small across the company, but certainly not small to the people losing their jobs…

Minicucci warned employees that, if conditions persist, Alaska Air Group could record its first adjusted pretax annual loss outside the pandemic since 2003. That is a warning about a potential full-year result, not a $700 million loss already reported. It also concerns the parent group, which includes Alaska Airlines and Hawaiian Airlines.

In its October 20 earnings release, Alaska forecast a full-year adjusted loss of $5.00–$5.50 per share and said fuel would cost $1.8 billion more than its original annual plan. Its fourth-quarter forecast assumes fuel at $4.65–$4.85 per gallon. Third-quarter revenue rose 14% to $4.3 billion, but that commercial growth has not been enough to deliver the annual profit management expected.

Those numbers help explain why simply selling more tickets is not solving the problem.

There was another revealing part of Minicucci’s message:

“We must return to the financial discipline that has long been a key competitive advantage.”

He also acknowledged that overhead expenses had grown disproportionately to revenue in recent years, adding these costs had “grown at a disproportionate rate to revenue.”

Fuel prices are largely beyond an airline’s control, but the size and cost of its management structure are not. The question is why overhead was allowed to grow that way and whether these reductions address the problem or merely provide some near-term savings. Cutting 200 positions cannot will certainly not offset a fuel bill running $1.8 billion above plan.

It also puts management in the uncomfortable position of asking employees to absorb the consequences of spending decisions those employees may have had no role in making.

I have been following Alaska’s efforts to become a more substantial global competitor, including its latest European expansion to Athens and Paris and investment in premium cabins and lounges.

These job cuts do not automatically mean that strategy is wrong, though Alaska cannot cut its way to growth. It needs a product passengers will choose and a network that gives them a reason to return. Pulling back on customer investments when conditions deteriorate could leave Alaska with the costs of expansion but less ability to earn a premium.

Still, growth deserves scrutiny when the company is losing money. Management must be willing to adjust if demand or achievable fares do not justify the investment. Ambition is useful, but it cannot become an excuse to keep spending on something that is not working, though with every investment, it takes time for a sappling to grow into a tree and the “no pain, no gain” mantra certainly applies here too.

Minicucci intends to continue investing in the fleet, customer experience, and network while tightening spending elsewhere. That is a defensible approach…and it could be that these jobs are no longer needed with AI and simply a low-hanging fruit to trim unnecessary expense in order to focus on what is more important.

Alaska Air Group is cutting about 200 jobs while warning of a potential $700 million annual loss. Fuel costs explain much of the pressure, but management’s acknowledgment that overhead grew too quickly deserves attention too.

I want Alaska’s expansion to succeed. Heck, I want JetBlue and Alaska to merge. A stronger competitor is good for passengers. But the company needs to demonstrate that its growth can produce sustainable earnings, and employees losing their jobs should not be the only ones held accountable for restoring financial discipline.

Matthew is an avid traveler who calls Los Angeles home. Each year he travels more than 200,000 miles by air and has visited more than 135 countries. Working both in the aviation industry and as a travel consultant, Matthew has been featured in major media outlets around the world and uses his Live and Let's Fly blog to share the latest news in the airline industry, commentary on frequent flyer programs, and detailed reports of his worldwide travel.

WWII submarine returns to Michigan after extensive restoration

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 3, 2026 4:00 PM EST

A piece of World War II naval history has officially returned home to Muskegon, Michigan, looking like a brand new ship. On October 31, the USS Silversides arrived back at its museum after a 15-hour journey across Lake Michigan. The submarine was in Wisconsin for three months for its dry dock preservation in over 50 years. The endeavor involved completely removing the nearly 312-foot-long Gato-class submarine from the water in order to inspect and repair its lower hull.

Although workers noted the highly decorated vessel was holding up much better than anticipated, vessel curator Scott Hardin explained that the USS Silversides still required a sizable amount of attention. The $3.5 million repairs included welding a reinforcement steel to its underside, as well as multiple other fixes across all portions of the ship.

“Literally, from top to bottom, she got refurbished,” he told Michigan Live in a recent interview.

Built within one year and launched on August 21, 1941, the USS Silversides patrolled the Pacific theater armed with 10 torpedo tubes, a .50 caliber deck gun, as well as 40 mm and 20 mm cannons. It completed over 90,000 long tons of shipping and sank 23 enemy ships during that time. The U.S. Navy decommissioned the Silversides in 1946, and it later served as a Naval Reservist training ship in Chicago. It was later transferred to the Great Lakes Naval Memorial & Museum in 1987, before receiving its own USS Silversides Submarine Museum in Muskegon, Michigan.

“World War II vets in general are few and far between. And so what we have left from that generation are some of the machines that these guys used,” Hardin added. “It served the entire length of World War II. A man died on board, and it’s a piece of American history.”

During its dry docking earlier this year, workers also discovered another bit of unexpected U.S. history aboard the Silversides. Amid inspections, an attendant noticed a flood valve stamped with “Trigger” next to an accompanying serial number. A further review revealed the ship part is the only surviving piece of the USS Trigger, a nearly identical Gato-class submarine that sank during WWII. The Silversides and Trigger were constructed next to one another at California’s Mare Island Navy Yard, and many of their components were interchangeable with one another.

The USS Silversides is now receiving an interior cleaning before once again opening to public visitors on Saturday, November 7. However, there is still that $3.5 million repair bill to tackle. Hardin and his colleagues encourage supporters to help fund the ongoing preservation work through donating directly to the museum online.

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

How open source is driving positive change.

Organizations worldwide are incorporating open source methodologies into the way they build and ship their own software.

Helpful tips on securing web applications.

Learn about core challenges in DevSecOps, and how you can start addressing them with AI and automation.

Louis Erbkamm built the first prototype in his free time. Now GitHub Copilot helps him maintain it alone.

Louis Erbkamm knows his way around Arnis, the smallest town in Germany, but he’s never set foot in it.

I know where the shops are. Where the boats are… I know the city pretty well, but I haven’t been there yet.

Arnis is also the name of his open source project, which takes real-world data from OpenStreetMap and other sources to turn places you know into Minecraft worlds you can freely explore. Since Louis started Arnis in 2022, it’s reached close to 900,000 downloads from GitHub and about 70 contributors.

Louis grew up playing Minecraft and started coding at 13, always looking for ways to bring code into the game. Arnis began as a fun diversion combining Minecraft with geospatial data, and it went on GitHub, where he already kept his side projects. He finished the first prototype within two weeks while on a break from school.

That first version was Python written by hand, built only on OpenStreetMap, and generating a town took a while. He needed somewhere real to test it, so he looked up towns in Germany, where he lives, and found Arnis. A small town helped while early generation was slow.

Arnis has a lot of buildings, it has streets, it has waters, it has all kinds of different elements that are useful to test with.

The project didn’t have a name yet, so it borrowed the town’s unique moniker.

When Arnis added elevation, its worlds stopped being flat, and geospatial specialists from all over showed up with ideas on how best to integrate it. They started discussions on their own, and Louis says that’s exactly what he needed.

Bigger calls happen in the open too: before porting Arnis from Python to Rust, Louis opened discussions on GitHub to hear different perspectives. Contributors have since translated Arnis into about 20 languages and built major features of their own.

The best thing that has happened to me is essentially this big community on GitHub to see actually people from all kinds of different fields coming together to work on something that I started as a side project.

Some features start on a walk. Louis noticed how many trash bins there were in his city, and there were none in his generated worlds.

Minecraft has no dedicated bins, so he took the question to the community, and together they found a block that fit. Placing one follows the same path as everything else: for the area a user selects, Arnis queries OpenStreetMap for every mapped element, then looks up each element’s coordinates and type.

If an element isn’t mapped, Arnis can’t place it. For structures too detailed for the map to describe, like the Statue of Liberty in New York, or Christ the Redeemer in Rio de Janeiro, Arnis converts 3D models into Minecraft blocks.

As the sole maintainer, Louis reviews every pull request himself, and they stack up. He usually spends evenings or weekends working on Arnis, which means the community has to wait for him to be done with work for the day.

He uses GitHub Copilot to get an overview of open issues and bugs from the latest release, see how many people are running into them, trace where a bug came from in the codebase, and create a fix. Since he brought AI into the project, he says, code reviews and documentation have both increased.

I could delegate those annoying and boring tasks to Copilot, while I could still have a look at those really cool topics that are related to the development of it.

Big features arrive as big pull requests.

Louis opens a large pull request in the GitHub Copilot app for a first overview and a performance review, with Copilot running in the background looking for small changes that would cut memory usage or generation time.

It takes just one wrong calculation in street lights, for example, to make the generation time of an entire city much slower.

He also keeps a mental list of the areas of the codebase that need extra care whenever someone changes them, and he has written the same warnings out by hand over and over.

I’m starting a few sub agents with the GitHub Copilot app to have a look specifically at those cases, and to come up with corner cases to test to make sure that we do not introduce any regressions into new releases.

He likes that the terminal, his files, the code changes and the pull request all sit in one app next to the chat.

Louis’s favorite use of Arnis is in schools, where teachers have generated their city or a neighborhood park.

Some turn it into a contest: every student starts from the same generated area, improves a nearby park or playground using Arnis and Minecraft, and the class looks at the results together.

Recent releases have gone beyond the bounds of Earth, adding the Moon and Mars. Louis wants Arnis to stay easy to use while getting closer to reality, with more detail in less time, and he would like to see it in more schools.

Download Arnis from github.com/louis-e/arnis, pick a place on the map, choose where to spawn, and click Start Generation: within a few minutes, the world is ready to open in Minecraft. Louis suggests a place you know, like your hometown or somewhere memorable you’ve been, such as the Grand Canyon or Niagara Falls. Then look for what’s missing.

So maybe you see a bench that was not generated even though it is there.

Map it on OpenStreetMap, or tell Louis if it’s a bug and he will fix it.

Why Mary Cassatt was more than just a painter of the 'modern Madonna'

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Mary Cassatt's The Boating Party (1893-94) National Gallery of Art, Chester Dale Collection

As the centennial of the death of Mary Cassatt (1844-1926) brings renewed attention to the US-born Impressionist, a new publication challenges the image of her as a sentimental painter of mothers and daughters. In Cassatt Reconsidered, Mary G. Morton uses her expertise as a curator and head of French paintings at the National Gallery of Art in Washington, DC, to examine how the art world shaped Cassatt’s reputation, and posits a broader view of her within radical French Modernism.

The book coincides with major exhibitions on Cassatt, including at the Musée d’Orsay in Paris (until 31 January 2027), the Art Institute of Chicago (until 3 January 2027) and the New Britain Museum of American Art in Connecticut (7 November-14 February 2027). Looking closely at the artist’s work and framing her within the experience of women in a modern Paris, Morton shows why Cassatt deserves another look.

The Art Newspaper: What prompted your reassessment?

Mary G. Morton: I thought I knew enough about Cassatt until a 2014 exhibition at the National Gallery on Cassatt’s artistic pas de deux with Degas emphasising their radical technical experimentation. A few years later, an encounter with the PhD candidate Nicole Georgopulos transformed Woman with Sunflower (1905) in my mind from a generic mother and child image to a complex realist allegory, a kind of feminist manifesto responding to and supporting the contemporary suffrage movement. The public recognises Cassatt’s name but associates her with images of maternity. There is so much more to her art, to her story.

How did the art market contribute to the narrowing of Cassatt’s reputation?

Her dealer essentially marketed her as a painter of motherhood, the painter of the “modern Madonna”, hiring critics to write about her in this vein. And while he had success selling her work, the strategy did not serve her well in art historiography. Another dynamic is the burgeoning field of American art history in the 20th century, when Cassatt became an asset to curators as an Impressionist. In most major US museums, her work hangs with US artists, depriving her of the aesthetic context in radical French Modernism. She looks loud, garish, awkward, whereas if you put her with Degas, Renoir and Morisot, she fits in.

Cassatt's Woman with a Sunflower (around 1905) National Gallery of Art, Chester Dale Collection

What would you like museums, scholars and the public to see differently about Cassatt?

It would be great if Cassatt could exit the limited box she has been put into. Cassatt was the most successful avant-garde artist of the 19th century to represent the lived experience of women in modern urban Paris. She created a fresh visual vocabulary for women’s interests and concerns, refusing the oppressive social norms of her class and producing an extraordinary body of work. Cassatt brings us women’s side of the story.

The primary aim of this book is to provoke readers who might be familiar with Cassatt as a misty-eyed painter of mothers and children to look again. My hope is that the book compels people into museums to find Cassatt’s art and experience what I have: brilliantly expressive, innovative objects that relay a unique vision of humanity and of women’s lives as they were lived more than 100 years ago.

• Mary G. Morton, Cassatt Reconsidered, Getty Publications, 208pp, $35 (hb)

OpenAI introduces program to reduce AI data center energy demand during peak hours

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

This move could help data centers operate sooner without waiting for the rest of the grid to catch up to its peak demand.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Electricity consumption is one of the biggest concerns that residents bring up whenever a data center is proposed in their community, which is why OpenAI is taking steps to minimize its impact on the local electricity grid. According to the company, its Grid Protectors Program aims to reduce the power consumption of its data centers as local demand increases, ensuring that the grid is not overwhelmed during peak hours. Aside from ensuring that the lights remain on for everyone, it would also allow its data centers to connect to the grid and go online sooner instead of waiting for the additional power sources to come online.

“Whether it’s heating homes during a northern cold snap or running air conditioning through a southern heat wave, families need reliable electricity. Our goal is to make campus flexibility a resource that utilities can call on when communities need it most,” OpenAI said in its blog. It also added, “Through Grid Protectors, our ambition is to make this kind of flexibility a core part of how we build and operate future campuses. That means designing facilities to reduce electricity use when needed and working with utilities to agree on how much demand can be reduced, how quickly and for how long. The arrangements will differ by location, and we will share additional commitments as they are finalized.”

OpenAI is testing the program in its planned Effingham County campus in Georgia, with the company saying that it has already signed a 3.2-gigawatt agreement with Georgia Power that included 1,000 megawatts of flexible demand response. This ensures that if demand jumps on the hottest days of the year, there would be enough power to accommodate the hundreds of thousands of air conditioners keeping Georgians cool. With the average American home drawing around 1,200 kilowatts, the 1,000-megawatt buffer is more than enough to power around 800,000 households — nearly 20% of the state’s homes.

This technology, called demand response, has been around for decades now, where large operators reduce their demand in response to a peak in demand from other consumers. Before automation, grid operators would manually call up industrial customers like steel mills and ask them to cut consumption to ensure the reliability of the entire grid. But with modern technologies and IoT, it can now be done automatically, allowing data centers to reduce their load in under a minute. In fact, Nvidia has already been experimenting with a similar system in the UK and has proven that it works.

Hyatt Dining & Spa Awards: Are They Worth Redeeming Points For?

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

While the most popular way to redeem World of Hyatt points is for free night award redemptions, there are some lesser known ways to redeem Hyatt points. One of those is the ability to use points for Dining & Spa awards, which can be a surprisingly good deal.

This doesn’t get a lot of attention, and it’s particularly useful if you want to minimize your out-of-pocket when staying at a Hyatt property, while still indulging in on-property experiences.

Most people don’t realize that you can redeem World of Hyatt points toward the cost of on-property dining and spa experiences, and the value proposition can be surprisingly good. Specifically, you can redeem a certain number of points for a certain amount of credit to use toward on-property spending, even if you’re not staying at a Hyatt property. Let’s take a closer look at how this works.

Below is a chart showing the various redemption opportunities available with Hyatt’s Dining & Spa awards, along with how much value you can potentially get per point:

As you can see, you can redeem anywhere from 1,000 points for a $10 credit, to 65,000 points for a $1,000 credit. The value per point ranges anywhere from 1.0 cents per point, to 1.54 cents per point.

1.54 cents per World of Hyatt point sounds like a solid deal. Based on my latest miles & points valuations, World of Hyatt points are worth 1.4 cents each, so this actually offers solid value, at least on the surface. Is there a catch, though?

The exact redemptions that are allowed vary by hotel, but generally you can expect that these awards can be used for food and beverage purchases, spa and salon services, and spa and salon retail items.

On top of that, when charged to a member’s guest room, the following charges may be eligible:

laundry, dry cleaning, telephone charges, pay-per-view movies, parking, gift/retail shop, service charges, taxes (not including room taxes) and gratuities

Meanwhile the following charges are expressly prohibited from being eligible:

room rates, room taxes, alcoholic beverages (in certain jurisdictions), catering or banquet charges, and casino charges

If you’re staying at a Hyatt then you should be able to redeem these awards for cumulative charges over the course of your stay. However, I’d recommend confirming this with the front desk at check-in, including which outlets would be eligible for these redemptions, since individual hotels could have different policies.

Not all Hyatt hotels participate in this program. Miraval, Unscripted by Hyatt, Hyatt Select, Destination by Hyatt, Hyatt Vacation Club properties, Hyatt Vivid, Bahia Principe, Impression by Secrets, Secrets, Dreams, Breathless, Zoëtry, Alua, Sunscape, and other Legacy AMR Collection resorts, as well as some Dream properties, do not participate. Furthermore, redemption of these certificates is limited to registered guests at Hyatt Zilara, Hyatt Ziva, and Hyatt Studios properties.

To be positive, you can search participating properties at this page.

You don’t need to redeem for these certificates in advance, but rather the World of Hyatt member should just inform the Hyatt employee at the eligible outlet that they’d like to redeem points toward their purchase at the outset. You’ll need to have your membership information, and should then be asked how many points you want to redeem.

If you’re a registered guest at a hotel then you can apply one of these at check-out toward multiple purchases over the course of a stay. If you’re not a registered guest then you’ll have to settle your folio at the particular outlet you’re at.

There are a few other things to potentially be aware of:

On the surface, being able to redeem World of Hyatt points for up to ~1.54 cents toward the cost of a purchase is a solid deal. Is it exceptional? No. But as a way of essentially “cashing out” your points to offset cash costs I think it’s very good.

I do think it’s important to create a more realistic valuation of these redemptions, though. When you redeem these awards you’re forgoing the opportunity to earn points on these purchases:

As a Globalist member I earn 10.5x points per dollar spent, which I value at a return of ~15% (1.4 cents per point). That’s an opportunity cost that’s worth factoring in, so in reality, I’d adjust the maximum value from 1.54 cents to ~1.3 cents.

Personally, this isn’t how I’d redeem my World of Hyatt points, though I do think many people could find this to be worthwhile for a splurge:

World of Hyatt offers Dining & Spa awards, allowing members to redeem points for dining and spa experiences at most Hyatt properties. This has the potential to be a solid deal, as getting 1.54 cents per Hyatt point toward the cost of a purchase is a good redemption. However, do factor in the points you’re forgoing, which brings down the redemption value a bit.

While I wouldn’t say this is the best way to redeem Hyatt points, this could be worth it to lower how much cash you’ll pay for a Hyatt stay, especially at a secluded resort where you’re likely to spend most time on-property.

What do you make of Hyatt’s Dining & Spa awards? Could you see yourself redeeming points for them?

Stubborn Asteroids Around White Dwarfs Resist Becoming Dust

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

White dwarfs are the remnants of a stars like our Sun. For their sizeâthey're roughly the size of Earthâthey're extremely dense, and have about as much mass as the Sun. That means they have enormous gravitational power, enough power to tear apart objects that get too close.

For a long time, white dwarfs appeared to be alone in space, not including any stellar companions. Then observations showed that some of their surfaces were polluted with metals. Astronomers surmised that these metals were raining down onto their surfaces from rocky debris. Then came observations that some white dwarfs were surrounded by debris disks. After that, even more piercing observations found evidence of rocky bodies in the disks: small planets, moons, or asteroids.

An example of these is WD 1145+017, which seems to be orbited by several small rocky bodies, being torn apart and raining debris down onto the white dwarf's surface.

Scientists are curious about this process and how effectively white dwarfs can shred smaller bodies and form debris disks. As they find white dwarfs with disks, asteroids, and even planetary systems, explanations need to keep pace.

In new research in The Astrophysical Journal Letters, researchers examined how efficiently white dwarf tidal forces can break asteroids apart. The research is titled "Size Limits on Tidal Debris around White Dwarfs: The Kilometer-size Barrier." The lead author is Jordan K. Steckloff, senior scientist at the Planetary Science Institute.

"Compact disks of planetary debris orbiting white dwarfs provide a crucial window into our understanding of evolved planetary systems," the authors write. "The formation of these disks has been widely modeled with tidal fragmentation of minor planets that are rubble piles with no internal strength."

This artist's illustration shows a white dwarf surrounded by a debris disk, with asteroidal fragments in the foreground. The more astronomers find white dwarfs with debris disks, the more they want to understand how they're created. Image Credit: NASAâs Goddard Space Flight Center/Scott Wiessinger

That's a problem because real rubble piles have some cohesive strength. Also, all three of the asteroids we've sampledâItokawa, Ryugu, and Bennuâare rubble piles. The force that holds these types of asteroids together is called the Van der Waals force. It's a weak, short-range force that attracts and repulses atoms and molecules. But as gecko feet show, it can be very effective.

In their work, Steckloff and his co-authors show what happens when this strength is included in modelling: "breakup of these rubble piles sets a maximum fragment size, and we calculate this size for water ice, iron, and material densities corresponding to the lunar highlands, Vesta, and the Earth," they write.

âNothing is truly strengthless,â Steckloff said. âTiny attractions between grains called Van der Waals forces give comets and asteroids a minimum strength 10 to 100 times weaker than pulling apart freshly fallen snow.â

According to this research, the typical internal strength of a rubble pile is â¼10â1000 Pa, which is about one million times weaker than solid rock. In that range, the maximum size for fragments is about the same as small asteroids, between about 0.1 and 1 km in diameter. "This limitâthe kilometer-size barrierâalso represents the characteristic sizes of tidal fragments," the authors explain. "Most of the debris mass is contained in fragments of this size."

These panels show characteristic debris size by distance from a white dwarf with about 0.6 solar masses and cohesive strength. The panel on the left is for a strength of 10 pascals, and the panel on the right is for a strength of 1 kilopascal. The results show characteristic fragment sizes of about 100 meters and 1,000 meters respectively. Image Credit: Jordan K. Steckloff et al 2026 ApJL 1004 L27

The authors say that as a consquence, models of disk formation around white dwarfs need to include another dust-generating factor in order to explain the dusty disks in observations. That likely means collisions rather than tidal forces.

The force that shapes the end-state of the disk is called PoyntingâRobertson drag. It explains how solar radiation affects dust grains around stars, including white dwarfs, making the dust spiral inward. This only works if the dust grains are small enough to be subjected to the drag but not so large that radiation pressure drives them away. This work shows that fragmentation itself isn't enough to create dust this small.

Observations in our Solar System agree with these results. When the comet Shoemaker-Levy 9 approached Jupiter in 1992, the gas giant's powerful tidal force pulled the comet apart. But not into dust; instead, the comet was torn into fragments 100m to 1,000m in diameter, which then plunged into the planet.

This Hubble Space Telescope image shows comet Shoemaker-Levy 9 broken into fragments after it approached Jupiter in 1992. Two years later the fragments approached Jupiter again and plunged into its atmosphere. Image Credit: NASA.

The research extends beyond asteroid fragment sizes in disks. It also shows that fragments maintain their orbits. They experience only tiny, fractional changes in their velocities "on the order of one part per million to one part per 100 million," the authors explain. "Such tiny changes in speed result in negligible changes to the orbits of the fragments, which remain in practically the same orbit as prior to disruption."

There are a lot of factors involved in the creation of a white dwarf's dust disk, and this work places an important constraint on one of them. "Understanding the progression from an intact minor planet to a sea of dust that is accreted onto a white dwarf is an outstanding challenge," the authors write. "In this Letter, we demonstrate a sharp constraint on the first step of this process." The work shows that some type of grinding process like collisions is required to generate white dwarf dust disks.

âUnderstanding the details of how debris around white dwarfs is produced and evolves is important for putting the observations into context,â said study co-author Kat Volk from the Planetary Science Institute. âThis will help us understand what happens to the small bodies in orbit around a star as it undergoes the end stages of its life.â

This Is Not Your Ordinary Flu Season

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

The CDC released new data that changes what to expect this season.

EVERY FLU SEASON is different. Some come earlier, others later. Some are mild, while others—like last year—barrel through like a tornado. Every year, scientists do their best to predict what type of influenza virus will hit the US based on data from countries in the Southern Hemisphere whose flu seasons come earlier than ours.

The key word here is predict. Since the influenza virus mutates quickly, it’s hard for scientists to give a 100 percent guarantee that what’s ravaging Australia will also come for the US. That’s what happened in the brutal flu season last year—and this year.

A few weeks ago, scientists gave a forecast of H3N2 (that’s the one dubbed “Super-K”) as the dominant flu strain for the US. But early surveillance in flu cases from the West Coast found that a different type—Influenza A virus subtype H1N1—has overtaken H3N2 as the dominant flu strain in October. The CDC also released new flu data reporting a spike in flu-related hospitalizations.

Does H1N1 sound eerily familiar? H1N1 is also known as swine flu—a strain that sparked a pandemic back in 2009. So far, researchers have linked early flu ER visits to a mutation in H1N1 (a G155E mutation) that changes the way the virus attaches to and infects cells. Past research has found antibodies from past H1N1 infections or vaccinations may not recognize it, especially in younger populations. In other words, there is a chance the mutated influenza virus could evade some pre-existing immunity.

Here is what you need to know about this early jump to flu season and how to best protect yourself against this mutated strain.

HERE’S WHAT THE CDC learned: In mid-October this year, the agency saw 10 times more hospitalizations for flu than it did in 2025 (2.1 hospitalizations per 100,000 people compared to 0.2 per 100,000 during the same week last year).

Among other changes are when and where the virus is spreading. While influenza cases typically tick up around fall, infections began rising in August. The boost of early cases was mostly on the West Coast, including Arizona, Nevada, and California. Hawaii and Louisiana are already at moderate levels. Typically, the US flu season starts in the South.

Flu activity remains low nationally. But as of October 28, the CDC estimates that infections are growing or likely growing in 39 states. The numbers coincide with early reports of flu activity ramping up on the East Coast in states including Pennsylvania and New York.

WHILE SCIENTISTS INITIALLY predicted a “Super-K” flu strain to be dominant, this year’s vaccine accounts for multiple flu strains. The current flu shots will target three strains: H1N1, B/Victoria lineage, and H3N2. It’s too soon to see if the vaccine will fully protect against the mutated H1N1 strain, but it’s still expected to provide some meaningful protection.

“A mismatch is not a black-or-white situation. We may have less immunity, not no immunity,” Andrew Pekosz, PhD, virologist at the Johns Hopkins Bloomberg School of Public Health told TODAY.com. You can also take some reassurance from last year when people who still got the mismatched flu shot were still less likely to have severe illness and be hospitalized than those who were unvaccinated.

ALONG WITH YOUR traditional flu shot, adults ages 50 and over are eligible for a new flu vaccine called mFLUSIVA made with the same mRNA technology as the COVID vaccines. A phase 3 clinical trial on the mFLUSIVA vaccine found it was more effective at preventing illness from influenza virus subtypes A and B than a standard vaccine. Reminder, the dominant strain right now is Influenza A virus subtype H1N1.

Of the 20,179 people injected with the new shot, only 411 developed a lab-confirmed case (2 percent) 14 days after vaccination during flu season last year. Meanwhile, 557 of 20,124 recipients of the standard vaccine got sick with the flu (2.8 percent).

One caveat, however, was that the new flu vaccine has a greater chance of severe side effects. In the clinical trial, people were more likely to report injection site pain, fatigue, headaches, and muscle weakness.

Don’t want a shot? That’s fine! You also have your choice of a nasal flu vaccine a.k.a. FluMist if you’re ages 2 to 49. “We tend to develop less of an immune response in the nose as we get older, which means the nasal spray vaccine doesn’t work as well in older groups,” John Sellick, DO, infectious disease expert and professor of medicine at the University at Buffalo, previously told MH.

Ultimately, time isn’t running out yet to get yourself armed and ready for the US flu season. The influenza virus is fast and mutates easily, but vaccination remains your best option to weather the storm.

Jocelyn Solis-Moreira, MS is the associate health & fitness for Men's Health and has previously written for CNN, Scientific American, Popular Science, and National Geographic before joining the brand. When she's not working, she's doing circus arts or working towards the perfect pull-up.

Former U.S. Government Contractor Pleads Guilty to Spying for Ethiopia

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

An official website of the United States government

Official websites use .gov A .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPS A lock ( Lock Locked padlock ) or https:// means you’ve safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

Abraham Teklu Lemma, 53, of Maryland, a former information technology contractor at the National Reconnaissance Office, the U.S. Department of State, and the U.S. Department of Justice pleaded guilty today to conspiring to commit espionage on behalf of the government of the Federal Democratic Republic of Ethiopia.

Lemma pleaded guilty to conspiracy to transmit national defense information to a foreign government and has agreed to be incarcerated for a period of between 120 and 262 months.

“Lemma betrayed the United States to an extraordinary degree,” said Assistant Attorney General for National Security John A. Eisenberg. “He exploited his IT administrator access to remove classified national defense information, including Top Secret information, from a secure facility and transmitted it to Ethiopian government officials hoping they would use it to target and kill paramilitary fighters in a regional conflict. Clearance holders pledge undivided loyalty to the United States and bear a legal and moral responsibility to protect classified information. Those who betray this trust will be prosecuted to the fullest extent of the law.”

“For more than two years, Lemma exploited a position of trust to provide highly classified U.S. government information to a foreign government,” said U.S. Attorney Jeanine Ferris Pirro for the District of Columbia. “In doing so, he betrayed his oath, his colleagues, and his country, while jeopardizing our national security. His guilty plea sends a clear message: those who compromise America’s most sensitive secrets will be investigated, prosecuted, and held accountable.”

“Despite swearing an oath to defend the Constitution of the United States, Abraham Lemma betrayed our country by committing espionage on behalf of the Ethiopian government,” said Assistant Director Roman Rozhavsky of the FBI’s Counterintelligence and Espionage Division. “By his own admission, Lemma provided classified national defense information to Ethiopian officials, who then used it in military operations during an ongoing conflict. This guilty plea should serve notice that the FBI and our partners will work tirelessly to stop foreign intelligence services, and anyone who decides to work with them, against the interests of the United States.”

“Today’s guilty plea makes clear that any attempt to betray the United States, violate the trust of the American people, and compromise our national security will not be tolerated,” said Assistant Director in Charge Darren B. Cox of the FBI Washington Field Office. “Lemma abused his position of trust and access to some of our nation’s most highly classified information, conspiring with foreign government officials to unlawfully disclose materials protected to the highest level. In doing so, he not only endangered our national security but also betrayed the trust placed in him by the American people.”

According to court documents, Lemma held a Top Secret U.S. security clearance. From 2021 until his arrest in August 2023, Lemma conspired with senior Ethiopian government officials to collect and transmit dozens of highly classified documents, notes, materials, and photographs, classified up to the Top Secret/Sensitive Compartmented Information (TS/SCI) level, to Ethiopian government officials. Material identified as “Top Secret” means that unauthorized disclosure of the material is reasonably to cause “exceptionally grave damage to the national security” of the United States.

Much of the highly classified information Lemma shared with the Ethiopian government concerned an armed conflict in Ethiopia. Lemma’s conspirators included senior officials within political, military, and intelligence branches of the Ethiopian government.

During his espionage activities, Lemma took numerous steps to conceal his conduct. and surreptitiously removed classified documents and information from U.S. government holdings.

Specifically, Lemma wrote notes by hand with classified information and then hand-carried those notes out of secure U.S. government facilities. Additionally, as an IT administrator, Lemma removed electronic versions of classified information from classified U.S. government repositories and then removed the same classified information from secure U.S. government facilities where he worked.

On some occasions, Lemma encouraged his Ethiopian government conspirators to take direct action against paramilitary groups who were fighting the government of Ethiopia. For example, on Dec. 18, 2021, Lemma told one conspirator, “We have to finish these people once and for all, otherwise there will never be peace in Ethiopia.” Later that day, Lemma shared additional information with his conspirator and wrote, “Please send Dro[nes].”

According to the plea agreement, Lemma agreed to plead guilty to conspiracy to transmit national defense information to a foreign government. If accepted by the court, Lemma has agreed to be incarcerated for a period of between 120 and 262 months. Sentencing is set for April 14, 2027.

The investigation was conducted by the FBI Washington Field Office.

The matter is being prosecuted by Assistant U.S. Attorneys Tejpal Chawla and Adam P. Barry for the District of Columbia, and Trial Attorney Kathryn DeMarco of the Department of Justice’s National Security Division.

Delta CEO Calls Heathrow Move ‘Short-Sighted’

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Delta CEO Ed Bastian is pushing back against a planned terminal move at London Heathrow, calling the airport’s decision “short-sighted, deeply disappointing and bad for airline consumers.”

In comments posted Monday on LinkedIn and published by Delta, Bastian objected to Heathrow’s decision to relocate the airline from Terminal 3 to Terminal 4. The move, planned for 2028, would separate Delta from joint venture partner Virgin Atlantic, which would remain in Terminal 3.

Bastian said the airlines’ shared operation has served millions of passengers for more than a decade, providing connections, premium amenities and access to their combined networks. He argued that splitting the carriers would undermine years of investment and the benefits of operating from the same terminal.

Delta will “pursue all available remedies to correct this decision,” he said, urging Heathrow to reconsider. Bastian did not outline specific legal or regulatory steps.

Virgin Atlantic has also challenged the plan. CEO Corneel Koster said separating the two airlines would complicate journeys, weaken competition and reduce passenger choice. The carrier is considering its options to contest the relocation, Sky News reported.

Heathrow says the changes will help ease congestion and make better use of its terminals. The airport began notifying airlines of the outcome of its terminal occupancy review on Oct. 27.

A Heathrow spokesperson told Sky News that the plan followed consultation with airline customers and would improve the passenger experience, strengthen operational resilience and provide room for growth.

Delta is one of five airlines slated to change terminals. Finnair and Japan Airlines would also move from Terminal 3 to Terminal 4, while LATAM would relocate from Terminal 3 to Terminal 2. China Southern would move from Terminal 4 to Terminal 5.

Delta consolidated its Heathrow operation in Terminal 3 in September 2016 to operate alongside Virgin Atlantic. In announcing that move, Delta cited easier connections and last-minute flight changes, along with access to Virgin Atlantic’s premium facilities for eligible passengers.

Is Yellowstone National Park (Back) in Its Wild West Era?

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

A crowd waits for Old Faithful geyser in Yellowstone (Photo: Grant Faint / Getty)

I was sitting on my tailgate, listening to the sounds of songbirds and car honks, when a scuffle broke out in a nearby picnic area. Shouts echoed from the woods as a crowd swarmed two dudes who were shoving and swinging at each other.

Welcome to a hot summer day in Yellowstone National Park. The tussling group seemed to be a motorcycle club, based on their headwear and matching T-shirts. Given their sweaty disposition, I’m not sure “staying hydrated” was listed in the bylaws.

“This place is nuts,” said my wife, returning from the restroom as the two dudes were separated. “What happened here?”

“Fist fight at the Old Faithful corral.” I shrugged.

Then we embarked on a long trek through the semi-apocalyptic parking lot. Frantic drivers whipped around corners, cutting each other off. Exasperated families double-parked and hurried away. Frustrated sightseers returned to their blocked vehicles and laid on the horn. Finally, my wife and I breathed a sigh of relief as we reached the safer confines of the world’s most active hydrothermal zone, the scalding Upper Geyser Basin.

Yellowstone seems to be having a moment as the poster child for rising threats facing the National Park Service (NPS), including bad visitor behavior. While the parks attract record-setting crowds, the Trump administration simultaneously gutted the workforce, cutting NPS staff by nearly 25 percent since 2025. Interpretive signs about important topics like Indigenous displacement and slavery were torn down and replaced by nationalistic propaganda. More trash than ever was recently pulled from Yellowstone geysers, including strange stuff like fidget spinners, fake nails, and a baseball cap displaying the words cowboy hat.

For outdoor enthusiasts watching these disturbing events, it can feel like there’s never been a worse time for national parks such as Yellowstone. My wife, who’d never been there before, was shocked by the wackier things we saw during one high-season day. I arrived better prepared, having come to Yellowstone a grand total of TWO times. My first visit happened 20 years before, when some young whitewater paddlers and I came through on a cross-country road trip. The park felt somewhat calmer back then, but by no means sane. There were about 3 million visitors that year, instead of about 4.5 million now.

Back then, occasional thrill seekers with camcorders chased wildlife, while today’s park paparazzi are influencers with smartphones. Then, like now, I saw a few tourists hopping off boardwalks and stepping across dangerous thermal crusts. I don’t think I’ve ever witnessed anyone stick their fingers in toxic waters, but I’ve definitely seen videos on Tourons of Yellowstone. Each time I’ve gone, we talked about the poor souls who fell and the impulsive daredevils who flung themselves into acidic hot springs, where they were fatally burned or entirely dissolved.

After my first visit, I mostly gave Yellowstone a wide berth. Later, I returned to the mountains and canyons of Wyoming, exploring real places and stories from the Wild West era for a book, Discovering the Outlaw Trail. That’s how I learned that Yellowstone National Park never saw any “good old days,” though some periods have been better or worse for various reasons.

In 1872, when President Ulysses S. Grant signed the first national park into law, word had already spread in the East. Yellowstone was a landscape like no other, filled with abundant wildlife, erupting geysers, and plunging waterfalls. For ages, it was home to nomadic and resident Native peoples like the Mountain Shoshone. Now this volcanic plateau in the Rocky Mountains was threatened by westward expansion of the United States.

For a decade, no more than roughly a thousand visitors made the long and jolting stagecoach journey. Yet the bad behavior started immediately. Seeking souvenirs, vandals hammered away at mineral deposits. Poachers slaughtered countless bison, elk, and bears for their hides, leaving fields of carcasses. Squatters built illegal cabins within the boundaries, hoping the park law would be repealed. Tourists tossed trash, timber, and soap into geysers like Old Faithful. When the erupting fountains sent foaming debris sky high, onlookers cheered. The handful of park staffers watched on, horrified.

After the railroad arrived in 1883, things only got worse. With growing crowds came petty pickpockets, horse thieves, and gunslinging outlaws. Bandits hid behind bends in the road and robbed the passing tourist wagons one by one. The largest stagecoach robberies in U.S. history were said to have happened in Yellowstone. Not once, but three times.

The reason for the mayhem? During the lean federal budgets following the Civil War, Congress had been willing to establish the park, but they weren’t willing to fund it. With limited rules and near-zero enforcement, Yellowstone developed a reputation as a fun place to get away with things.

During our summer day in Yellowstone, my wife and I managed to break away from the crowds. To start, we took a six-mile hike through quieter parts of Upper Geyser Basin, which reminded us why this is a special place worth protecting.

We watched the angled eruption of Daisy Geyser with maybe a dozen fellow visitors. Then we walked past Punch Bowl Spring to a unique feature that few visit, Black Sand Pool. Under our feet, the ground tremored with loud thumps, the result of steam implosions happening beneath the pool’s surface.

During our hike, we met a friendly Oregon family that ranks Yellowstone among their favorite parks. To avoid the craziness, they devised a unique itinerary, staying a few nights each at several park lodges and hiking outward during the quieter mornings. In most ways, this family resembles the majority of parkgoers. Hikers who love the parks, follow the rules, and appreciate the rangers. While poor behavior may be increasing again, such offenders remain a highly visible minority of visitors.

That said, without a few key decisions made during the fading days of the Wild West, Yellowstone might have gone bad altogether.

Tucked away in the woods bordering Norris Geyser Basin, there’s a log building overlooking a small river. Today, this old Army post ironically holds the Museum of the National Park Ranger. Inside, a bronze bust depicts Harry Yount, a legendary figure from Yellowstone history. If the frontiersman’s face looks particularly tired, well, there’s a reason for that.

In 1880, as this nascent national park was being overrun by poachers and miscreants, the meager budget was enough to hire exactly one game warden. He was expected to single-handedly patrol over 3,300 square miles of parkland. Instead, Yount quit in protest after 14 months, reporting that one guy couldn’t do a job that required a well-funded ranger service.

And guess what, the government listened! It just took 35 years. In the meantime, they tried pretty much anything else. They invited some vigilante cowboys, offering them half the collected fines. Naturally, these volunteers avoided the gunslingers and targeted wealthier tourists. So, the U.S. Army was sent in. For decades, half-hearted soldiers chased outlaws beyond park boundaries. The soldiers went back to their posts and waited until the villains cartoonishly returned.

In 1916, the National Park Service was established. Finally, professional rangers assumed responsibility for management, interpretation, and enforcement within an improving park system. Disorder gradually decreased across the West and in Yellowstone, but not entirely. The national parks went on to become a beloved feature of the U.S.

The story of Yellowstone’s destructive early days, and its first park ranger, offers a cautionary lesson that’s easily forgotten: when crowds increase and park budgets and staffing decrease, lawbreakers rise again. We’re seemingly back where we started, with Yellowstone in its Wild West era again.
