<b><strong>I’m a Run Coach. Here’s Why a Marathon Doesn't Have to Be Your Ultimate Running Goal</strong></b>

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

This time of year, after most fall marathons have ended and our feeds have seen weeks of people crossing finish lines at some of the biggest in the world in Berlin, Chicago, and New York, it’s really hard to avoid feeling like you should run a marathon. I personally have posted countless videos of myself on the internet saying, “I’m never doing this again,” and even I’m tempted to go back on my word every November.

To be fair, I love the marathon. I’ve run 12 of them. I’ve run them very, very fast. And I’ve run them very, very slow. My first one changed my life. It gave me the courage to move to New York City and try to figure out what to do after graduating college. Six months later, I became one of the first running influencers, and I’ve spent the 12 years since building a global community, Badass Lady Gang, to help thousands of women belong and redefine their relationship with strength, power, and what it means to be a runner.

But I also feel complicated feelings about the marathon. As a running coach, I’ve watched the same conversation happen online for over a decade. Somewhere along the way, newer runners are told, or quietly absorb, that the marathon is the ultimate finish line. Which makes sense, right? Our brains love order and hierarchy. When you think about what it means to progress in running, it feels logical: going faster and farther are straightforward ways to show you’ve improved. But that’s not how it works. The marathon isn’t the objective ultimate goal. Or always the most fun one. And, it’s definitely not something that every runner needs to chase.

Every year I watch too many runners struggle through the training not because they lack commitment or drive, but because they’re chasing a goal they didn’t fully want or that their life can’t accommodate right now. And honestly, it’s just a really hard distance to master! A fellow coach and pillar in the community, Peter Bromka, put it best: “The marathon owes you nothing.” I hate that he’s right. You spend months killing yourself (metaphorically) for one huge goal, and then anything can happen on the day. It’s too hot. You get hurt despite doing everything right. You have an off day and watch your goals fly out the window.

We’re in the largest running boom the sport has ever seen, and I think it’s largely because we’re lonely. One in five Americans said they always or often felt lonely or socially isolated in a 2018 study by the Kaiser Family Foundation. And the stakes are real: the U.S. Surgeon General has warned that loneliness carries health risks comparable to smoking up to 15 cigarettes a day. Running is one of the few activities that genuinely makes a dent in the loneliness epidemic because pretty much anyone can be a runner (even though it is easier and more accessible for some more than others). You can be fast, slow, old, young—if you’re lacing up your sneakers and moving, you’re a runner. Come marathon season, we also hear stories of the incredible adaptive athletes who cross finish lines with a range of disabilities, too.

Basically, there’s a reason run clubs are hot right now. They give people a way to put their phones down and find a community. The more you show up, the more you see people of all shapes, sizes, ages, fitness levels, and backgrounds crushing distances you thought weren’t possible for someone like you. Before you know it, you’ve signed up for your first half marathon and everyone is asking when you’ll run a marathon. Once you get on that path, it’s hard to stop.

Loneliness isn’t the only thing fueling the boom. We’ve also lost the sense that we control our livelihoods, our schedules, our futures. A marathon is something you choose. It’s one place where you get to call the shots: you set the goal, decide how to train, and define what success looks like, even when curveballs come. It gives you back agency in a world that feels uncontrollable. And in an age when we produce our lives online, it looks great on a feed. It’s badass to train for a marathon, especially when you feel like you have nothing else exciting to share.

So yeah, it’s not just a feeling that everyone’s running a marathon lately. It’s real. Over 1.1 million people applied to London. Chicago saw a 12.2 percent jump in participation from 2023. New York jumped 15.1 percent in the same time period. Races sell out in hours. Getting a bib is almost as hard as finishing 26.2 miles. That’s not an accident.

I work in this industry, and I can tell you a lot of money is spent each year making you feel like you should run a marathon. Last year, New York Road Runners, the nonprofit behind the New York City Marathon, spent $3.8 million on advertising and promotion. And much of that content is built around one image: a runner crossing the finish line, arms overhead, tears in their eyes, sharing how they became the person they always dreamed of being. It works. It makes you believe transformation is possible, and it is. But the marathon industry has weaponized our deep desires to belong, to achieve, to become, and forced us on 26.2-mile slogs across our respective cities.

The result: This overwhelming pressure that everyone needs to get out there and run a marathon—and an exponentially growing number of sign-ups. Never in a million years did I think we’d get to a place where getting into a marathon was almost as hard as running it. But here we are.

I spent a decade being paid by the biggest brands in the world to promote the marathon. I built my business on being vulnerable online and sharing my own transformation through running. So believe me when I say: Not everyone needs to run a marathon.

Before I tell you why you don’t need to run a marathon, let me explain why I first decided to run one. It was never something I envisioned for myself. In my mind, “real” runners ran marathons. (Kooks, if I’m being honest.) Then, I graduated from college with a theater degree and had to start paying off my student loans. My community scattered. And I couldn’t run from the grief I’d suppressed after losing my brother my freshman year of college in a tragic accident.

On Thanksgiving morning in 2012, I started actually running from my problems. Running was brutal and wonderful in a way that left no room for my feelings. It demanded every ounce of attention just to keep putting one foot in front of the other or fall over and pray for a quick end.

I know. Dramatic. But I was the person who made the jokes about only running from the cops. Or a bear. I missed over 70 days of school my senior year ditching PE to avoid running. But desperate times! Running really did feel like a lifeline.

Running gave me structure and something to feel excited about. Becoming a runner gave me something to work toward and confidence when friends and family asked me what my plans were now that I graduated from college. Instead of saying, “I don’t know if I’ll ever work a day in my life doing the thing I love most,” I could say, “I’m training to run a marathon” and feel pride and purpose. They would look at me gobsmacked and impressed. “YOU! Running a marathon? WOAH!” Yeah, I’d think to myself beaming with pride. ME.

I went from couch to marathon in seven months. The running magazines I turned to for advice told me I shouldn’t do what I was doing. “You should run for at least a year,” “Going from couch to marathon shows you don’t respect the distance.” (Those were actual pieces of advice in articles I remember reading when I was training for my first marathon.) But I wasn’t running for a time goal. I felt like I was running for my life. I had to do it.

Training was horrible in the most fulfilling way. This was 2013 and learning to run was very different from what it is now. There were blogs written by former high school or collegiate runners or message boards with the type of runners who told you you didn’t belong if you loved to hate running like I did or ran slow. I didn’t know what I was doing. I made every single mistake you could possibly make training for and then running a marathon.

The biggest? I wasn’t ready. I grabbed a free plan from an app, ramped up my mileage way too fast, and got hurt almost immediately. I ran in shoes a size and a half too small. I didn’t know how to take energy gels (and ran the race with an absurd number of them sandwiched between my two sports bras). I wore nipple guards made for men, terrified my nipples would chafe.

And despite all the mistakes and missteps, running my first marathon saved my life. When I crossed that finish line, I immediately had this sense of if I could do that, there really isn’t anything I can’t figure out how to do.

I’ll never forget watching my first New York City Marathon from the sidelines the year I moved here. We were near the finish, at Columbus Circle, and I knew immediately that I had to run it the next year. I felt so proud to be one of the crazy people who had run a marathon, and so connected to what the runners were going through. There’s nothing like belonging to a group that has shared something so grueling and rewarding. And so, one marathon turned into two and before I knew it, I was a person who ran marathons for fun.

Coaching changed everything. I’ve watched a mom of three in her 40s break two and a half hours in the half marathon after years of believing she’d never be fast enough. I’ve watched countless women who’d been convinced they couldn’t be runners, because of what they endured in PE class, finally feel strong in their bodies while training to run a fast mile. I’ve watched women facing everything from depression to addiction pick up running as a tool and use it to get through the hardest stretch of their lives. And I realized the same joy, transformation, and belonging happen at every distance. There is no hierarchy.

But, it’s more than that. Oftentimes, when we talk about the marathon, we don’t grapple enough with the reasons it may be easier for some. Back in my 20s, the marathon was genuinely easier. Everything is easier in our 20s, even if it doesn’t feel like it at the time. We have bodies that recover much quicker, time that’s actually our own, and energy that feels endless.

I had all of that when I ran my first marathon at 24. I was braver, more naive (a gift!) and had no idea how to sit still in discomfort. I thought movement was the quickest way through, so I threw myself fully into crossing finish lines. (Funny enough, taking a break from marathons taught me what it means to sit in the hurt and actually do the work to heal.) That doesn’t mean that once you hit 30 or 40, your hopes of running a marathon are out the window, but it does mean that as you get older, you should be kinder to yourself if things feel hard or don’t look as shiny as they may seem online.

Which brings me to something we don’t say enough: a lot of the runners we look up to or love following on social media—the ones who are making us feel like we have to be a part of this—are often younger, and what we see is a story they’re producing. When you’re feeling inspired by a creator, or measuring your own training, chip times, or total races against theirs, remember who and what you’re comparing yourself to. This may be someone whose whole job is to run and work out, who has access to endless training and recovery resources, and who isn’t sharing the full story about their highs and lows. Social media literacy matters. Without it, we’re measuring ourselves against a bar that wasn’t built with us in mind.

When runners come to me and are deciding whether to throw their name into a marathon lottery or sign up, here’s what I tell them.

Not pressured. Not because you think it’s the logical next step. Compelled. That pull that screams, “I have to try this,” or “This matters to me more than anything else in the world.”

Marathon training is long and it beats you down, both physically and mentally. As a coach, my job is to help my runners uncover what’s really driving them: their real why. Because your why is the only thing that gets you out the door when you’re tired, sore, and regretting your entire decision. It’s the north star that keeps you lacing up on vacation, when your kid is sick, on a lunch break, before a wedding, on an 89-degree day, when every part of you is done. Without it, you’re just suffering for the sake of suffering.

It really is an unpaid (and expensive) part-time job. It requires hours of running and strength training every week—for months. It requires flexibility in your schedule and will leave you feeling exhausted, hangry, and spread thin for at least 16 to 20 weeks, depending on what your running looked like before you actually started marathon training.

And don’t even get me started on timing. You’re either training in the dead of winter where it’s freezing, dark, and icy when you wake up and get off work or in the summer when you’re juggling summer vacations and brutal heat.

How do you feel about running 15 miles around the Grand Canyon or in Italy? Is that doable? What about the weddings on your travel schedule? What do your weekends look like for the next four to five months? If you sign up for a marathon, they look like a whole bunch of staying in, waking up early to run double-digit miles, and obsessing over the forecast for this weekend’s long run.

Moms. Caretakers. People with busy, unpredictable lives.

I spend half my coaching time talking athletes off the ledge when life throws wrenches in their training and they feel like they’re failing. You end up traveling relentlessly for work and aren’t comfortable running in the dark in a city you don’t know. Or you hate the treadmill and have to do most of your training on one. Your kids get back-to-back-to-back sicknesses and you spend four weeks not able to run because you’re fighting off what they had. A parent ends up in the hospital. A partner gets a diagnosis no one saw coming. Extreme weather, which is becoming a new normal for many of us, makes training complicated or even inaccessible. The list goes on and on. Anything can and will happen during marathon training.

The question isn’t, “How bad do you want it?” That’s not how life works. No excuses? Tell that to a parent who doesn’t have reliable help. It's whether you have the time—real, uninterrupted time—to train the way you envision and the wiggle room to adjust to any and all of the above scenarios.

It’s about so much more than everyone posting their curated, perfect peak weeks.

Influencers know what to say to speak to whatever insecurities or fears you’re feeling. Or what to say to get just enough engagement, or outrage, to get our numbers higher. Running a marathon is big business and most of your favorite influencers are making a lot of money sharing their stories. Sometimes, it honestly feels like exploitation. Which is gross. But I wish more people understood that no one is sharing their actual lives.

The second you start filming yourself, whether you’re aware of it or not, you’re producing. You’re making choices that will resonate or earn engagement. So whether someone is sharing the highlight reel or highlighting everything that’s going wrong, remember that you will never know what’s actually happening.

Keep that in mind if you catch yourself comparing your undertrained or ordinary reality to someone else's seemingly perfect one. And keep it in mind when you relate to someone who says their training has been a train wreck too. I can tell you firsthand that making running interesting and entertaining is very, very hard.

If you struggle with perfectionism, marathon training may feel like you’re riding the struggle bus. But if you can train, show up imperfectly, and work with your perfectionism, look at social media with a grain of salt, and feel proud of what you could do, then you're in for an incredible ride.

I’m here to remind you that not everyone needs to run a marathon to transform their life, find belonging, or to have a great time being a part of something spectacular. I say this as someone who loves, reveres, and had her life changed because of the marathon: It really is just a distance. Real change doesn’t come from running 26.2 miles. It comes from specificity and intention and showing up for yourself, whatever that distance is.

There are so many other goals you can pursue with running that will give you the same result. Have you ever gone all in on a 5K? Or a 10K? Spent 16 weeks working to see how fast you can run a mile? Run the perimeter of your town with a group of friends? Can you train for a half marathon with a friend who has never done it before and be their cheerleader every step of the way? These aren’t less impressive goals. They’re your goals. And they hit just as hard.

Goals are empty without meaning. The marathon is a spectacle that feels like the ultimate test. But taking on the biggest, most difficult challenge in a sport isn't the only place where magic lives. You can find that same feeling in other distances, other challenges, and other ways of pushing yourself. The real work starts when you ask yourself one honest question: "Why does this goal matter to me?"

The marathon is an incredible accomplishment. But there is no hierarchy when it comes to distances and goals. It’s not a ladder. Just because you run a half doesn’t mean that you should run a marathon next. And, you can spend your whole life running and never even think of attempting a marathon.

You get to decide what fills your cup, what challenges you, and how you spend your time and energy. Only you have the power to choose what changes you.

So the next time FOMO creeps in and you feel like you’re missing out if you don’t run a marathon, remember this: You already have everything you need to change your life. You don't need 26.2 miles. You just need to show up for yourself.

Can you run a marathon? Yes. Should you? That’s entirely up to you.

Xbox CEO Congratulates Bethesda As The Elder Scrolls 6 Gets 'Official Name Reveal' Trailer

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Spoilers ahead if you don't want to know!

It's time for The Elder Scrolls 6 to get its official name! Following a report last week that outed how Bethesda was going to reveal the name in November, the company has just done that as part of its 40th anniversary celebrations.

Like we said last time, there are spoilers ahead if you don't want to know!

The Elder Scrolls 6 will officially be known as "Sentinel", and although today's trailer doesn't go into any detail whatsoever, it does mention Hammerfell by name, which is where the game was rumoured to be set.

Here's the accompanying description for the trailer:

“The scroll offered only a shimmer, a veil thinly lifted to my eyes. A future lost. A victory undone. Sands of time scattered by fate.” Yazar, Moth Priest to High Queen Iradne of Hammerfell (4E 206).

Your Orchid Might Be Dying If You Notice This Strange Growth – but It Could Also Be Wonderful News

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Beautiful plants and ideas to brighten your garden.

Expert tips for growing fruit, vegetables and herbs at home.

What Your Christmas Cactus Needs in November – Ace These 4 Tasks for Fabulous Holiday Flowers

What Your Jade Plant Needs in October – 7 Tips to Keep It Gorgeous and Glossy Through Winter

Why You Should Never Ignore a Spider Plant With Brown Leaf Tips in Fall

Sign up for the Gardening Know How newsletter today and receive a free copy of our e-book "How to Grow Delicious Tomatoes".

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Little leaves on an orchid's old flower spike may signal trouble – but a keiki doesn’t always mean the plant is struggling. Here's how to tell the difference.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

It usually gets spotted by accident, halfway through watering. The flower spike that finished months ago has a few green leaves sticking out partway up, and maybe a stubby root or two hanging under them. That's an orchid keiki. For a minute, finding one feels like getting a second orchid for nothing.

Then the doubt sets in. Read up on what a keiki is, and you'll soon come across the idea that orchids are more likely to sprout them when they're stressed or failing. Anyone whose plant has also dropped a leaf or two lately will find that hard to shake.

Stress can play a part, though it's far from the only thing. Heat, fertilizer, and genetics all factor in, and plenty of keikis grow on plants with nothing wrong at all. A few minutes at the sink with the parent plant will tell you more than the keiki does itself.

The name is Hawaiian – keiki (KAY-kee) means baby. Look closely at a moth orchid (Phalaenopsis) spike, and you'll see small bumps along it, each wrapped in a papery sheath. Those are nodes, each holding a dormant bud, and once in a while one of those buds grows leaves. What grows is a miniature copy of the parent.

Keikis aren't an orchid disease or a parasite, and they can't spread to your other plants. They're simply one way orchids reproduce without seed. Where it grows matters more. A spike keiki sits up on the flower stem. A basal keiki comes up at the bottom, squeezed in next to the lower leaves, and on a moth orchid, that second kind can point to trouble at the crown.

A stem keiki growing on a Phalaenopsis orchid stem

Sometimes a keiki does show up because the parent is in trouble. Moth orchids grow from one point only, right in the middle of the leaves. Lose that tip to rot, or a break, and no new leaves can form up there. With energy in reserve, the plant may instead push out a basal keiki below the damaged tissue. Dendrobium canes that lose their roots, or their link to the rest of the plant through rot or injury, sometimes sprout keikis as well.

Plenty of keikis turn up on orchids with nothing wrong, though. On moth orchids, they come naturally from the middle and upper nodes of the spike, and certain species and hybrids are just more prone to it. Hormones are what flip a dormant bud into a keiki. Dab cytokinin, one of those growth hormones, on a node and it can pull a keiki out of a perfectly healthy plant. Crazy Keiki Cloning Paste, available on Amazon, is a well-rated product that contains cytokinin.

Sign up for the Gardening Know How newsletter today and receive a free copy of our e-book "How to Grow Delicious Tomatoes".

Long spells of heat as a spike finishes, heavy nitrogen feeding late in the season, and too little light or humidity have also been tied to keikis.

Some longtime growers take a keiki as a sign orchid care could use adjusting – a dim corner or heavy feeding is a good place to start. On a plant with firm, flat leaves and plump roots, a spike keiki usually isn't hiding anything. A basal keiki pushing up beside a soft, dark center is the one to worry about.

A basal orchid keiki growing on plant with some shriveled old roots

Start by checking for orchid leaf problems. Healthy moth orchid leaves are thick and firm, almost leathery. An occasional yellow bottom leaf is normal – the oldest leaves dry up as the plant ages. Several leaves yellowing at once is another matter, and so are newer leaves that have gone limp and wrinkled. Leaves that shrivel like that usually aren't getting enough water. And in fall, don't be fooled by a cold windowsill – nights below 60°F (16°C) can yellow lower leaves on their own.

Next, slide the pot off and check the orchid roots. Good ones feel firm and plump. They look green right after watering and silvery once they dry. Rotted ones are brown and mushy, and if you pinch one, the outer layer may slip off, leaving nothing but a thin string.

Now peer into the middle where new leaves come from. That's the crown. It should be firm and green. If it has gone soft, black, or wet-looking, crown rot has likely set in, often from water molds such as Pythium ultimum and Phytophthora cactorum, and rot that spreads through the crown can kill the whole plant.

One yellow leaf proves very little. Slow decline is different. If the leaves feel a little softer each time you check and nothing about the care has changed, look underground – usually at the roots, or at old bark mix that's broken down into soggy muck. Good news shows up in the same places. New leaves unfolding from the crown, or fresh green root tips near the base, mean the plant is still growing.

A basal orchid keiki has been left to grow into an established offshoot on this neglected plant

Did everything look fine? Then leave the plant alone, keiki and all. If the check turned something up, fix that and only that. Cut rotted roots back to firm tissue with something small and sharp, and wipe the blades with rubbing alcohol between plants. Fiskars' micro-tip pruning snips are narrow enough to get in between roots.

For repotting, use a specialist orchid potting mix, like Better-Gro Phalaenopsis mix, available at Home Depot, which is a fir bark blend. Use a clear slotted orchid pot, like these Holanhing pots, so you can keep an eye on the roots.

After that, water orchids sparingly until new roots reach the fresh bark, and water in the morning so the leaves dry before dark. Tip out any water that pools in the crown. Moth orchids do well in bright, indirect light, just not pressed up against cold glass or sitting above a heating vent.

The keiki itself can stay put. Without roots of its own, a spike keiki draws water and nutrients through the parent's flower stem, and snipping it off does nothing for rotting roots or a damaged crown. Many growers leave a moth orchid keiki alone until it carries a couple of leaves and roots about 1–1.5 inches (2.5–4 cm) long. Smaller ones often struggle on their own. Once it reaches that size, separating and potting the keiki is a quick way to propagate your orchid.

It depends on the damage. A moth orchid that still has some plump roots and a firm crown can come back once its care is fixed, though you may wait a while for the first new leaf. Rot through the crown is a much tougher case. So is a pot where you find almost nothing but mush. Even then, a plant with enough energy stored up can sometimes send up a new plantlet from its base.

This is where a keiki helps. It grew from the parent's own tissue, so it carries the same genetics and can keep that orchid going if the parent doesn't make it. Leave it attached while the parent holds up.

What if a declining plant has a green flower spike but no keiki? Try using keiki cloning paste, mentioned above, to coax one out, though results vary.

So it’s not time to write that orchid off, just yet, because of one odd growth. Check the plant it's growing from. If the leaves are firm and the roots and crown look good, the keiki is no warning at all, just a free second orchid hitching a ride on the first. With a little TLC, you can get that orchid blooming beautifully in no time.

Tyler’s passion began with indoor gardening and deepened as he studied plant-fungi interactions in controlled settings. With a microbiology background focused on fungi, he’s spent over a decade solving tough and intricate gardening problems. After spinal injuries and brain surgery, Tyler’s approach to gardening changed. It became less about the hobby and more about recovery and adapting to physical limits. His growing success shows that disability doesn’t have to stop you from your goals.

Disney plans several thousand layoffs, asks some managers to cut costs by up to 20%

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Disney is planning to cut several thousand jobs, people familiar with the matter told CNBC.

Some managers at the entertainment giant have been asked to reduce costs by up to 20%, according to the people, who asked not to be named because they were not authorized to speak publicly.

It marks the latest round of layoffs since CEO Josh D'Amaro took the reins earlier this year. The update comes after separate plans in April to cut as many as 1,000 employees, predominantly in the marketing department, and in September to cut about 300 employees, many in human resources and technology divisions.

Bloomberg was first to report the latest round of layoffs.

Company executives warned of the possibility of more reductions in its August earnings report.

"We remain highly focused on reducing costs across the enterprise to create incremental capacity to invest for growth and are evaluating a variety of levers," D'Amaro and CFO Hugh Johnston wrote in an Aug. 5 letter to shareholders. "We are mid-stream in this work and will provide future updates on our progress."

Disney introduced plans in August for an early retirement program for some employees.

"This is one of several actions we're taking to reshape our organization, including involuntary staff reductions that have already begun in some areas and will continue into next year," Chief People Officer Sonia Coleman wrote in a letter to employees on Aug. 24.

Disney had about 231,000 employees as of the close of its fiscal year 2025, which ended in September of that year, according to the company's annual financial report.

Disney is not the only entertainment company looking to reduce costs. Netflix said in a memo last month that it would lay off roughly 6% of its workforce. Skydance, the newly combined company of Paramount and Warner Bros. Discovery, is also expected to cut staff in an effort to realize $6 billion in synergies over three years.

Disney will report its fiscal fourth-quarter earnings on Nov. 17.

The story behind Grant Wood’s American Gothic and what made it go viral

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Grant Wood’s sister Nan Wood Graham and his dentist Byron McKeeby were the models for American Gothic (1930) Photo: rarehistoricalphotos.com. Painting: © 2026 Figge Art Museum, successors to the Estate of Nan Wood Graham/Licensed by VAGA at ARS, NY

Grant Wood’s painting American Gothic (1930)—the realist depiction of an austere rural man and woman posed stiffly before a modest white house with a gothic-styled window—has seeped into the public consciousness on a par with Leonardo da Vinci’s Mona Lisa and Edvard Munch’s The Scream. “I never stopped watching the ways American Gothic has transitioned into the iconic ‘couple with the pitchfork’ over the past six decades,” says the US art historian Wanda Corn, whose new book The Couple with the Pitchfork: American Gothic from Easel to Icon delves into the cultural history of the painting.

Over the decades, Corn says she accumulated an “immense archive of reimagined couples” and her publication “sheds critical light on the cultural forces that transformed an image with humble roots into a global icon whose reach and charisma extend far beyond the world of art”, according to the publisher, Princeton University Press.

Corn, professor emerita of art history at Stanford University, outlines in detail how Wood picked his sitters: his 30-year-old sister, Nan Wood Graham, and his 62-year-old dentist, Byron McKeeby. “The two men shared a sense of humour, often chuckling over their ‘bridge for a bridge’ exchange; the dentist had provided the artist with a dental bridge and Wood paid him with a painting of a Paris bridge,” Corn writes.

Crucially the image has been misconstrued over the decades, with viewers interpreting the couple as married when in reality the pair represented a father and his daughter, according to the artist’s sister. “In Wood’s mind, the two people in front of the house represented a father and his aging daughter—a spinster—living in small-town America,” writes Corn. Wood and his closed circle described it this way repeatedly, she adds.

“Yet when the painting went out into the world, not one person saw the couple as a parent and child, nor did they see them as from a small town … this is one of the best examples in the history of art of how an artist’s intentions in a work can differ from the public’s reception of a painting.”

The person who corrected Corn about this widespread misconception was Wood Graham herself. “I too thought it represented a married couple,” Corn says. She explains that when she was photographed as the pitchfork couple with her husband in the 1970s, for a feminist work called Joe and Wanda Corn, Holiday Card (1975) by Keith M. Jones Sr, she was “under the assumption that [Wood’s sitters] were a married couple. But when I went to see [Wood Graham], I talked about them as a couple and she said in no uncertain terms: ‘They’re not a couple. It’s a father and daughter.’”

The image, and Wood’s achievements as an Iowa-based artist, were consistently lambasted and dismissed though. Early art historical texts initially ignored him, such as A History of Art (1962) by H.W. Janson, which only included the work in its seventh edition published in 2006. Corn’s survey of such texts establishes, she says, “how passive-aggressive the coverage of American Gothic was in this literature”.

Wanda Corn, the author of the new book, with her husband Joe Corn in 2024 Photo: Keith M. Jones, Jr.

The critical reaction to a Wood show Corn curated in 1983 was also brutal. “A writer of a Wall Street Journal article in May 1983 … labelled it the ‘Great Grant Wood Redemption Campaign’ and quoted the reaction of a well-known art dealer in town: ‘Flush it down the drain. It’s just cornball, provincial, nationalistic propaganda and it has nothing to do with art!’”

Corn’s 1983 exhibition of Wood’s work, Grant Wood: The Regionalist Vision, which launched at the Whitney Museum of American Art in New York, toured to other venues such as the Minneapolis Institute of Art (Yale University Press published an accompanying book). “The last landing of the exhibition was in San Francisco [at the de Young museum], and what better place to launch the first gay Gothic [work] on the walls of a major museum than in San Francisco,” she says, referring to the 1983 front cover of the gay magazine Frontiers that was included in the show.

The author unpicks the thorny issue of Wood’s sexuality, which has come under the microscope in recent years. “The upshot is that Wood today is seen as a far more complicated and tragic figure than he presented and for many years was understood to be,” she writes, acknowledging that two art historians, Tripp Evans and Sue Taylor, have contributed the most to recent psychosexual interpretations of Wood’s life and “the uncovering of his homosexuality”.

“I can see his struggles constantly,” Corn says. “But on the other hand, I also know people who found him just boundless fun to be with, lovable and endlessly creative, not dark and gloomy … we have to get a more balanced view [of him].”

How American Gothic came to be assimilated into the art history canon, across high and low popular culture is also explored through a wide variety of images including Obama Gothic (2012) by Mario Piperni, which shows Michelle and Barack Obama in front of the White House as the pitchfork couple, and Barry Kite’s photocollage Gothic Wave (2010) that draws on Hokusai’s famous 19th-century woodblock print.

“I actually could not have written the book I wrote now back then [in 1983] because I didn’t have the raw data,” Corn says. “I didn’t have this notion of the episodic uses of [the image] for everyday transient events and political events. I didn’t have any sense of what the computer was going to do to the image: the way it was going to translate it but also just make it a resource anybody could tap into.”

• Wanda Corn, The Couple with the Pitchfork: American Gothic from Easel to Icon, Princeton University Press, 312pp, $49.95/£42 (hb)

Arc Raiders devs say that using generative AI voice acting was necessary as actors "would just go crazy if they had to read all of that"

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

CCO states: "We are not in the industry of replacing actors.".

Developers at Embark Studios, the company behind Arc Raiders, have spoken on the use of generative AI voice acting in the extraction shooter. It's argued that the initial use of generative AI line reads was a benefit to the voice actors, as they: "would just go crazy if they had to read all of that".

This comes from a recently released video by Embark, focused on Arc Raider's sound and music. In it, several key members of staff speak on the game's use of generative AI, be it the initial use of it for voice lines, and the gradual replacement of it with human line reads.

Audio director Bence Pajor first spoke about the struggles he ran into at previous studios; how gameplay decisions couldn't be changed because voice lines were already locked in. He describes this as a "true pain", and that it places stress on the writing team, dialogue team, and voice actors who may have scheduling clashes. He said that using reachable voice actors in Stockholm, and using the tools available, made life easier for everyone.

Later in the video, Pajor explained that training data was made with the voice actor's performances. Due to the voice lines being required for each new feature, item, or weapon added into the game, he argues it would be too impractical to record every possible version of a line read. "The actors would just go crazy if they had to read all of that," Pajor said.

Pajor continues by pointing to Arc Raider's many updates and changes since its release in October 2025, it would be impractical to call in voice actors to record lines every time an altered line read needed to happen. Two voice actors present in the video - Jo Rideout (Shani) and Diana Gardner (Celeste) - also shared their feelings on it. Gardner said it was "freeing" that she could take a job somewhere else, without worrying about cancelling other shoots or projects.

However, Gardner would later speak on the topic of her ownership of Celeste's many lines in the game: "The contract seemed so fair [...] so it was a no-brainer in that sense. But I didn't know what to expect once I heard the voice come out. I was like, 'Can I take ownership for the performance?' [...] This is hours of work that I've put in, but can I take ownership for the final product? No."

Even with these proposed benefits to using AI-powered performances, these voice lines are being replaced. Embark CCO Stefan Strandberg said: "we are not in the industry of replacing voice actors", and acknowledges that immersion-breaking experiences many players had when playing Arc Raiders and hearing these performances. Strandberg and Pajor both admit to understanding the AI critique, and point to the work being done to replace what they can.

"We are not in the industry of replacing actors," said Strandberg. "Actually I think quite the opposite. We should celebrate the performance and give them more space. That's something I'm hoping to give to players over the coming years."

Part of this critique over generative AI's use in Arc Raiders came from reviews, including our own. Embark Studios faced similar pushback for gen-AI voice acting in The Finals, however Arc Raider's popularity while using the technology led Nexon to describe it as a "trojan horse" for gen AI's use in small team game development.

"We need to understand what the need is here" - Arc Raiders studio still undecided about the future of PvE despite a seemingly successful test

Holst completes Adjaye-designed mass-timber library in Oregon

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Photography by Christian Columbres

US architecture studio Holst has created a mass-timber library in Oregon, with a series of tall columns at the entrance and a central atrium, after taking over the project from Adjaye Associates.

The East County Library in Gresham features a trapezoidal roof structure on top of a mostly square building envelope, creating multi-storey covered entry programmes on each side.

These entry programmes are lined with massive glued-laminated (glulam) mass-timber columns that Holst said speak to a primary effect of the design: “awe and accessibility”.

“One of our major main vision points of the project was this idea of awe and accessibility,” Holst principal and creative director Brittany Shreiner told Dezeen.

Shreiner said that the accessible component comes from the dual entry programmes that open up to a plaza on one side, with space taken from a converted street, and an elevated entryway on the other that features ramps.

A rail line is located on one side and a bus line on the other, connecting the building to the wider public realm.

For the team, the building needed to be a “big civic moment”, thus the massive columned entryway. However, Shreiner said that the awe the studio aspired to create was more akin to that of a forest, and the mass timber helped them to express that.

Structurally, the building has a mix of concrete and mass timber elements, with mostly wooden floorplates and some walls, and concrete-and-steel cores.

The facade is a mix of vertical timber elements interspaced with high-performance concrete bands with scalloped shapes and vertical bands of glass.

At one point, a grey-toned concrete panelling system marks the semi-circular protrusion where the structure’s amphitheatre is.

The appearance of the building is close to the original design submitted by Adjaye Associates – Holst was the architect of record at the start.

However, after misconduct allegations against Adjaye Associates founder David Adjaye were made public in a Financial Times investigation, the studio was released from this project, among others. Adjaye denies the allegations.

Shriener said that the biggest changes in the design were the removal of screens that were supposed to overhang from the covered entryways and a tower that extended past the roof plane.

“There was still a lot of design to be done [at that time], but the bones of the building were there. The community buy-in was there. The stakeholder buy-in was there,” said Shriener.

“So, the county was definitely clear in their intention that they did not want to proceed with Adjaye as part of our team, and that they basically trusted us to bring the project forward, but they didn’t want to see a total redesign by any means.”

Having recently solidified itself as a name in the US mass timber game with a tall building in Portland, the studio focused on optimising the timber usage as it took over the design. It worked closely with local mass-timber supplier TimberLab for the materials.

One detail that stands out is the notches in the timber columns and beams that give the structure a cabin-like or residential appearance, while remaining monumental in scale.

More than just a style conceit, the notches play a role in the ceiling plane, hiding the mechanical systems and lighting.

Inside, vestibules compress the entry to the library, which then opens up into a main core that runs between the entrances, with a double-height atrium space in the middle, complete with a wood-slatted spiral staircase.

For Shriener, this middle space represents a “clearing” in the forest.

The primary book-storage spaces surround the main central area, with smaller breakout rooms and classrooms lying mostly on the perimeter.

Mass-timber elements remain visible throughout the interior spaces, with green carpets at times continuing the forest metaphor.

On the roof, a series of sawtooth lightwells bring diffuse light into the main central space, while a rooftop patio allows guests to enjoy the views of the mountains beyond.

Elsewhere in Oregon, Holst created a Gaudí-informed apartment building wrapped in wood.

Other projects that were completed after the fallout from the Adjaye allegations include the Princeton Art Museum, which was finished by the executive architect Cooper Robertson after Princeton University distanced itself from Adjaye’s studio.

Photography by Christian Columbres

One thing Italy boss Roberto Mancini did recently shows exactly what he thinks of Giovanni Leoni

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Giovanni Leoni appears to be firmly in the mind of Italy head coach Roberto Mancini, judging by one recent report from his homeland.

The Liverpool defender has been out injured for more than a year after rupturing his ACL on his debut for the Reds against Southampton in September 2025, with the 19-year-old since undergoing a lengthy road to recovery.

Despite some hopes that he’d resume team training during the most recent international window a month ago, Andoni Iraola has since indicated that such a step will hopefully be taken during the upcoming round of national team fixtures in mid-November.

Even though Leoni hasn’t played a match of any description for more than 13 months, it seems that he hasn’t been forgotten by Mancini.

According to Gazzetta dello Sport, the Italy head coach travelled to England in the hope of seeing the Liverpool defender (among several other candidates for Azzurri selection) on the pitch, but the teenager hadn’t recovered in time for the 61-year-old’s visit.

The report stated that the Reds’ number 15 ‘may return after the international break’, although that didn’t specify whether it’d be in the form of team training or clearance to finally resume playing.

Want more Empire of the Kop coverage? Add us as a preferred source on Google to your favourites list for news you can trust

Even with Leoni sidelined for so long, that Mancini reportedly wanted to see him in action on his vist to England attests to how highly the Italy boss regards the Liverpool defender.

The heartbreaking ACL injury overshadowed just how well the centre-back played on his Reds debut against Southampton before the cruellest intervention of fate, and the ex-Manchester City manager clearly has plenty of faith in the 19-year-old’s abilities.

The day that the young Italian is finally back in action will be one to cherish, although it’ll naturally take him time to get back into the rhythm of playing after being ruled out for 13 months and counting.

He could also have a difficult task on his hands trying to break up the uncompromising centre-back partnership of Virgil van Dijk and Jeremy Jacquet, with the latter particularly excelling since coming to Liverpool from Rennes in the summer.

Right now, though, simply getting back on the pitch will represent a seminal moment for Leoni, who should be given a tremendous boost by knowing that Mancini has him in his plans for the Italy squad.

Intel 14A moves closer to production as PDK 0.9 becomes available

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Intel Foundry last week made available its Intel 14A process design kit (PDK) version 0.9, an important milestone in the development of this next-generation manufacturing technology. The company says that the production node is reaching performance and yield maturity expected at this stage of development, and the results are backed by silicon validation. Intel plans to release PDK 1.0 in the first quarter of 2027, which will validate the technology's readiness for high-volume manufacturing (HVM).

A process design kit contains transistor models, design rules, and other information that enables chip designers to develop products for a particular manufacturing process using electronic design automation (EDA) tools. The accuracy and quality of the PDK are crucial for modelling how chips will perform once manufactured, and the release of PDK 0.9 is particularly important because it provides customers with a representation of Intel 14A based on actual silicon measurements rather than on theoretical models or simulations. At this stage, customers can proceed with product development with greater confidence that the technology will meet their requirements when it comes to performance and power consumption.

Intel says that PDK 0.9 lets customers develop designs intended to enter risk production around the time the process is certified, which — provided that PDK accurately reflects the characteristics of manufactured silicon — in many cases enables chip developers to greatly reduce the number of silicon revisions between initial tapeout and mass production. Of course, availability of PDK 0.9 does not indicate that Intel 14A is ready or even close to mass production, as the company expects to validate HVM readiness only with PDK 1.0, sometime in Q1 2027. Again, this does not mean that 14A will enter mass production in early 2027, as Intel itself expects to begin mass production using its 14A process technology in 2028, following risk production for its own products in the second half of 2027.

"From a customer perspective, the PDK 0.9 gives customers the confidence to design products on Intel 14A, as the technology is reaching the industry-standard performance and yield maturity expected at this stage of development, supported by silicon-backed validation," said Ben Sell, corporate vice president and general manager of front-end technology development at Intel Foundry.

Intel 14A builds on top of architectural foundations introduced with Intel 18A but incorporates several substantial enhancements. These include 2nd Generation RibbonFET 2 gate-all-around (GAA) transistors, PowerDirect backside power delivery (which delivers power right to the transistor source/drain regions without using intermediate connections like TSVs used by PowerVia), and Turbo Cells (high-performance standard cells designed to accelerate timing-critical paths in CPUs and GPUs), which provide chip designers with additional ways to increase performance and optimize power efficiency. Since Intel has previously outlined advantages of these technologies, the latest announcement does not provide updates regarding transistor performance, power consumption, or transistor density figures for Intel 14A.

Beyond improvements to transistor, power delivery, and interconnect technologies, Intel is applying manufacturing experience gained during 12 months of Intel 18A production to accelerate maturation of 14A. Specifically, the company says it has increased investments in inline defect detection, modeling, and simulation to identify potential manufacturing problems earlier in the development cycle. These initiatives are intended to improve manufacturing yields, process variability, ultimately transistor performance, and reduce production cycle times. Intel also says it has introduced several strategic initiatives to accelerate technology development, although it does not quantify their impact.

Another important change concerns Intel's relationships with equipment and materials suppliers. The company says it has revised its collaboration model to involve technology development teams and external suppliers earlier in the process development cycle. The goal of such close collaborations is to address manufacturing defects and improve equipment capabilities before they become obstacles for yield improvements after the technology enters mass production. Intel also conducts regular yield summit meetings with suppliers to accelerate progress on critical development objectives.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

"This has required evolving our collaboration model with equipment and material suppliers, and earlier engagement from our [technology development] leaders with suppliers to drive improvements in defects and equipment capabilities," Sell said.

A bonus of such early collaboration is that equipment and materials suppliers may incorporate Intel's requirements into their standard products and development roadmaps. This could give Intel an advantage over competitors, which would have to either adapt their manufacturing processes to equipment optimized around Intel's requirements or work with suppliers to develop alternative solutions from scratch.

Historically, Intel frequently set the direction for semiconductor manufacturing technologies as other chipmakers subsequently adopted similar approaches. One notable exception was immersion DUV lithography, which Intel adopted after TSMC, which led the development of immersion DUV lithography. By engaging equipment and materials suppliers earlier, Intel could regain some of its historical influence over the development of industry-standard manufacturing solutions, though the importance of this should not be overestimated.

Meanwhile, Intel continues to expand the design ecosystem for its 14A technology. Cadence and Intel have established a design-technology co-optimization (DTCO) collaboration focused on next-generation AI-driven design tools and intellectual property, whereas Keysight, Siemens, and Synopsys have announced design-tool certifications for the process. Intel is also working with other software developers and IP providers to validate design flows and reusable building blocks required by prospective foundry customers.

Intel says it is encouraged by customer feedback regarding the progress of 14A development. However, the company has not identified any customers that have committed to manufacturing products using the technology, disclosed customer tape-outs, or announced specific manufacturing agreements, which is normal for the foundry industry. For Intel Foundry, attracting external customers remains particularly important because development costs of modern leading-edge process technologies can only be recoupled when they are used to make hundreds of millions of chips, way more than Intel sells these days. Meanwhile, the commercial success of Intel Foundry with advanced manufacturing technologies depends not only on transistor performance and manufacturing yields, but also on its ability to deliver predictable development schedules.

Looking ahead, Intel intends to continue improving performance, yields, variability, and manufacturing cycle times of 14A, though for now the company does not disclose any actual goals. Intel also plans additional process enhancements and performance refinements for 14A-E and 14A-P nodes after the initial technology becomes available. However, exact schedules for these nodes are not known at the moment.

"Our next major milestone is the Intel 14A PDK 1.0, expected in the first quarter of 2027," said Ali Farhang, corporate vice president and general manager of Intel Foundry's design technology platform organization. "I am looking forward to sustaining this level of quality across future releases and continuing to earn our customers' trust by delivering on our commitments with a customer-first mindset."

New Route Attempt on Lunag Ri Falls Just Short of Summit

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Between October 23 and 27, Alexander Parfenov and Alexander Semenov of Russia opened a demanding new route on 6,895m Lunag Ri in the Rolwaling Himal on the Nepal-Tibet border. They turned back short of the summit, which some sources list as 6,907m.

The pair climbed a previously unexplored southeast-facing buttress of the Lunag Ri massif. The line would have ended on the southeast summit. It involved mixed and rock climbing to M6, aid sections of A2–A3, ice up to WI5–WI6 in places, and long stretches of steep snow with limited protection.

They placed a piton on the final part of the pillar and began rappelling from there, reaching the base of the wall on October 28, according to gore-ljudje.net. The duo turned back short of the summit due to a coming storm and health issues. The complex summit area — multiple closely linked peaks and ridge features — also left some uncertainty about how close they came to the main summit.

Even without summiting, this was a significant exploration of a major, unclimbed feature on a remote peak.

The new line opened by the Parfenov and Semenov. Photo: Alexander Parfenov

Lunag Ri, the highest peak in the Lunag massif, has long stood as one of Nepal’s highest unclimbed summits. Early attention focused on the broader massif after Joe Puryear and David Gottlieb made the first ascent of the neighboring 6,778m Jobo Rinjang via its South Face in 2009.

In the same year, a Swiss-led team under Stephane Schaffter attempted lines in the area, including approaches to Lunag I, though their claims about summits on the massif were later met with skepticism in the American Alpine Journal.

In the autumn of 2010, a French team that included Max Belleville, Mathieu Detrie, Mathieu Maynadier, and Sebastien Ratel climbed the southeast face of Lunag Ri. Their route, Close the Door (1,200 m, IV/5 F5), was completed in a three-day alpine-style push with two bivouacs. They reached a distinct southeast top (6,812m to 6,830m) on the long ridge connecting Lunag I to Jobo Rinjang. By this time, they were exhausted and did not continue the short remaining traverse to the higher main summit. This remains the only major completed route on the Lunag I massif before the first ascent of the true summit.

From the recent climb on Lunag Ri. Photo: Alexander Parfenov

Attention then shifted to the west ridge and southwest face of the main peak. In November 2015, Conrad Anker and David Lama’s alpine-style attempt reached a high point roughly 300m below the summit before retreating due to the unexpected complexity of the ridge.

They returned in 2016 to try a similar line. On the first day, at about 5,800m, Anker suffered a heart attack and was evacuated by helicopter. Lama subsequently continued alone on a variation that moved farther right onto an icefield. He established two bivouacs and pushed 50m higher than the previous year’s high point before turning around 250m shy of the summit.

In late October 2018, Lama returned solo. Over three days (October 23-25), with two bivouacs, he climbed the southwest face and northwest/west ridge (1,500m of vertical gain, terrain to 90°). He moved mostly ropeless, with limited self-belaying on the harder mixed sections. Lama negotiated the previously unclimbed headwall and made the final exposed traverse onto the main summit.

This was the first ascent of Lunag Ri’s highest point. He descended largely by rappelling down the line of ascent. Lama was awarded a Piolet d’Or for this solo first ascent of Lunag Ri.

From the recent climb on Lunag Ri. Photo: Alexander Parfenov

KrisAnnapurna is a writer with ExplorersWeb.

Kris has been writing about history and tales in alpinism, news, mountaineering, and news updates in the Himalaya, Karakoram, etc., for with ExplorersWeb since 2021. Prior to that, Kris worked as a real estate agent, interpreter, and translator in criminal law. Now based in Madrid, Spain, she was born and raised in Hungary.

The Elder Scrolls 6: Sentinel is confirmed, finally getting new trailer and full name, 8 years, 4 months, and 23 days after its reveal

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Today, Xbox and Bethesda have finally revealed the name and more information about The Elder Scrolls 6. The game, which we now know carries the subtitle Sentinel, was revealed in a special video that took place today, 3rd November 2026, almost exactly 15 years since the launch of The Elder Scrolls 5: Skyrim and eight years, four months, and 23 days since the first reveal of the title card for The Elder Scrolls 6. That's over 100 months, by the way, or 3067 days, if you want to get specific.

In the trailer, a glimpse of the new game - mostly with some fancy sand physics - before the name, Sentinel, is revealed formally. "The scroll offered only a shimmer, a veil thinly lifted to my eyes. A future lost. A victory undone. Sands of time scattered by fate," says a quote in the video, attributed to Yazar, Moth Priest of High Queen Iradne of Hammerfell.

"For 40 years, we've explored new worlds, told unforgettable stories, and gone on incredible adventures together," says a blurb for new video. "From everyone at Bethesda Game Studios, thank you for joining us along this journey, for making these worlds your own, and for inspiring us to keep creating. Here's to 40 more."

This reveal comes after a report last week suggested we'd be seeing more of the highly-anticipated fantasy RPG in November, and which also predicted the name of the game. That report suggested that the title will take place in the Tamriel region of Hammerfell, will touch on the "history of the Elder Scrolls" and will "help uncover the secrets of these mysterious ancient artifacts". This trailer seems to confirm all that.

Asha Sharma, CEO of Xbox, said she'd played the game in August on a trip to the Bethesda offices, as a giant inflatable rat and a union protest took place outside. Notably, the reveal of the game is taking place not six months after Bethesda suffered sweeping job cuts (alongside many other Microsoft-owned studios) as part of Xbox's "reset".

So why has it taken eight years for Bethesda to show us this game? Previously, Skyrim's lead designer Bruce Nesmith noted that the studio and boss Todd Howard felt pressured to reveal the game to the world because "the pitchforks and torches were out". "[...] Todd's opinion - one which I share, by the way - is that the video game industry has short memories," he said in an interview in 2023. "Those companies that start touting their games years ahead of time, actually, you know, they screw themselves. The best time to start talking about it is six months before release." It's worth noting, then, that this is what Bethesda did with Fallout 4.

Does that hint at a timeline for more Sentinel footage being shown soon, and a potentially sooner-than-we-thought release date? Who knows. If we are going to see the game again anytime soon, it's most likely going to be during The Game Awards 2026, which is taking place on 10th December.

Carbon dioxide-spilling converted turbines have been installed in a Mississippi xAI data center despite the known health risks.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Elon Musk's xAI is using eight converted jet engine turbines to power a Mississippi data center. While the generators have the required state-level permission and permits, there remain concerns about the impact they could have on the local environment. The permits allow for a 25% higher limit in pollutants known as volatile organic compounds, but those compounds, paired with the expulsion of carbon monoxide, have environmentalists warning that the use of jet engines to power data centers is a step in the wrong direction. The New York Times reports that the Southern Environmental Law Center has already challenged Mississippi's decision to approve the turbines for use at xAI's data center.

The group argues that xAI has failed to provide guarantees that the jet-powered turbines meet emissions standards for specific pollutants, including formaldehyde. It also accuses xAI of what the report terms the "undercounting" of projected emissions in relation to the data center's power sources. This comes after the same group sued xAI over its use of gas generators without the required permits in an area with more than 100,000 inhabitants.

The generators used by xAI are converted by ProEnergy, a Missouri-based outfit that repurposes the unused General Electric turbines that are typically found attached to Boeing airplanes. The company was quick to turn its attention to the possibility of using the jet engines to power data centers after it was identified that the demand for energy was outpacing the ability of traditional utility companies to keep up. Each turbine is capable of producing up to 50 megawatts of electricity. Normally, that would be enough to keep around 15,000 homes up and running.

The availability of energy has long been an issue for data centers, both existing and those still on the drawing board. A July 2026 report predicted that data centers would use 20% of all U.S. power by 2035, a figure that was a huge 83% increase over the same forecast that was made seven months prior. Despite that news, as of April this year, half of data center buildouts had been canceled or delayed due to, among other things, a lack of power. This is an issue for xAI especially, with Musk saying in August that xAI would increase its data center capacity by 7x before the end of 2027. Delays and cancelations have the potential to derail those plans.

Public sentiment around data centers has turned sour in recent months amid power issues and concerns over how much water these facilities use. While using converted jet engines could potentially help with one issue, it seems unlikely that the pollution they generate will help assuage the concerns of locals anytime soon.

Will an ability to hold federal officers accountable survive this term?

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Type to search articles, cases, and authors.

At a time when there is a desire to hold ICE agents accountable for their constitutional violations, the Supreme Court might be poised to eliminate the only way to hold them civilly liable under federal law.

By way of background, if state or local officers violate the Constitution, they can be sued under a federal statute, 42 U.S.C. Section 1983. But no federal law authorizes suits against federal officers for constitutional violations. Rather, the ability to sue a federal government officer for money damages comes from a 1971 Supreme Court decision, Bivens v. Six Unknown Named Agents of the Federal Bureau of Narcotics. There, the court said that it would allow a cause of action for money damages under the Fourth Amendment even without a federal statute authorizing it.

Nevertheless, for decades, the Supreme Court has refused to extend or even apply Bivens to other violations of constitutional rights by federal officers, and several conservative justices have called for it to be overruled. Now, in Nielsen v. Watanabe, to be argued on Nov. 9, the court will again confront whether to allow a Bivens suit – and perhaps whether to end such claims entirely.

The underlying issue is about separation of powers: should the court provide a remedy for constitutional violations or is that entirely the province of Congress? But it also is about accountability: without Bivens, how can those who violate constitutional rights, including ICE agents, be held responsible and how can those injured be compensated?

Webster Bivens alleged that he had been subjected to an illegal and humiliating search by agents of the Federal Bureau of Narcotics. Bivens sought money damages as compensation for the harms he suffered. The court held that a federal law cause of action for money damages could be inferred directly from the Fourth Amendment. The majority, in an opinion by Justice William Brennan, emphasized that individuals whose rights have been violated should not be relegated to state law remedies, which might be inadequate or hostile to the federal constitutional interest.

In an important and often-cited concurring opinion, Justice John Marshall Harlan explained that the federal courts long have devised remedies for violations of federal law. Harlan said that it is essential that federal courts be able to provide such relief because “it is apparent that some form of damages is the only possible remedy for someone in Bivens’ alleged position. … For people in Bivens’ shoes, it is damages or nothing.” Harlan rejected the claim that the court violates separation of powers when it creates such a cause of action and remedy. In his view, the court is responsible for upholding and enforcing the Bill of Rights, and, hence, the judiciary need not wait for the legislature in order to act.

Chief Justice Warren Burger and Justices Hugo Black and Harry Blackmun dissented. Each wrote an opinion arguing that separation of powers means that Congress, and not the court, should create a cause of action for damages against federal officers. Black explained that “[s]hould the time come when Congress desires such lawsuits, it has before it a model of valid legislation, 42 U.S.C. §1983, to create a damage remedy against federal officers.” Similarly, Blackmun argued that “it is the Congress, and not this Court, that should act.”

For a decade after Bivens, the court applied and extended it. In 1979’s Davis v. Passman, the court allowed a Bivens suit under the due process clause of the Fifth Amendment for an equal protection claim. Congressman Otto Passman fired his administrative assistant, Shirley Davis, because he wanted a male to fill the position. In allowing the suit against the congressman, the court emphasized the judiciary's role in ensuring effective protection of constitutional rights.

And only a year later, in Carlson v. Green, the court allowed a Bivens suit for violation of the Eighth Amendment. A mother sued federal prison officials on behalf of her deceased son, claiming that he was the victim of gross inadequacies of medical facilities and staff, which caused his death and constituted cruel and unusual punishment. A remedy was available under the Federal Tort Claims Act; thus, the issue before the court was whether a Bivens suit should be allowed in light of this alternative. The court concluded that Bivens suits were a “counterpart” to the Federal Tort Claims Act because the act creates liability for the federal government and a Bivens cause of action permits recovery from federal officers themselves. The court found no indication that Congress intended for the act to preclude Bivens suits.

Since 1980, however, the court has not ruled in favor of a Bivens claim. In its most recent decision about Bivens, 2022’s Egbert v. Boule, the court noted that it had “declined 11 times to imply a similar cause of action for other alleged constitutional violations” and that “in all but the most unusual circumstances, prescribing a cause of action is a job for Congress, not the courts.”

Specifically, the court has held that that such claims are not allowed if there is a statute that precludes suits (in 2010’s Hui v. Castaneda), or that provides an alternative remedy even if it is inadequate (in 1988’s Schweiker v. Chilicky). It has precluded Bivens claims for those injured while in the military, even for egregious violations of their rights (in 1987’s United States v. Stanley). It has ruled that Bivens suits should be allowed only if they are on balance desirable and consistently has found that they are not (in 2007’s Wilkie v. Robbins, 2017’s Ziglar v. Abbasi, 2020’s Hernandez v. Mesa, and Boule). And it has precluded Bivens suits against private prisons that contract with the federal government (in 2001’s Correctional Services Corp. v. Malesko) and the guards employed by them (in 2012’s Minecci v. Pollard).

Based on this line of cases, the court in Egbert v. Boule stressed that recognizing a Bivens cause of action “is ‘a disfavored judicial activity.’” The court stated that

The court then laid out the framework for Bivens claims going forward. According to the majority, this should be guided by a two-step inquiry:

This is a test that puts a very strong presumption against recognizing Bivens claims.

Like Carlson v. Green, Nielsen v. Watanabe involves an Eighth Amendment claim for cruel and unusual punishment.The case involves a prisoner, Kekai Watanabe, who alleges that he suffered a broken tailbone when attacked by other prisoners that caused him excruciating pain and that a staff nurse, Francis Nielsen, denied him medical care. The court granted review as to whether the U.S. Court of Appeals for the 9th Circuit “erred [here] in recognizing a Bivens cause of action.”

Watanabe argues to the court that this case is governed by Carlson v. Green and does not seek to extend Bivens in any way. His brief states: “Watanabe asserts the same kind of violation of the same constitutional right by the same type of defendant in the same institutional setting pursuant to the same substantive standards as the plaintiff in Carlson. This is precisely the kind of claim this Court has long understood Carlson to encompass.”

In response, Nielsen argues that Bivens suits are strongly disfavored and that there are “significant factual differences such as the immediacy and severity of the harm alleged” compared with Carlson. But that is not all: Nielsen also expressly argues that “Bivens should be overruled” and that “Congress, not the judiciary, creates causes of action.”

The court could affirm the U.S. Court of Appeals for the 9th Circuit and allow a Bivens suit, saying that this is governed by Carlson. Or the court could distinguish Carlson, accepting Nielsen’s argument that there are sufficient factual differences between the cases, essentially limiting Bivens claims only for cruel and unusual punishment claims involving deaths. Or the court could overrule Carlson but not overrule Bivens.

Or the court could overrule Bivens entirely, which several conservative justices have advocated in the past.

The fundamental disagreement, as it was in Bivens, is whether it is the judicial role to provide remedies for violations of constitutional rights or whether that is just for Congress. The practical reality is that without Bivens suits, those whose rights have been violated – even horribly – are left with no recourse. In Hernandez v. Mesa, for example, a boy in Mexico was killed by shot fired across the border by a federal agent in Texas. The court’s rejection of a Bivens suit left no remedy at all.

In that case, and in many other situations, the Federal Tort Claims Act does not allow suits against the United States. Often there are not state tort remedies available either. To paraphrase Justice Harlan, for people in the shoes of many injured by federal officers, it is Bivens suits or nothing.

I would like to think that the reports of egregious constitutional violations by ICE agents, including killings, would cause the court to realize the importance of Bivens suits and to not end such causes of action. That is what makes Nielsen v. Watanabe so important.

Erwin Chemerinsky is the Dean and Jesse H. Choper Distinguished Professor of Law, University of California, Berkeley School of Law.

Virgin Atlantic → Delta One Suites

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →
