The best workaround for Delta dynamic pricing on premium cabins

Picture this: you're settling into a private suite at 35,000 feet somewhere over the American heartland. The door clicks shut. There's a full-flat bed, a sliding privacy door, a personal minibar, and a cabin crew member who just handed you a glass of something sparkling before you've even thought to ask. This is Delta One Suites on the Airbus A350-900 — one of the best domestic business class products flying today — and you're on your way from New York to Los Angeles for 50,000 Virgin Atlantic Flying Club miles. Let that sink in for a moment. The same seat on the same flight regularly retails for **$2,500 to $6,000 in cash**. At the low end of that range, your 50,000 points are delivering **5 cents per point (CPP)**. At the high end — during peak travel seasons or last-minute premium bookings — you're looking at **12 cents per point**. For context, most travel rewards programs consider 1–2 CPP a "good" redemption. You're potentially doing six times better than that. The ChooseFI community is built on a foundational principle: the gap between what something *costs* and what you *pay* is where wealth is built. That principle applies to points redemptions just as powerfully as it applies to index fund expense ratios. When the market (Delta's dynamic pricing engine) says $4,000 and your award chart says 50,000 points, you've just found a pricing inefficiency — and exploiting it intelligently is exactly what savvy travel rewards strategy is all about. What makes this redemption especially compelling is the *product*. Delta One Suites on the A350-900 isn't a legacy business class seat in name only. The sliding door gives you genuine privacy. The fully flat bed means you arrive in LA rested instead of stiff. The suite design is genuinely luxurious — the kind of experience that, for most people, lives permanently in the "someday, maybe" category. Travel rewards exist to move that experience from "someday" to your actual calendar. For a couple traveling together, this redemption scales elegantly: 100,000 points gets two people into private suites on one of America's most competitive premium routes. That's a combined retail value potentially north of $10,000 — for points you may have earned simply by paying your regular monthly expenses on the right credit cards. This is what the system looks like when it works.

Here's the insight that changes everything: Delta prices its own award seats dynamically, which means when you search Delta.com for a SkyMiles redemption, you're at the mercy of an algorithm that reads demand, load factors, and booking timing. A Delta One Suite that costs 50,000 SkyMiles one week might jump to 120,000 or even 250,000 SkyMiles the next. It's unpredictable, often punishing, and fundamentally unfair to the loyal customer who saved up miles expecting consistency.

Virgin Atlantic Flying Club doesn't play by those rules.

Virgin Atlantic is a Delta SkyTeam partner, which means Flying Club members can book seats on Delta-operated flights using their Virgin Atlantic miles. But critically — and this is the entire mechanism — Virgin Atlantic prices Delta award seats on a fixed award chart, not Delta's dynamic pricing system. That chart currently prices Delta One (business class) on transcon routes like JFK–LAX at 50,000 miles one-way. No matter what Delta's algorithm is doing that day, your Virgin Atlantic redemption rate stays fixed.

This is a classic pricing inefficiency created by partner award charts lagging behind airline pricing strategies. Delta has every incentive to raise its own SkyMiles pricing — it captures more revenue from its own loyalty currency. But Delta has far less control over what a partner program charges to access its seats. Virgin Atlantic's award chart is, in effect, a door into Delta inventory that bypasses Delta's own toll booth.

The points you need to fund this redemption are almost certainly already sitting in currencies you have or can easily earn:

For a redemption this valuable, the 1:1 instant transfers from Chase, Amex, Citi, and Bilt are your primary path. You transfer exactly what you need, the miles appear in your Flying Club account immediately, and you book. No waiting, no uncertainty.

Virgin Atlantic has a reputation for imposing high fuel surcharges on its own operated flights — and that reputation is well-earned. But here's the critical nuance: surcharges do NOT apply to partner awards. When you book a Delta-operated flight through Virgin Atlantic Flying Club, you pay only the standard carrier-imposed taxes and fees, which are minimal on domestic U.S. routes. On JFK–LAX, expect to pay roughly $5–$15 in taxes. That's it. The surcharge concern that makes some people avoid Virgin Atlantic entirely simply doesn't apply here.

Flying Club miles expire after 36 months of account inactivity. Keep your account active with at least one earning or redemption activity every three years, and your miles will remain intact indefinitely. This is a very traveler-friendly policy compared to programs with hard expiration dates.

Free calculator shows when you could reach financial independence — plus a savings rate tracker, expense audit, and more.

Do NOT start at Virgin Atlantic's website — their search tool for partner awards is limited. Instead, go directly to delta.com and search for your desired route (e.g., JFK → LAX) in the date range you're targeting. Select "Use miles" as your payment method and filter by Business class. You're not booking here — you're prospecting.

What you're looking for: Delta One availability showing in the results. When you see it, note the exact flight number, date, and departure time. Delta.com will show you the SkyMiles price (which will be high under dynamic pricing), but ignore that number entirely. You just need to confirm the seat exists.

You need 50,000 Virgin Atlantic Flying Club miles per person. Before transferring anything, confirm which flexible points currency you're working with:

All transfer at 1:1 instantly. If you're using Marriott Bonvoy points, the ratio is 3:1 (so 150,000 Bonvoy points = 50,000 Flying Club miles), and transfers take 1–5 days.

Once you've confirmed availability on a specific flight, transfer your points. Use the transfer portal within your credit card program's website (Chase, Amex, Citi, or Bilt). You'll need your Flying Club membership number — create a free account at virginatlantic.com/us/en/flying-club if you don't have one.

For Chase, Amex, Citi, and Bilt transfers: miles appear instantly in your Flying Club account. Confirm the balance before proceeding.

Critical warning: Do not transfer points until you've verbally confirmed availability with a Virgin Atlantic agent (Step 4). Points transfers are irreversible.

This is a phone booking, not an online booking. Call Virgin Atlantic Flying Club at 1-800-365-9500. Have your flight details ready:

Ask the agent to confirm that Delta One award availability exists on your chosen flight before you transfer points. If they confirm it, then transfer and call back — or ask to be placed on a brief hold while you complete the transfer (instant transfers make this possible).

Once your miles are transferred and the agent has confirmed availability, they'll complete the booking. You'll pay only standard carrier taxes and fees — expect roughly $5–$15 for a domestic U.S. route. You'll receive a Virgin Atlantic booking confirmation, but your ticket will show Delta as the operating carrier.

Virgin Atlantic can book Delta awards up to 331 days in advance. For the best seat selection and availability, aim to search within that window as early as possible.

Delta SkyMiles uses fully dynamic pricing with no published award chart, meaning business class awards can range from 80,000 to 500,000 SkyMiles for the same route depending on demand. Virgin Atlantic, as a Delta joint venture partner, maintains a fixed award chart for Delta-operated flights — creating a massive pricing arbitrage. A JFK-to-London Delta One Suite that Delta prices at 350,000 SkyMiles can often be booked through Virgin for 47,500 Flying Club miles. Both programs see the same award inventory, but Virgin prices it using their distance-based chart instead of Delta's demand-based algorithm. Since Virgin Atlantic's Flying Club accepts 1:1 transfers from Chase, Amex, Capital One, Citi, and Bilt, you can earn these miles through credit card spending without ever flying Virgin Atlantic.

Let's walk through a real example so the numbers become concrete.

The Route: New York JFK → Los Angeles LAX The Aircraft: Airbus A350-900 The Cabin: Delta One Suites

Pull up Delta.com on a typical travel date and you'll find Delta One Suites on this route priced anywhere from $2,500 for a off-peak weekday to $5,000–$6,000 during peak summer weekends or holiday travel. Let's use a realistic midpoint of $3,500 — a completely standard price for business class on one of America's most competitive routes.

If you earned those 50,000 points via a Chase Sapphire Reserve welcome bonus (currently 60,000 points) and some organic spend, your real cost to acquire the points was a small fraction of the flight's retail value.

You check in at JFK's Terminal 4 with Delta status access or a Priority Pass card in hand, clearing into the Delta Sky Club or the premium Centurion Lounge (if you carry the Amex Platinum). Then you board the A350-900 and find your suite.

Delta One Suites on this aircraft feature a sliding privacy door — a genuine door, not just a partition — along with a fully flat bed (78 inches), direct aisle access from every seat, a personal storage console, a large in-flight entertainment screen, and dedicated overhead lighting. The meal service is genuinely elevated for a domestic route: pre-departure drinks, a multi-course meal, and a soft-serve ice cream sundae that has become a kind of cult favorite among frequent flyers.

The five-hour flight from New York to Los Angeles becomes an experience rather than a commute. You arrive rested, full, and having spent roughly $11 out of pocket on a seat your fellow passengers paid $3,500 for. That gap — that's the ChooseFI travel rewards mindset in action.

Virgin Atlantic can book Delta awards up to 331 days in advance. Delta One availability on high-demand routes like JFK–LAX is not unlimited — it's released in waves and often fills quickly for peak dates. Set a calendar reminder to search exactly 331 days before your target travel date. This is especially important for holiday travel, summer weekends, and any dates where you have a hard deadline.

Since transfers from Chase, Amex, Citi, and Bilt are instant, you can do a same-call transfer — confirm availability with the agent, ask them to hold for a moment, transfer your points in a separate browser tab, and confirm with the agent that the miles have landed. This eliminates the risk of availability disappearing between your transfer and your booking attempt.

While this sweet spot is available year-round, Delta One availability is generally better on Tuesday, Wednesday, and Thursday departures when leisure demand is lower. If your travel dates are flexible, searching across a week of dates will often surface better availability on midweek flights.

Virgin Atlantic's award chart has high fuel surcharges on Virgin Atlantic-operated flights. This guide is specifically about Delta-operated flights. Never confuse the two. When you call to book, confirm the operating carrier is Delta. The surcharge-free benefit applies only to partner awards.

Points transfers are irreversible. A common and painful mistake is transferring points to Flying Club only to discover the specific flight you wanted has no award availability. Always confirm availability verbally with a Flying Club agent before initiating any transfer.

The most efficient path to 50,000 points is a single credit card welcome bonus. The Chase Sapphire Preferred or Chase Sapphire Reserve both offer welcome bonuses that can fully fund this redemption — and both transfer to Virgin Atlantic at 1:1 instantly. Alternatively, the Amex Gold Card or Platinum Card welcome bonuses work equally well via Amex's instant transfer path.

Flying Club miles expire after 36 months of account inactivity. A simple fix: make a small earning transaction (even a hotel stay or car rental through their partners) at least once every three years.

This redemption is ideal for couples and solo travelers who want a genuinely luxurious experience on one of America's premier transcontinental routes and are comfortable with a moderate amount of booking complexity — primarily a phone call to Virgin Atlantic.

Minimum points needed: 50,000 Virgin Atlantic Flying Club miles per person (100,000 for two). These are most efficiently sourced via a single credit card welcome bonus.

For the FI-minded traveler who has earned a moderate points balance and wants to experience premium cabin travel on a meaningful domestic route, this is a standout use of currency. The 5–12 CPP range makes it one of the most efficient domestic business class redemptions available.

This redemption is rated **moderate** — not because any individual step is technically complex, but because it requires coordinating two separate systems (Delta.com for availability searching and Virgin Atlantic by phone for booking) and making an irreversible transfer decision under some time pressure. **What makes it moderately challenging:** 1. **Availability is the main hurdle.** Delta One award inventory released to partners is limited, particularly on peak dates. You won't always find seats when you want them, and the 331-day booking window means planning further ahead than most travelers are accustomed to. 2. **The booking requires a phone call.** There's no self-service online booking for Virgin Atlantic partner awards. Some travelers find this intimidating; in practice, Flying Club agents are helpful and accustomed to partner award bookings. 3. **The transfer-before-booking sequence requires discipline.** The rule is: confirm first, transfer second. Getting that order wrong is an expensive mistake. **Three tips to make this easier:** - **Use Delta.com as your search engine.** It shows real-time award availability far more reliably than third-party tools for Delta-operated flights. - **Call Virgin Atlantic during off-peak hours** (early morning Eastern time tends to mean shorter hold times and more patient agents). - **Have your flight details written down** before you call — flight number, date, origin, destination, and Flying Club number. A prepared caller gets a faster, smoother booking. If you've ever transferred points to an airline program and called to book an award, this process will feel completely familiar. If this is your first partner award booking, treat it as a learning experience — the 5–12 CPP reward on the other side is well worth one phone call.

Log into your Delta SkyMiles account and search for one-way business class (Delta One) award flights. Filter for nonstop flights on the A350-900, which is the aircraft with Delta One Suites. Note the dates that show availability — this is the same inventory Virgin can book.

If you do not already have a Virgin Atlantic Flying Club account, create one at virginatlantic.com. You need an active account to receive transferred miles and to reference when calling to book.

Transfer the required miles from your preferred partner: Chase Ultimate Rewards (instant transfer), Amex Membership Rewards (typically 1-2 business days), Capital One (1-2 days), Citi ThankYou (instant to 24 hours), or Bilt (1-2 days). Always confirm availability before transferring.

Call Virgin Atlantic Flying Club at their US number. Provide the specific flight number, date, and route you found on delta.com. The agent will search for the same availability and quote the mileage cost from Virgin's fixed award chart. Confirm the booking and pay taxes and fees.

After booking, manage your reservation on delta.com using your confirmation number. Select your preferred Delta One Suite — window suites offer the most privacy, while center suites are available for couples who want adjacent seats.

Virgin Atlantic's fixed award chart prices Delta One flights based on distance. Transatlantic routes (US to Europe) typically cost 47,500 to 60,000 Virgin miles one-way in business class. Transpacific routes cost more, around 60,000 to 80,000 miles. Compare this to Delta SkyMiles, which can charge 200,000 to 500,000 miles for the same seats.

No. As of 2026, Virgin Atlantic does not support online booking for Delta partner awards. You must call Virgin Atlantic's Flying Club phone line to book. Search for availability on delta.com first, then call with the specific flight details. The call typically takes 10-15 minutes.

Delta One is the business class cabin available on multiple aircraft types with lie-flat seats but no closing door. Delta One Suites are only on the A350-900 and feature a fully enclosed suite with a closing door, a wider seat, and a more private experience. When booking, verify the aircraft type is A350-900 to ensure you get the Suite product.

Chase Ultimate Rewards transfers to Virgin Atlantic are instant. Citi ThankYou points are also fast, typically completing within minutes to 24 hours. Amex Membership Rewards and Bilt usually take 1-2 business days. Capital One transfers take 1-2 days as well. If you find availability and need to book quickly, Chase is the best source.

Booking Delta One Suites through Virgin Atlantic Flying Club is the definitive workaround for Delta's punishing dynamic pricing. Paying 50,000 transferable points for a seat that Delta prices at 350,000 SkyMiles is one of the largest arbitrage opportunities in travel rewards. The fully enclosed suite with a closing door is the best international business class product any US carrier offers. Yes, you need to call to book — but that 15-minute phone call saves you 300,000 miles. Build your transferable points balances with Chase, Amex, or Bilt, search delta.com for A350 availability, and let Virgin Atlantic's fixed award chart do the rest.

The Trade Desk Brings Back Dave Pickles Amid Growing Pains

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Three years after he stepped away, Dave Pickles is coming back to The Trade Desk amid the worst stretch in its history as a public company.

The co-founder and former chief technology officer, who left his role and the company’s board in 2023, will establish and lead a new CTO Council, The Trade Desk announced today. He has stayed on as advisor since leaving.

The council will work with The Trade Desk’s engineering and product teams on its core technology and product roadmap. The company, in a blog post, said that work includes “architectural direction, operational excellence, and key engineering investments—including agentic capabilities and advanced AI-powered advertising workflows.”

“I’m so excited Dave is back. I’ve missed working with him,” CEO Jeff Green said in a statement. “We’ve had so many successes together over the years, and I’m energized to take all of that history and point it toward the future. This is a pivotal crossroads for our company and the industry.”

The announcement lands a week before The Trade Desk is expected to report third-quarter earnings on Nov. 9.

The Trade Desk went public in 2016, hitting $1.1 billion market capitalization on its first day, betting the open web would hold against the walled gardens. Pickles ran the company’s technology through most of the climb that followed.

The company he is rejoining has slowing revenue growth, partners pushing back on its products, and a stock sitting 91% below its peak.

In August, TTD reported second-quarter revenue of $715 million, up 3% year-over-year but about $37.6 million short of analysts’ forecast. It guided third-quarter revenue to at least $650 million, roughly 12% below the same quarter a year earlier, which would be its first YoY revenue decline as a public company.

Pickles framed his return around the company’s existing team. “I’m thrilled by all the progress our engineering and product teams have made in recent years,” Pickles said in a statement. “The foundation here is strong, and I’m excited to support the team and help guide them through a new era of rapid change and opportunity.”

Green and Pickles founded The Trade Desk in 2009. Pickles came from AdECN, an ad exchange Microsoft acquired, where he led a team that built its real-time building exchange.

When he stepped down in 2023, he handed day-to-day technical leadership to Sairaj Uddin, then SVP of engineering, and Gruia Pitigoi-Aron, then SVP of product, both reporting to Green.

The Trade Desk’s technology has been at the center of its troubles since then. The adtech company launched the Kokai platform in 2023, around the time Pickles left. ADWEEK reported that advertisers frustrated with Kokai moved millions of dollars in ad spend away from the platform to Amazon, and the company later sunsetted Kokai.

The Trade Desk has also seen turnover in its finance leadership, including a string of CFO departures. More recently it pushed into AI agents with Koa, its conversational AI interface.

The company did not say whether Pickles is returning full time, rejoining the board or holding an officer title, or how the CTO Council will divide authority with the company’s current engineering and product leaders. The Trade Desk declined to comment on those details and pointed to its blog post announcing the move.

Trishla is an Adweek staff reporter covering AI and tech.

‘It affected my family’ – VAR official reveals full impact of huge decision which cost Liverpool

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

The official who was on VAR duty for the wrongly disallowed Luis Diaz goal in Liverpool’s 2-1 defeat to Tottenham in 2023 has spoken about the impact that the controversy had on his family.

The Colombian thought he had put the Reds 1-0 up in the 35th minute of the game in north London, but he was incorrectly flagged for offside, and a subsequent review from Stockley Park saw that erroneous decision upheld.

When the audio from the VAR conversation was publicly released a few days later, it was revealed that Darren England – the official on duty – had given the message of ‘check complete’ to the on-field referee in the mistaken belief that the goal was allowed to stand.

The 40-year-old has given an interview to BBC Sport in which he reflected on the regret from that hugely controversial decision, and how the fallout seeped into his family life.

England recalled: “Once I realised I’d made the error, it’s a really difficult place to try to forget that and move on. I can’t just physically stand up and walk out and leave it alone. A lot of resilience was needed – a lot of experience as well.”

He added: “The aftermath obviously was really difficult to deal with. It was challenging because it’s the goldfish bowl of the Premier League, but when mistakes happen, then you’re in the public eye and criticism’s inevitably going to come your way.

“That was difficult because it affected my family as well. We had people outside my girls’ school, journalists and things like that. That then affects you not as a referee, but as a human being.”

Want more Empire of the Kop coverage? Add us as a preferred source on Google to your favourites list for news you can trust

The catalogue of errors which led to Diaz’s legitimate goal being disallowed was farcical, with all of the officials on duty collectively culpable for the shambolic process which unfolded.

England did acknowledge in the VAR audio that he thought the on-field decision was onside and immediately held his hands up to the error, but that came as no comfort to anyone associated with Liverpool after such a costly and preventable outcome.

🚨 OFFICIAL: PGMOL have released VAR audio from Spurs v Liverpool 🔊 pic.twitter.com/zNLDyHkBbe

— Football on TNT Sports (@footballontnt) October 3, 2023

Criticism of the officials botching the process was inevitable and justified, but obviously the media intrusion into the referee’s family life crossed an unacceptable line, and we can empathise with how difficult the fallout must’ve been for the now 40-year-old.

Unfortunately, more than three years on from that chaotic blunder, refereeing standards in the Premier League haven’t improved to any great extent, as we saw at the weekend with the inexplicable decision not to award a penalty for a clear pull by Jurrien Timber on Alexander Isak’s shirt.

Pro Ref chief executive Howard Webb needs to do much more to ensure that a satisfactory standard of officiating is reached, but no person deserves to have an error in the course of the work turn into an unwarranted invasion on their family life.

How Alain Delon Got Caught Up in a Murder Investigation at the Height of His Fame

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

The actor lies still. Eyes closed. Waiting for the signal.

‘Silence sur le plateau . . .!’ shouts director Jacques Deray.

The crew falls quiet. A silence descends upon the Saint-Tropez villa. Just the clicking of the cicadas, drunk on the late-summer heat in the scrubland surrounding the azure pool. Thick cables everywhere. Lighting rigs. Flight cases.

‘. . . Moteur . . . Ça tourne . . .’ calls the assistant director.

The camera motors fire up, a soft clicking as the film turning on the reels gets up to speed. The entire crew poised, ready . . .

The camera pans slowly across the glimmering, phosphorescent surface of the swimming pool until, languidly, it finds him: bronzed, lean, 33. Reclined by the water. Dark hair resting on hot sandstone slabs. A glass of orange juice perched on his belly, rising and falling with his breath.

His other hand toys with the water’s surface. Delicately it is withdrawn. Glistening droplets run the length of his fingers and drip sensuously onto his face, revealing to the camera for the first time one of the most famous men in the world.

Not Narcissus, but something colder, l’homme fatal. Nonchalantly, from being mesmerized by the water’s surface and his own reflection, he turns his head towards the sky, raises the glass from his chest and pours the juice into his mouth, slow and sensual.

Dark glasses on. Eyes closed. Pinned to the tiles by the late-summer heat. The remnants of the orange juice drying on his tanned skin.

He is, for now, untouchable. At the height of his powers.

Once the initial observations are complete, the two forensic pathologists begin the next phase of the autopsy. They make a clean incision across the scalp and peel the skin down on either side, revealing the smashed cranium beneath. The top of the skull is sawn off, the brain removed. Next, they open the torso, slicing through the abdomen to extract the vital organs. No major anomalies, except the liver. Enlarged, discoloured. Consistent with long-term alcohol abuse.

Once the examination is complete, the organs, including the brain, are placed back in the body cavity. The skull is reassembled; the skin pulled back into place and stitched at the crown. Fingerprints are taken and sent to the central police archives in Paris. The man still has no name.

Based on decomposition, the doctors estimate the time of death to be a week earlier, most likely during the night of 22 to 23 September. In their report, they write plainly: the skull had ‘completely collapsed in’. Cause of death: blunt force trauma to the head with a heavy object. The theory holds – for now – that it was a fight, something that got out of hand.

They sign the paperwork. The report is dispatched to the investigating judge.

Patard reads the autopsy report and gives the photographs a cursory glance. He can’t place it, but something feels off.

It’s the hands that catch his eye. Everyone insists the victim is a vagabond. But how many vagabonds have manicured nails?

The next day, a fingerprint match comes through. The deceased is one Stevan Markovic. Thirty-one years old. Born in Belgrade, 1937. The same prints first appear on records in 1958, when this man applied for political asylum in Paris. Then again:

Prison in Chambéry, 1962 La Santé, Paris, 1965 Namur, Belgium, 1964 Annecy, 1967

His last registered address? 22 Avenue de Messine, in the prestigious 8th arrondissement on the Rive Droite. A stone’s throw and the quartier of the Elysée Palace.

Then another twist. When cross-referenced, it would appear that 22 Avenue de Messine is also home to a famous actor: Alain Delon.

A file arrives from the Brigade Mondaine – the Paris vice squad. Technically tasked with investigating moral crimes, the Mondaine is unofficially tasked with monitoring the rich.

Patard flicks through their dossier on Delon, it’s only a few pages long; a couple of minor run-ins with the law. But then there are the photos: Delon with known gangsters, Corsicans, men who once fought for the Resistance and now run rackets out of Pigalle or control the ports in Marseille.

Who exactly is this Delon? Patard wonders. He calls in two detectives. ‘Go to Avenue de Messine,’ he says. ‘Interview Delon. Find out who this man was to him.’

The detectives stand there, open-mouthed.

‘Right,’ Patard says. ‘Let me know what you find.’

Patard glances at the paperwork again. The residency permit lists the dead man as a political refugee. Occupation: doublure – a stand-in. A stand-in, it would seem, for Alain Delon.

Unlike the two detectives, Patard isn’t impressed. He’s never seen a Delon film. Doesn’t plan to. But something tells him – this case is about to get complicated.

Edward Chisholm, author of A Waiter in Paris, was born in England and moved to Paris after graduating from the School of Oriental and African Studies in London. His work has appeared in The New York Times, The Guardian, and The Financial Times magazine. He lives in England.

Chord Buys Its Rival Zenlytic To Build An Agentic Version Of A CDP

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Two data infrastructure startups with agentic commerce ambitions are combining forces.

On Tuesday, Chord, a 6-year-old commerce tech startup, acquired Zenlytic, an AI startup with an anthropomorphized data analyst agent named “Zoë,” for $5 million.

Chord’s co-founder and CEO, Bryan Mahoney, told AdExchanger that the combined business has around 30 full-time employees and some 60 customers, with an annual run rate nearing $10 million.

Chord launched as a “headless commerce” software company, Mahoney said.

At the time, the headless approach was catching on with Shopify merchants and social-native sellers. The idea was to decouple the front-end website from back-end fulfillment, making for a more agile business and faster-loading pages at a time when Google’s SEO prioritized speed. Shopify has since addressed the problem by taking steps to make its own storefronts faster.

Since the rise of LLMs, Chord and Zenlytic have both pivoted to become explicitly AI-based data infrastructure services. For instance, Mahoney said, Chord is now paid on a usage basis rather than traditional subscription pricing. And because pricing is usage-based, Chord closely tracks how often users prompt its AI agent for an answer – a rate that has accelerated to about once every 20 seconds, he said.

Although the more people use agents, the more ways they find to abuse them.

Mahoney pointed to a recent story in which someone figured out they could use McDonald’s online customer support and ordering agent to answer demanding math and computer science questions – a workaround to having to pay for one’s own AI tokens. (McDonald’s has since fixed this, by the way.)

Chord guards against this kind of misuse. It’s trained its AI agent with the necessary context so that off-base prompts return a response that this isn’t the right agent for the task. That includes requests to use its agent “like a therapist,” say, or for something like “make these results look better to a CFO,” Mahoney said.

Those guardrails matter more as Chord’s business shifts.

The business model is now more like an agentic version of a CDP, Mahoney said. For example, Sonos, a Chord client, can use it to track customers and enrich first-party data, such as understanding when customers are moving to a new home – an opportune time to send emails or SMS notifications about new products.

The data analyst agent “largely replaces” Chord’s own analyst agent, Mahoney said.

Guess human data analysts aren’t the only ones who need to be worried about being replaced by AI. Even agents can be put out of work.

But Chord also has other agents that work better alongside Zoë, Mahoney added. Chord has gone deeper into commerce infrastructure, for instance, with use cases geared toward Shopify-type businesses, and customers using the Snowflake data warehouse in particular. Zenlytic, by contrast, has tended to work with large enterprise brands of all kinds, not just in ecommerce, and supports many more cloud data warehouses.

The goal, Mahoney said, is for some of Zenlytic’s large customers, including Black & Decker, Verizon, J.Crew and Stanley, to adopt Chord’s data infrastructure and activation, including the ability to segment profiles or audiences for campaign targeting and measurement.

But winning over large brands requires confidence in the technology, and right now there’s an odd mix of growing trust and distrust of AI agent outputs.

Mahoney noted that the outright “hallucinations” of earlier LLMs have become relatively rare. “Now it might be more like a lucid dream,” he said. The outputs are believable and often can feel correct.

And so marketers need to be aware that a confident-sounding AI agent can still lead them badly astray. It’s a concern Mahoney shares.

“We constantly [need to] evaluate the answers that we’re getting back to make sure that the way we’re using AI is increasing confidence on a daily basis, not getting eroded,” he said. “These are some of the things that keep me up at night.”

There is a powerful undercurrent of disaffection and outright anger from the American populace toward US sporting institutions right now. But this isn’t just about logos cluttering the playing field. Sports leagues and teams are making decisions that sacrifice player safety and the enjoyment of their own fans in various bids for marketing revenue.

People Inc.’s referral traffic from Google search is down 70% over the past two years, and by 26% in Q3 alone. But its digital revenue is still growing, albeit slowly.

If you ask the executive leadership of Affle and Perion why the two companies are merging, the answer you’ll get is “synergies.”

A combo of Tubi, sports and political ads powered Fox’s growth this last quarter, even as Fox News lost viewers and the Roku deal awaits DOJ clearance.

It’s fall, and M&A is in the air. Two acquisitions happened this week: Stagwell bought Freestar, and DoorDash bought Instacart.

Starting in Q4, Reddit will test logged-in-only mode to protect its user engagement, improve ad targeting and keep AI from scraping its content.

AdExchanger is where marketers, agencies, publishers and tech companies go for the latest information on the trends that are transforming digital media and marketing, from data, privacy, identity and AI to commerce, CTV, measurement and mobile.

Sailing through Cassiopeia

Andrea Arbizzi, taken from Maremma, Italy

A vast sea of faintly lit dust in Cassiopeia envelops the Sailboat Cluster (NGC 225, upper right), an open cluster named for the way its brightest stars and the blue haze of the reflection nebula VdB 4 sketch out a sail. NGC 225 lies about 2,200 light-years away, accompanied by the dark dust lanes of LDN 1291 (just to its left in this image). The photographer collected 15 hours of unfiltered exposure with a 6-inch f/5.6 refractor and one-shot color camera.

Numbers tell a story. Are you uncovering the right ones?

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Surface conversion rates can mislead sales managers when lead mix skews to harder credit buckets. Reviewing credit-bucket conversion and PQ-to-lock rates can pinpoint coaching opportunities and process leaks.   AI Summary

Helping loan originators get clarity on their business is important, especially when the headwinds can easily cause us to lose focus. How can we help our loan originators get more out of what they already have?

While analyzing some data recently, I dug for clarity I could share with a few LOs to help them better leverage what was already working at the top of the funnel.

The first case study was a branch that, on paper, showed a conversion problem. You know the story. How many of our at-bats are making it to the closing table? When I examined this branch, I found they were converting far below the company average.

Easy one. I can simply have a conversation with them about conversion and coach them through some strategies to help them convert better. Let’s take a deeper dive, though, and look at conversion based on credit buckets.

Looking at five different credit buckets, the story started to change. Yes, conversion was still weaker, but the reason why started to become clear.

The at-bats this branch was getting were coming from credit buckets that are harder to convert. Comparing the credit buckets, we found that they were converting higher-credit-score borrowers at the same pace as the rest of the company. Lower-credit-score borrowers and borrowers without a qualifying credit score were a large part of their top of funnel.

This was the reason their conversion was off.

Had I not dug deeper, a coaching conversation could have gone wrong, and we could have focused on some of the wrong things.  The layers of data are important, and we shouldn’t look at just what the surface tells us.

That led me even deeper, where I started to analyze credit-bucket conversion to see what was being left on the table. Yes, there were MLOs who had left significant dollars on the table because they weren’t converting the best borrowers at the same pace as the company average. This coaching conversation becomes about showing up as a vendor or as an advisor.

After all, a high-credit-score borrower knows who they are, and they also know they can get a loan anywhere. If you are just going to point them to a URL, you are teaching them they can get the same level of service and expertise at any URL.

If you’re a data nerd like me, you’ve stuck around to see what’s next.

One of the harder conversations in our business is giving clarity to a top producer about possible leaks in their business. After all, they are in the top 1% in the country among all originators, perennial President’s Club winners and have 5-star reviews in the thousands on lock.

Now imagine that top producer having an absolute banger of a year. Year-over-year volume and units are crushing the year before. The top-line numbers are fun. It’s sexy. It’s what everyone is looking at. Sometimes, though, it doesn’t hurt to take a bit of a deeper look.

In this case, you wouldn’t be surprised to find that this producer is also getting way more at-bats at the top of the funnel than they did the year prior.

If numbers tell a story and the numbers help us get clarity, where else should we look? In this case, I looked at conversion.

PQ-to-funding conversion is important. I looked at a different number, and that was PQ-to-lock conversion. It’s an earlier indicator than PQ-to-funding, and when the closings are coming in much hotter than a year before, you would think those numbers would be moving in parallel all the way through.

What we uncovered was that, compared with the same period the year before, lock conversion was down. Sometimes one of the byproducts of success is that we don’t see the whole field.

Think back to 2020 and 2021. When business was coming at us that fast, it was easy for inefficiencies to hide underneath production. Even today, when winning at a high level, it’s easy to overlook the tiny leaks.

The numbers underneath production told us there was still an opportunity to improve even more. The hard work of creating additional opportunities was already being done. A few adjustments in how those opportunities were handled could create an even greater increase in closed business.

Bryan Lovell is Vice President of Sales at Highland Mortgage.This column does not necessarily reflect the opinion of HousingWire’s editorial department and its owners. To contact the editor responsible for this piece: [email protected].

The border wall dispute isn’t fit for the emergency docket

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Type to search articles, cases, and authors.

In its latest request to the Supreme Court over President Donald Trump’s approach to immigration, the Justice Department turned to the court’s emergency docket to ask for fast-track permission to construct a border barrier system along a 517-mile stretch in remote west Texas. Stymied by a federal district court’s preliminary injunction issued in October, the Justice Department argues that Supreme Court intervention is needed to prevent irreparable harm to the country. But details of the government’s legal argument reveal that the Trump administration hopes to use the emergency docket as a fast track to victory regardless of the actual urgency of the legal dispute.

Early this year, the Department of Homeland Security began taking important preliminary steps to build physical barriers through Big Bend National Park, Big Bend Ranch State Park, and nearby property along the U.S.-Mexico border. Part of the Border Patrol’s Big Bend Sector, covering approximately one-quarter of the entire border, no artificial barriers exist there. Despite its massive geographic reach, the region is sparsely populated and at immense distance from large urban centers. Unlike other parts of the U.S.-Mexico border, few people or goods cross from one country to the other in the Big Bend Sector. Indeed, according to U.S. District Judge Kathleen Cardone, who issued a preliminary injunction blocking DHS from carrying out its plans to erect a border barrier in the area, this is the border region with “the lowest aggregate number of apprehensions [of migrants] and the lowest number of apprehensions per mile of border.”

In Mullin v. Friends of the Ruidosa Church, a group of individuals with personal or business ties to the region, along with two advocacy groups, argued that DHS had failed to comply with required environmental reviews. Pointing to a 1996 law signed by President Bill Clinton, the Justice Department responded that Secretary of Homeland Security Markwayne Mullin and his predecessor, Kristi Noem, waived application of those environmental laws. The government also argued that the district court lacked the power to decide the lawsuit in the first place.

Cardone disagreed with the government on both counts. Congress narrowed courts’ jurisdiction in border-barrier disputes, but left room for courts to consider constitutional challenges to DHS’s conduct, she concluded. The challenge to Mullin’s waiver of environmental laws raises a constitutional question, Cardone added.

Having concluded that she could consider the challengers’ lawsuit, Cardone then turned to the criteria that Congress imposed on the secretary of Homeland Security to waive environmental review. The 1996 law directs the immigration agency “to waive all legal requirements” that could slow or impede construction of border barriers and roads. But the same law also instructs DHS to build barriers and roads “in areas of high illegal entry into the United States.” Considering the government’s data about apprehensions of people and seizures of drugs in the Big Bend Sector, as compared to other parts of the U.S.-Mexico border, Cardone concluded that the region cannot be considered to have high levels of illegal entry. “[I]f the Big Bend Sector is an area of high illegal entry, so is every other sector,” Cardone announced.

Seven days after Cardone released her opinion, the U.S. solicitor general asked the Supreme Court to intervene. Much of the government’s argument turns on its disagreement with Cardone about what constitutes an area of “high illegal entry.” Mullin determined that the Big Bend Sector constitutes an area of high illegal entry because Customs and Border Protection, the DHS agency that enforces immigration laws at and near the border, apprehended 89,000 people and “seized enormous amounts” of illicit drugs from fiscal years 2021 to 2025, the government explains. In addition, failure to build a border barrier in this region “would act as a dangerous magnet” for additional illegal entries, the solicitor general claims. Despite Mullin’s assessment that the facts on the ground met the “high illegal entry” threshold required by Congress, “the district court nonetheless issued an unprecedented injunction blocking those waivers and halting associated border-wall construction,” the solicitor general wrote to the court.

Unsurprisingly, the challengers disagree. Opposing the government’s request for relief from the justices, they describe Mullin and Noem’s waivers as “unprecedented” and claim they “carry undisputed and extraordinary political and economic significance.”

The solicitor general’s claim is fundamentally a dispute about the proper interpretation of the text of the 1996 law. The government argues that courts cannot second-guess Mullin’s decision to waive environmental laws, and that Mullin’s view of what constitutes “high illegal entry” is all that matters. Cardone disagrees and contends that courts have a role to play here.

By turning to the emergency docket, the Justice Department has dispensed with the ordinary appellate process in favor of a fast-track path to, it hopes, final victory. Wanting an answer sooner rather than later is understandable, but a party’s interest in “line-jumping,” as Justice Ketanji Brown Jackson has described the emergency docket, isn’t reason enough to circumvent the slower course of the ordinary appellate process. Jackson would prefer to limit the emergency docket to situations in which an actual emergency exists. It’s hard to see what exigency exists in west Texas, however. The solicitor general argues that drugs and people will continue to enter through the Big Bend Sector. That is undoubtedly true, but, as that stretch of land has been unfortified since it became a border on March 18, 1848, nothing is likely to occur in the near future that is markedly different from past decades.

The government’s request for fast-track intervention by the Supreme Court also does not meet the standards set by Jackson’s ideological opposites. Explaining his view of the court’s emergency docket in Trump v. CASA, the court’s fast-track decision announcing that district courts are not authorized to issue universal injunctions, Justice Brett Kavanaugh wrote that its alternative pathway is appropriate when “Congress passes a major new statute, or the Executive Branch issues a major new rule or executive order.” Neither applies to the border-wall dispute. Congress enacted the contested statute 30 years ago and the waivers issued by Mullin and Noem are neither an agency rule nor an executive order.

Nor is the border-wall legal battle the type of dispute that only the Supreme Court can resolve. In a 2021 speech, Justice Samuel Alito compared the court’s emergency docket to a hospital’s emergency room, suggesting that, in his view at least, the fast-track process is proper only when no suitable alternative exists. But an alternative does exist to decide if Cardone overstepped. The government could have appealed Cardone’s decision to the U.S. Court of Appeals for the 5th Circuit. Like other circuit courts, the 5th Circuit regularly reviews preliminary injunctions granted by district courts. As Kavanaugh explained in CASA, “The courts of appeals and this Court can (and regularly do) expeditiously review district court decisions awarding or denying preliminary injunctive relief.” Instead of asking the 5th Circuit to correct the mistake it claims Cardone committed, the solicitor general skipped the 5th Circuit and went directly to the Supreme Court.

The reason it did this is not difficult to figure out: The administration is hopeful that it will receive a friendly audience at the Supreme Court. But this is a fast-track procedure meant for exceptional circumstances. And there is nothing exceptional about the legal fight over the administration’s attempt to build new segments of border barrier in rural west Texas. If the court grants the government’s request to pause Cardone’s preliminary injunction, allowing construction to move forward where no barrier currently exists, it should, at the very least, explain how this case meets the criteria that the justices have previously provided and if the term “emergency docket” is a misnomer.

César Cuauhtémoc García Hernández is the author of three books about immigration law and policy.

Recommended Citation: César Cuauhtémoc García Hernández, The border wall dispute isn’t fit for the emergency docket, SCOTUSblog (Nov. 3, 2026, 9:30 AM), https://www.scotusblog.com/2026/11/the-border-wall-dispute-isnt-fit-for-the-emergency-docket/

How Flavored Syrup Became a Coffee-Shop Culture War

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

For some coffee shop customers, premade syrups are now a major red flag. It presents a new opportunity for small business owners.

In a TikTok video with over 82,000 likes, the person behind the camera zooms into the shelves at a Pennsylvania coffee shop, three rows stacked with an array of Torani syrups in flavors like blueberry, brown-sugar cinnamon, English toffee, and salted caramel. “I just know my latte isn’t going to be good,” the caption reads. Across social media, similar sentiments appear. “If I see these at a cafe, I’m walking out immediately,” says one tweet, co-signed by many commenters. In a multipart video series, another creator documents her journey of replacing all of her Torani syrups with ones from small businesses instead. Last year, the niche food newsletter Unsnackable published what was described as a “Torani tirade.”

It’s not just a Torani problem, though the brand — by virtue of its market dominance — tends to get the most flack on social media. While some people seem to prefer competitor Monin, it, too, draws backlash over allegations of tasting “like chemicals.” The problem, for some drinkers of dessert-like specialty beverages, is premade syrups across the board.

For a certain sect of consumers, they’ve become a coffee shop red flag, a sign that a so-called “specialty” establishment asking them to fork over $8 plus tip for a latte is unfairly cutting corners when it comes to quality or craft. For these consumers, mass-market syrups now symbolize exactly that: They’re synonymous with coffee chains and corporations, the kind of impersonal institutions that many people are trying to get away from when they patronize a small business. The flip side: For independently owned coffee shops across the country, house-made syrup now represents a major opportunity.

When Carrie and Dennis Medina first envisioned their coffee shop, Room Service, in Ventura, California, they thought they would take an approach similar to the one at Sightglass Coffee, where they’d both gotten their start. “Very purist, with no syrups, no specialty drinks,” Dennis says. They imagined eight kinds of pour-over coffee and an emphasis on single-origin beans, since the couple also runs a roastery. “We said we’re never gonna do cream tops; we’re never gonna do these crazy drinks,” Carrie says. But in the first week alone, every customer was asking for them.

Now, Room Service offers 10 to 12 different syrups at any given time (and six different cream tops), all of them made in-house. It offers drinks such as a cinnamon roll latte topped with a cinnamon roll, and a honey cornbread latte with an ube cream top. “I hear that question all the time,” Dennis says. “‘Hey, do you guys make your syrups in-house?’” To him, a shop that isn’t trying to do at least the basics — like vanilla — in-house is “falling behind what the times are pushing for.”

The demand for syrups and other embellishments is so intense that the Medinas have brought on a full-time prep person who works exclusively on “all these things that we do in-house that make us special,” Carrie says. Room Service’s PSL, for example, starts with kabocha squash. It’s roasted, blended with spices such as cloves and cinnamon, and cooked into syrup, a process that takes two days. Echoing a sentiment that seems common among coffee shop owners right now, Carrie describes their current approach as “not necessarily the coffee shop we wanted, but it’s more like the coffee shop we have to be.” And if the market demands syrups and cream tops, then the Medinas wanted to ensure that the intention and emphasis on quality that they applied to their coffee beans also carried over.

In 2025, Michael Smith opened Trust Coffee Co. in Bay Village, Ohio, taking over, remodeling, and rebranding an older coffee shop. He, like the Medinas, imagined a more “pour-over, third-wave” style establishment. “But I’ve been a business owner for a long time,” says Smith, who also runs a restaurant and a catering business. “I’ve realized over the years that your customers tell you more about who you are than who you actually think you are.” His customers, it turned out, wanted seasonal syrups in niche flavors like maple rosemary, caramelized banana, and strawberry rhubarb.

The purist is no longer coffee’s north star; the maximalist is.

Syrups like Monin, which the shop’s previous iteration used, “serve a purpose in the industry,” Smith says, but he wanted more control over the menu. The two full-time pastry chefs from Smith’s other businesses oversee the shop’s syrups. “It’s become such a huge thing,” he says: Every time Trust’s social media manager posts a new syrup, the line is out the door for a week. “It sets us apart,” he explains.

When Sabrina Kaylor opened the first location of Bizarre Coffee in Canton, Georgia in 2020, the shop used bottled syrups. She hadn’t worked in coffee prior, and she said that approach felt like the norm at the time. “It allowed us to hone our craft and learn what we were doing,” she says. Over time, Kaylor — who’s quite active on the company’s official social media platforms — noticed that the tenor of the conversation was changing: In the immediate post-COVID years, people were making more things at home and there was more pride to be gained in “scratch-made.” With her business becoming known for lattes in flavors such as blueberry, banana, and guava, Kaylor decided to shift toward more house-made syrups. Previously, her customers didn’t ask about their sourcing, but now, they ask all the time.

As much as there is a sense of valor in the entirely from-scratch approach, Kaylor retains some skepticism about the current torrent of Torani and Monin backlash. Today, 90 percent of Bizarre’s drinks use house-made syrups, though one of its best-selling drinks still relies on the bottled stuff; Bizarre’s menu is extensive and making everything in-house would be a significant investment of labor. On the part of coffee shops, “I think a lot of [the house-made syrup conversation] is marketing, quite honestly,” Kaylor says. As for the bandwagoning that is all the anti-Torani memes, “I think it’s part of consumer behavior,” she says. And that, she acknowledges, can be really “discouraging” for small business owners.

In a sense, that’s what the premade-versus-house-made-syrup conversation boils down to: the stand between small businesses and big ones. For more consumers, the economic and social conditions of the current moment have inspired increased skepticism toward the influence of corporations and private equity in the food and beverage world.

The Minnesota coffee shop Little Joy has become famous across social media for posting the recipes of its signature drinks in detailed DIY videos. It has since launched a membership program through which other coffee shop owners can purchase its recipes, scaled up for commercial usage; gain access to training videos; and sell its drinks without even needing to attribute them to Little Joy, though the ones that would like to can pin their use of Little Joy’s recipes to a Drink Finder map.

Yes, these might just be recipes for syrups and foams, but in an interview with Eater’s industry newsletter Pre Shift earlier this year, Little Joy owner Cody Larson described the system as a way to build solidarity among independent businesses. “The big coffee chains, they’re never gonna do house-made syrups, so I think that’s where independent shops can get a leg up,” he said. “Ultimately, the chains are our competition... If independent shops can, in any way possible, raise the bar across the board, that’s good for all independent shops, including us.”

It’s hard to look at the drinks coming out of specialty coffee shops today — fluffy with foam, flavored to emulate nuanced desserts like princess cake and tiramisu— and not think of the Starbucks Frappuccino. The combination of app-ordering and social media “hack” culture have mainstreamed super-elaborate beverage requests, allowing Starbucks and coffee chains like it to become purveyors of highly complicated customizable beverages, mainstreaming the mindset of the aforementioned “hyperflavor.”

When it comes to why consumers have moved away from the single-origin pour-over approach at shops like his, “my guess is that these are a lot of people who used to be Starbucks loyalists and they’re looking for something that’s more local,” Smith says. “It’s a trend across everything where people want local, and we’re able to provide what Starbucks did, but in a more high-quality, more local way.”

Intentionality is what people are gravitating towards, especially as prices rise. If consumers are spending so much on a drink that’s undeniably a luxury, then they want to know that every part of that drink has been made carefully. And if they’re going to make the trip to a new, independent shop, they want to know that the offerings there aren’t the ones they can get anywhere, made with easily attainable, big-brand flavorings. For small coffee shops, the key to success could be as simple as syrup.

You can learn a lot from a great founder session. But sometimes the conversation that changes what you do next happens five minutes after the session ends. And with the event happening tomorrow, there is another reason to make sure you are in the room: The format is designed to help founders learn in different ways throughout the day.

On November 4, TechCrunch Founder Summit 2026 brings founders and investors together at SoWa Power Station in Boston for one day of practical learning, candid conversations, live pitching, smaller roundtables, and networking.

Register now to save up to $100 on your pass, plus get a second for free. Or save up to 30% on group bundles.

The Build Stage focuses on the decisions founders make while they are still shaping, testing, and growing the business. These sessions get into the mechanics behind building a company, from distribution and fundraising to team building and pitching.

Hunter Worland of NEA opens the conversation on why distribution can become a startup’s next competitive advantage, especially when strong technology alone is not enough to win customers.

Kristen Craft of Fidelity Private Shares brings a current view of fundraising trends, helping founders understand how the capital environment is shifting and what that means for the way they approach their next raise.

The stage also gets tactical about building high-performing teams and sharpening the story you tell investors, customers, and potential hires.

Dave Balter of True Ventures and Edisyl takes on a deceptively simple question: Does your pitch earn the next conversation?

The value of the Build Stage is straightforward: These are lessons founders can take back to their companies immediately.

If the Build Stage is about creating momentum, the Impact Stage is about what happens once that momentum becomes real. This is where founders can step back and think about bigger decisions around product-market fit, scaling, enterprise relationships, leadership, and long-term growth.

Steve Fredette, president, co-founder, and director of Toast, brings the perspective of building and scaling one of Boston’s most recognizable startup success stories.

Rudina Seseri, founder and managing partner of Glasswing Ventures, explores the tension every founder eventually faces: when to scale and when hitting the brakes may actually protect the business.

Kent Bennett of Bessemer Venture Partners tackles product-market fit before scale, helping founders think more critically about whether the signals they are seeing truly justify the next phase of growth.

Chase Garbarino, CEO and co-founder of HqO, brings the conversation home with the Boston Founder Playbook and a perspective on building ambitious companies outside the Bay Area.

The Impact Stage is valuable because it creates space for founders to get out of the weeds for a moment and ask whether they are scaling the right things, in the right way, at the right time.

Main Stage sessions can give you perspective. Roundtables give you room to get specific.

Founder Summit’s smaller roundtables are built around highly focused problems that many founders are actively solving right now, from entering difficult verticals and building enterprise trust to creating lean teams and opening doors with major customers.

Chirag Shah of Define Ventures digs into what happens when your first vertical is also one of the hardest to enter.

A technical session with Shishir Tewari of Procore Technologies moves the AI conversation from broad hype to the practical challenge of reducing hallucinations with better enterprise context.

Merve Kitapci of ADIN.AI gets highly specific about enterprise selling and what it takes to land major global customers before Series A.

Andy Tang of Draper Associates opens a different kind of founder conversation: What if you are building something the market does not even have a name for yet?

Linden Tibbets of IFTTT explores how AI may change the assumptions founders make about team size, productivity, and what a smaller company can realistically accomplish.

The value here is participation. Instead of simply hearing how someone else approached a problem, founders can bring their own questions, compare experiences with the people around the table, and leave with ideas that are much closer to their actual business.

Founder Summit also gives attendees the chance to see what happens when founders put their stories in front of investors in real time.

During “So You Think You Can Pitch?,” founders make their cases in front of investors, including Dave Balter, Chirag Shah, and Simran Suri of Maveron.

This is one of the most interactive parts of the day because it turns advice about pitching into something founders can actually watch: what gets an investor’s attention, where a story loses momentum, and which questions come up immediately.

For anyone preparing to raise, sell, recruit, or simply explain their company more clearly, seeing those reactions live can be incredibly useful.

That matters because some of the most useful connections at an event happen after you have already shared a room, a question, or a conversation earlier in the day.

Founder Summit is not built around one type of conference experience. The Build Stage gives founders practical lessons they can apply now. The Impact Stage creates room for bigger growth and leadership decisions. The roundtables let you ask questions and go deeper. The live pitch competition puts founder storytelling to the test. Apple faces unprecedented memory cost increases as RAM prices continue to surge, manufacturing bill climbs by $33 billion

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

AI demand is tightening DRAM and NAND supply, pushing up costs for device makers and leaving less memory available for consumer electronics

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Apple is about to feel the squeeze from rising memory prices, with the cost of RAM climbing just as the company heads into its busiest shopping season. According to Reuters, Apple expects to pay more for memory in the December quarter, with the CEO, John Ternus, describing the current price increases as “unprecedented. The company has so far managed to soften the blow by using memory bought at lower prices, cutting costs elsewhere and raising prices on some products. However, it is reported that its manufacturing bill has climbed by $33 billion in just a few months.

But that cushion is getting thinner, and Apple expects memory purchases to become even more expensive into 2027. “What we're seeing with memory pricing right now is unprecedented. And as we said earlier in the September quarter, we did see higher memory costs as compared to the June quarter, which were partly offset by the benefit of carrying inventory and lower costs on certain non-memory components. Now for the December quarter, we expect even higher memory costs, partly offset by a few factors. So as you know, we changed the price on many of our products, including Mac and iPad at the end of June and iPhone in September, and these price increases provided some offset.” Ternus said during Apple’s November 2 earnings call.

Memory is used across Apple's hardware lineup, so sustained price increases could affect manufacturing cost for several product categories—although Apple has not announced another round of price increases. Absorbing higher components could, however, indefinitely become difficult. Dave Nicholson, Chief Research Officer at the Futurum Group, also identified memory prices as a growing pressure point for Apple's margins and hardware pricing through the holiday season and into 2027. According to him, Apple is in a different position from companies pouring hundreds of billions of dollars into AI data center infrastructure. The impact of higher component costs is easier to quantify than the eventual returns on massive AI capital expenditure, he said.

The next pressure point for @Apple may not be AI CapEx, but the rising cost of memory across its hardware portfolio. @TheFuturumGroup Chief Research Officer @DaveN007 explained that higher memory prices could put more pressure on Apple’s margins and pricing through the holiday season and into 2027. Even with demand holding up, the question is how much of those higher costs consumers will absorb. Dave also sees Apple in a stronger position than companies committing hundreds of billions to data center infrastructure, because the impact of higher component costs is easier to quantify than the long-term returns on massive AI CapEx. ▶️ Watch Dave’s analysis of Apple’s margin outlook, memory costs, and why its AI infrastructure strategy could give the company more flexibility. #Apple #AI #Semiconductors #FuturumNovember 3, 2026

And the warnings from Apple's suppliers suggest that the cost pressure is unlikely to ease quickly. Last month, Samsung, a major supplier of RAM(DRAM), NAND flash storage and OLED displays for Apple, said that memory demand would far exceed supply as AI infrastructure continues to expand. Its memory division also reported a quarter-on-quarter increase in DRAM average prices during the third quarter, while NAND prices rose by high-teen percentages. Senior executive vice president of its memory business warned the supply crunch could extend to 2028 and beyond. Elsewhere, SK Hynix delivered a similar warning, saying it had secured long-term agreements covering more than Half of its production capacity for2027 and 2028. Micron, the third major supplier for Apple aside from Samsung and SK Hynix, is also benefiting from the surging demand for memory. Its latest published outlook warns that supply shortages in memory and storage could take “considerable time” to improve.

We have previously reported several signs of the pressure now confronting Apple. In January, the company was already scrambling to secure memory supplies amid strong demand, with then CEO Tim Cook warning that rising prices could have a greater impact on earnings. By June, rising component expenses had translated into higher retail prices. Apple increased prices across several Mac and iPad models, including a $400 increase to the starting price of the MacBook Pro, due to the RAM crisis. In July, Cook described the memory market as a “hundred-year-flood” and said Apple expected to pay more for memory in the September quarter. The company had also increased its inventory to $11.09 billion as it built a buffer against supply and price pressures.

Want an iPhone Duo for the holidays? Apple’s latest stock update isn’t reassuring

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

The iPhone Duo likely won't make it into Santa's sleigh this year. Here's what Apple had to say about it.

Apple is unsure when it will have more of its foldable iPhone Duo in stock for customers to purchase ahead of Black Friday and the holiday shopping season.

During Apple’s earnings call on Monday, Nov. 2, Apple’s chief operating officer, Sabih Khan, told investors that the company couldn’t say when supply of its new iPhone Duo would catch up with current demand.

Also: iPhone Duo hands-on: Some magical touches

“iPhone Duo is constrained as of today … We’re working hard to get this revolutionary new product into the hands of our customers as quickly as possible. But at this time, it’s really hard to predict when supply and demand will be in balance,” Khan told investors.

The stock and shipping delays were among the broader concerns on an earnings call riddled with questions about rising memory chip costs driven by the AI industry, according to reports from TechCrunch.

Also: iPhone Duo shortage watch: Changing wait times, buying limits, launch-day odds

Last month, the iPhone Duo (Apple’s priciest smartphone to date) sold out shortly after its official launch on Oct. 23. Apple told the Financial Times that demand for the new Duo was “nothing short of extraordinary” after the smashing sell-outs, though veteran techies like Bloomberg’s Mark Gurman posited that Apple knew it would encounter supply chain issues, yet it was still the plan all along.

Those who didn’t initially jump to preorder the new device or wait in line at an Apple store are still facing shipping times that extend into 2027. Some outlets are even reporting that carriers like Verizon have offered gift cards as apologies for the extended delays.

Apple CEO John Ternus signaled the company’s effort to meet demand for the iPhone Duo, announcing that Apple will be “deepening” its partnership with Perryman, a titanium producer supplying pieces for the device.

Also: iPhone Duo screen cracks: Serious issue or overblown? What we know

“We’ve been thrilled with the early response from reviewers and customers, and we are working hard to get iPhone Duo into customers’ hands as quickly as we can,” Ternus said during his opening remarks on the earnings call.

While the iPhone Duo snafu is a rare event for Apple, which traditionally has a strong reputation for a smooth supply chain, rising demand for memory chips is raising hardware costs across the industry, and it’s something Apple can’t circumvent. On the call, Khan noted that Apple has had to increase prices on numerous products, though he said those decisions were made rather “reluctantly.”

Back in June, MacBooks and iPads saw price increases of up to 25% due to rising hardware and chip costs.

As Apple works to iron out its kinks, customers may be looking elsewhere. Reports from Korea indicate that Samsung Galaxy Z Fold 8 sales jumped 20% in the week after the iPhone Duo became available, and recent reports of cracked displays are another strike for Apple.

Also: Want an iPhone Duo before 2027? Try this

So, Santa may not be able to deliver the iPhone Duo this year, but Samsung’s foldables may make it onto Christmas lists across the globe instead.
