My Parents Took Me Voting. Now I'm Doing The Same With My Daughter.

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Your candidate might not always win. But, as my parents taught me, it’s important to show up anyway.

My earliest memories of voting take place at the elementary school up the street from my parents’ house. Every Election Day, my parents would bring me and my younger sister along with them to the polls. I remember standing in the long lines impatiently wondering when it’d be our turn. I remember at least one year where there was a mock ballot for kids. And, perhaps most importantly to elementary-school-aged me, I remember receiving the prized Future Voter sticker at the end.

At the time, I didn’t think of any of this as civic education. My parents weren’t necessarily sitting us down for lectures about democracy or giving us pop quizzes on the Voting Rights Act of 1965 (although that would come later during my AP U.S. Government class). My parents were simply voting and we were watching. But, as it turns out, we were learning by observing.

My parents made it abundantly clear to my sister and me that voting was both a right and a responsibility — and that this wasn’t always the case for people who looked like us. As Black girls growing up in America, that history mattered. We learned that Black people had marched, organized, been beaten and even killed for the right my parents exercised so routinely at the elementary school around the corner.

So, when Election Day came around, we went. And not just for the presidential elections every four years. My parents participated in every election — local, federal, didn’t matter. The responsibility was the same.

By the time I was finally old enough to vote myself, I couldn’t wait.

I missed being able to vote in the 2004 presidential election by a year, which meant the 2008 election would be my first chance to vote for president. And I felt elated that my first presidential vote would be for the country’s first Black president.

At the time, I was working as a reporter at my community newspaper in a conservative county in Maryland. On Election Day, a colleague and I spent hours driving around to various polling places, interviewing voters and reporting on turnout before returning to the Board of Elections to wait for results.

After work, I went home and watched the national returns with my dad. When Barack Obama walked onto the stage in Chicago’s Grant Park with his beautiful Black family, I remember staring at the television in disbelief.

Wow, I thought to myself, soaking in the moment. I just witnessed history.

But elections don’t always end with the history you hoped to witness.

In 2016, I proudly cast my ballot for Hillary Clinton and went to bed believing we were about the elect the country’s first woman president. We all know how that turned out.

Four years later, I voted for Joe Biden, but I was especially excited about his running mate, Kamala Harris, whom I’d supported in the Democratic primary. Watching a Black and Asian woman become vice president meant something different to me. Of course, representation isn’t the only reason to vote for someone and I think we’re all doing ourselves a disservice if we pretend as if identity is entirely separate from politics. As a Black woman in America, I’d spent most of my life looking at the highest echelons of power and rarely seeing anyone who looks like you.

In the spring of 2024, I picked up my then two-year-old daughter Violet from daycare, went to swim lessons and then swung by our neighborhood elementary school to vote in the Illinois presidential primary. With her little puffs and binky in tow, she naturally became the star of the polling place.

Our next-door neighbor, Mr. Steve, was working the polls that day and gave Violet a Future Voter sticker. She was entranced. To her, the sticker was probably the best part of the whole outing, which, when I thought about it, was exactly how I felt when I was her age (and, let’s be real, is also how I feel as an adult).

Afterward, we went outside to the school playground and, as I watched her run around, it hit me what a full-circle moment this had become. And then I had another thought: This could be a children’s book.

I wanted to write a book that could explain voting to children around Violet’s age: something that would talk about walking into a polling place; why grown-ups stood in line to fill out ballots; what even is a ballot; and why any of this matters anyway.

That fateful afternoon eventually became the premise of Violet Goes Voting, an early reader about a little girl accompanying her parents to the polls and learning that voting is one way we use our voices. But the book was just the beginning.

That fall, my husband and I took Violet with us when we voted in the presidential election. This time, I had a very specific picture in my head of what the moment might look like in both real life and the book version. My daughter would sit on my lap as I cast my ballot to elect the first Black woman president of the United States.

My parents had taken their Black daughters to the polls so we would understand that our voices mattered. Decades later, I would take mine with me as I helped elect a Black woman to the White House. Except, of course, that’s not what happened.

Like so many people, I woke up the next morning devastated. The history I had imagined sharing with my daughter would have to wait. Since then, I’ve thought a lot about what, exactly, I want Violet to inherit from me and learn from all of this.

Because maybe the lesson was never supposed to be that if you show up, your candidate will win. My parents certainly never promised that to me. What they taught me was the importance of showing up anyway.

Voting is a small part of civic engagement, but it was my introduction to it nonetheless. Long before I knew what a primary was, before I understood the harsh reality of voter suppression or could name any of my congressional representatives, before I was old enough to fill in a bubble next to a candidate’s name, I knew what Election Day looked like.

It looked like my parents making time to go to the polls. It looked like my sister and me eagerly tagging along. It looked like waiting our turn. And, yes, it looked like getting a sticker at the end.

Now, as a mom, I understand that those seemingly ordinary trips were teaching me something much bigger: This is what our family does. We show up. We pay attention. We responsibly exercise the rights people before us fought to secure. We don’t take them for granted.

Only time will tell who Violet will vote for when she’s old enough to cast her first ballot. And, frankly, we have a while before that happens. She’s much more interested in playgrounds than politics at the moment, which is exactly how it should be for an innocent five-year-old.

But I hope that when her first Election Day arrives, she’ll look back on our family selfie from outside the voting place in 2024. I hope she’ll remember me reading to her the book that she inspired. I hope she understands why walking into that booth and making her own choice carries a history older than the both of us combined.

But most of all and most importantly, I simply hope she shows up. Because that’s the inheritance my parents gave me and my sister. And now it’s mine to pass on.

L’Oreal Thompson Payton is an author, journalist and the founder of Zora’s Place, a Black feminist bookstore and community hub in Evanston, Illinois. Her books include Stop Waiting for Perfect, Amanda Gorman: Poet and Activist, Violet Goes Voting and the highly-anticipated memoir Infertile Black Girl (Fall 2027). Her byline has appeared in outlets such as Essence, Fortune, New York magazine and SELF. Learn more at lorealthompsonpayton.com.

Oldest recordings of Louisiana Creole discovered in Indiana archive

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 3, 2026 2:20 PM EST

A trailblazing Black linguist’s historical audio recordings have been rediscovered more than 90 years after his vital fieldwork. Originally etched onto aluminum discs, Lorenzo Dow Turner’s recordings are now the oldest known audio clips of authentic Louisiana Creole. Although the language has all-but-disappeared from daily life, the invaluable work resurfaces at a time when researchers and Creole descendents are striving to preserve—and even revive—the unique dialect.

As a language, Louisiana Creole is a distinctly American creation. Born from the international slave trade during the 18th century, the hybrid language primarily blended the French spoken by colonists and slavers with multiple West African dialects, particularly from the Senegambian region. Creole continued to evolve over ensuing generations among both enslaved and free communities, who infused French vocabulary with regionally unique grammar, sentence structures, and phrases.

“Africans with little common language were forced onto plantations where French was the language of administration. Everybody is learning approximations of French and then approximations of approximations,” Oliver Mayeux, a linguist at the University of Cambridge, recently explained. “Quite quickly, a new language is born.”

By the 19th century, Louisiana Creole was the common tongue of enslaved plantation workers and their overseers. It was also spoken by Haitian refugees, Caribbean immigrants, recently arrived Acadians from Canada, and much of New Orleans’ dock workers. Only a few thousand native Creole speakers remain today, almost all of them elderly and living in extremely rural areas of the state. But even by the 1930s, contemporary linguists like Turner understood that the language was in decline.

Although the specifics remain unclear, it appears that Turner traveled to Louisiana in 1935 to record Creole speakers for a project that ultimately never received proper funding. These clips included interviews with many people of color, including former field workers and sharecroppers. To accomplish this, Turner relied on a network of Black doctors and academics in Louisiana to find interviewees, who he then recorded in his colleagues’ private offices.

“These were trusted spaces for the participants during the era of Jim Crow segregation, and, crucially, were wired for electricity,” said Mayeux.

Recording instruments weren’t simple, portable tools at that time. Turner’s equipment consisted of a hefty microphone and two cabinet-like boxes that collectively weighed over 100 pounds.

“For these interviewees, the alien nature of the equipment alone would have made it a truly weird experience for them,” Mayeux added.

Turner apparently never published his work on Louisiana Creole, but a biography of the linguist tipped Mayeux off to the project. He eventually tracked the aluminum recording discs to Indiana University’s Archives of Traditional Music, where preservationists had already digitized them.

Mayeux was astonished to listen to the long-forgotten, clear recordings of native Creole speakers discussing their daily lives and personal histories. Some may have been more embellished than others, however. One participant, Odelia Edmond, vividly describes a childhood memory of swindling 30 cents from Zozo Labrique, a New Orleans folk figure who sold red brick dust used in protective Voodou rituals. After reviewing the clips, Mayeux described her as an “extraordinary character.”

“She had a husband who was murdered, after which she remarried. In the recording she says ‘I was so glad when they killed him,’” he said.

Another subject, George Doyle, worked as a cop until New Orleans segregated its police department around the turn of the 20th century. He remembers an instance of being assaulted by a white gang he described as a “band of rats,” which is corroborated in an archival newspaper article from 1893.

Turner’s once forgotten pivotal recordings are now available online for anyone to experience. The finds are not only a major development for researchers of Louisiana Creole, but for younger generations in the Kouri-Vini movement, a group working to revive the language in the 21st century.

“Young people are learning Creole to connect with the richness of their heritage, helping them discover a distinct identity in an increasingly homogenized world,” said Mayeux.

A word of caution to anyone fluent in French, however: don’t expect to have an advantage in your studies.

“Contemporary French speakers would simply not be able to comprehend this language,” warned Mayeux.

2026 Full Moon calendar: When to see the Full Moon and phases

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

The phenomenon of a Full Moon arises when our planet, Earth, is precisely sandwiched between the Sun and the Moon. This alignment ensures the entire side of the Moon that faces us gleams under sunlight. Thanks to the Moon’s orbit around Earth, the angle of sunlight hitting the lunar surface and being reflected back to our planet changes. That creates different lunar phases.

The next Full Moon will be the November Beaver Moon, which occurs at 9:54 a.m. EST on Tuesday, Nov. 24, 2026. It will also be a Super Moon.

We’ll update this article multiple times each week with the latest moonrise, moonset, Full Moon schedule, and some of what you can see in the sky each week.

Here’s the complete list of Full Moons this year and their traditional names.

(All times Eastern; * denotes a Super Moon.)

The images below show the day-by-day phases of the Moon in November. The Full Moon this month will be on Tuesday, Nov. 24, 2026.

The times below for sunrise, sunset, moonrise, and moonset are given in local time from 40° N 90° W. The Moon’s illumination is given at 10 P.M. local time from the same location. All other times are Eastern unless otherwise specified. For more details on upcoming sky events, check out our full Sky This Week column.

Tuesday, November 3The Moon now passes 0.8° south of the bright star Regulus in Leo at 4 A.M. EST. Some regions of the world will see our satellite pass in front of Regulus in an occultation, but again, the event is not visible from North America.

The pair rises together shortly after midnight on the 2nd. By 5 A.M. local time on the 3rd, the Moon-star pair is nearly 50° high in the southeast. The waning Moon has shrunk from roughly 43% lit yesterday morning to 33% lit today. It is just under a week from New phase, which occurs on the 9th.

Sunrise: 6:31 A.M.Sunset: 4:55 P.M.Moonrise: 12:34 A.M.Moonset: 2:07 P.M.Moon Phase: Waning crescent (26%)

Wednesday, November 4Venus sits 2° south of Virgo’s brightest star, Spica, in the early-morning sky. The pair is joined by a lovely crescent Moon just over 20% lit, high above them in Leo.

Sunrise: 6:32 A.M.Sunset: 4:54 P.M.Moonrise: 1:44 A.M.Moonset: 2:30 P.M.Moon Phase: Waning crescent (17%)

Sunrise: 6:34 A.M.Sunset: 4:53 P.M.Moonrise: 2:50 A.M.Moonset: 2:53 P.M.Moon Phase: Waning crescent (10%)

Sunrise: 6:35 A.M.Sunset: 4:52 P.M.Moonrise: 3:56 A.M.Moonset: 3:16 P.M.Moon Phase: Waning crescent (5%)

The phases of the Moon are: New Moon, waxing crescent, First Quarter, waxing gibbous, Full Moon, waning gibbous, Last Quarter, and waning crescent. A cycle starting from one Full Moon to its next counterpart, termed the synodic month or lunar month, lasts about 29.5 days.

Though a Full Moon only occurs during the exact moment when Earth, Moon, and Sun form a perfect alignment, to our eyes, the Moon seems Full for around three days.

There are a wide variety of specialized names used to identify distinct types or timings of Full Moons. These names primarily trace back to a blend of cultural, agricultural, and natural observations about the Moon, aimed at allowing humans to not only predict seasonal changes, but also track the passage of time.

For instance, almost every month’s Full Moon boasts a name sourced from Native American, Colonial American, or other North American traditions, with their titles mirroring seasonal shifts and nature’s events.

Wolf Moon (January): Inspired by the cries of hungry wolves.

Snow Moon (February): A nod to the month’s often heavy snowfall.

Worm Moon (March): Named after the earthworms that signal thawing grounds.

Pink Moon (April): In honor of the blossoming pink wildflowers.

Flower Moon (May): Celebrating the bloom of flowers.

Strawberry Moon (June): Marks the prime strawberry harvest season.

Buck Moon (July): Recognizing the new antlers on bucks.

Sturgeon Moon (August): Named after the abundant sturgeon fish.

Corn Moon (September): Signifying the corn harvesting period.

Hunter’s Moon (October): Commemorating the hunting season preceding winter.

Beaver Moon (November): Reflects the time when beavers are busy building their winter dams.

Cold Moon (December): Evocative of winter’s chill.

In addition, there are a few additional names for Full Moons that commonly make their way into public conversations and news.

Super Moon: This term is reserved for a Full Moon that aligns with the lunar perigee, which is the Moon’s nearest point to Earth in its orbit. This proximity renders the Full Moon unusually large and luminous. For a Full Moon to earn the Super Moon tag, it should be within approximately 90 percent of its closest distance to Earth.

Blue Moon: A Blue Moon is the second Full Moon in a month that experiences two Full Moons. This phenomenon graces our skies roughly every 2.7 years. Though the term suggests a color, Blue Moons aren’t truly blue. Very occasionally, atmospheric conditions such as recent volcanic eruptions might lend the Moon a slightly blueish tint, but this hue isn’t tied to the term.

Black Moon: A Black Moon is a recent, unofficial term that can describe one of two situations. Either the third of four New Moons in a single astronomical season or the second of two New Moons in a month is a Black Moon. As its name suggests, the Black Moon is completely dark, as it is a New Moon that occurs when the Moon is between the Sun and Earth. The New Moon cannot be observed except during a total solar eclipse.

Harvest Moon: Occurring closest to the autumnal equinox, typically in September, the Harvest Moon is often renowned for a distinct orange tint it might display. This Full Moon rises close to sunset and sets near sunrise, providing extended hours of bright moonlight. Historically, this was invaluable to farmers gathering their produce.

What is the difference between a Full Moon and a New Moon? A Full Moon is witnessed when Earth is between the Sun and the Moon, making the entire Moon’s face visible. Conversely, during a New Moon, the Moon lies between Earth and the Sun, shrouding its Earth-facing side in darkness.

How does the Full Moon influence tides? The Moon’s gravitational tug causes Earth’s waters to bulge, birthing tides. During both Full and New Moons, the Sun, Earth, and Moon are in alignment, generating “spring tides.” These tides can swing exceptionally high or low due to the combined gravitational influences of the Sun and Moon.

Old Town Sportsman PDL 120 Pro Kayak Review

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

We may earn revenue from the products available on this page and participate in affiliate programs. Learn more

The Old Town Sportsman PDL 120 Pro is a beast. I tested this boat on a 6-mile fishing trip, standing, pedaling, and accessorizing the kayak to see what it can do—and I was impressed. If the name Sportsman PDL 120 Pro sounds familiar, that’s because it’s been in the Old Town lineup for some time. But this year Old Town completely redesigned the top half of this kayak and re-released it. The new model features horizontal rod tubes, a deeper and wider tankwell and cockpit, pre-set grommets for mounting motors and anchors, and more. The seat’s mounting system is different, too. Basically, everything from the water line up is different—and better. Here’s a closer look at the redesigned Old Town Sportsman PDL 120 Pro.

The top half of this boat has been completely redesigned with thinner walls and deeper wells to create more room for storage and operating space. The Sportsman PDL 120 Pro has a combined 150 inches of track, pre-cut through-hull wiring ports, pre-set holes for mounting motors and shallow water anchors, and three access hatches to get down into the hull. That means you can modify this boat however you want. Plus, the front hatch is twice as big as the previous design, and there are four horizontal rod tubes.

Old Town also redesigned the seat so the angler sits higher and has a better paddling angle. The seat sits on adjustable tracks, so you can slide it forward or back for a custom fit. Four knobs beneath the seat can be loosened and tightened from the seated position, so you can adjust the seat even on the water. A large rudder at the rear of the boat deploys with a lever to the right of the angler and is controlled with a knob to the left. Overall, the seating and controls are top-notch. The Sportsman PDL 120 Pro retails for $3,599.

I tested the Sportsman PDL 120 Pro on Thurlow Reservoir while targeting bass and catfish. I laid my bass rods in the rod tubes, with the reels to the sides of the seat. I put my tackle under the seat, my phone and keys in the little waterproof hatch on top of the PDL Drive console, and a large cooler and 14 floats in the rear tankwell.

I peddled about 6 miles during that trip, targeting bass for the first 5 hours. I liked being able to control the boat relatively hands-free with the PDL Drive while still actively fishing. I had to turn the rudder control knob, of course, when moving from spot to spot. But while fishing, I found I could backpedal if I drifted too far forward, and I could stop or correct myself when needed. The PDL Drive let me cover significantly more water than my traditional paddle kayak.

I stood up a couple of times and found the boat extremely stable in both scenarios. I was also really impressed with how sturdy and powerful the PDL Drive is. I could scoot along pretty well with this pedal drive system, and it didn’t rock back and forth like some others I’ve tried in the past. Towards the end of the trip, I baited up 14 floats and tossed them in the lake to try to catch catfish. That ended up being the most fun I had all day. As the floats started bobbing, I chased them down with the PDL Drive, giggling as I got close. I landed three solid fish and tossed them in the cooler to clean up for a buddy.

What really impressed me was how sturdy this boat is. I weigh 260 pounds, and leaning over to grab something out of the water is enough to topple many kayaks. But even when I stretched as far as I could to grab the float, the Old Town PDL 120 Pro didn’t budge, with the top of the gunwale staying several inches above the water. And, using the rudder and control knob, I was able to slip right up within inches of the floats, once I got the hang of it.

Installing the Minn Kota Kayak Terrova trolling motor on the Old Town 120 PDL Pro elevated this boat to an entirely different class of vessel—quite literally, since I had to register it at the courthouse after adding the motor. But practically speaking, the troller put this boat into a whole other league. While I’m not the biggest fan of installation projects like this, I found some great tutorial videos on Old Town’s website and dove in headfirst.

Mounting the motor itself was super easy, with pre-set mounting hardware built into the bow, specifically for the Kayak Terrova. Just line the Quick Release MKA-21 Bracket up and screw in the four bolts. This bracket is sold separately, but I recommend it so that you can take the motor on and off when loading the boat, to lighten the load. It’s also good to have it so you can detach the motor and set it in the truck or hotel room when on the road.

With the motor mounted, I bought a 6-gauge marine wire to run the length of the boat from the trolling motor to the battery—I put the battery in the stern to disperse the weight. The kit includes an optional relay board you can use to add a safety switch. I highly recommend doing this, even though it’s a little bit of a headache. The safety switch acts like the kill switch on an outboard motor and stops the Terrova instantly should you fall off.

After installing the safety switch near the seat and connecting all the appropriate wires to the relay, I was ready to hook up the battery, pair the motor to the included Micro Remote, and test it out. To test the motor, I took the boat to a local lake on a windy day. It blew 15 miles per hour, sustained throughout most of the day—the type of day I just wouldn’t have fished from a kayak if all I had was a paddle. Even the pedal drive would have been brutal. But with the motor, I could set a course into the wind and set my speed using the AutoPilot feature, or hit Spot-Lock and sit in place while I fished a point, brushpile, or dock. I even used the AutoPilot feature to slowly drift backward in strong current under the dam; I plowed my way upstream with the motor on high, then pointed my motor into the current, set it to about 40 percent, and slowly drifted backward to where I could fish without the motor raging at full bore.

The Terrova is phenomenal, plain and simple. It was a bit of a headache to install, but if I can do it, you can too. Stowing and deploying can be a bit cumbersome at first. I saw people use a rope around the shaft to lower the motor slowly and raise it back up, so I did that, and it worked well. I found my paddle to be the best tool in the boat for pushing the locking deploy/stow lever to send the motor out or lift it back up. The Lift-Assist feature also helps with deployment and stowing.

All things considered, the redesigned Old Town PDL 120 Pro is a great boat. It’s sturdy, has a fantastic layout, and the PDL Drive system is top-notch. If you’re into accessories, you can trick this boat out a hundred different ways. Stability is my biggest concern when kayak fishing, and this boat is among the most stable I’ve ever set foot in. I really value a good seat and seat positioning, too. The higher seat and better angle make it much easier to stand, stay seated longer, and fish all day. Being able to slide it forward and backward to find the sweet spot for pedaling was great, too. The bottom line is that the redesigned PDL 120 Pro is more comfortable, stable, and better equipped—which means more time on the water and more fish in the net.

Our writers and editors eat, sleep, and breathe the outdoors, and that passion comes through in our product reviews. You can count on F&S to keep you up to date on the best new gear. And when we write about a product—whether it’s a bass lure or a backpack—we cover the good and the bad, so you know exactly what to expect before you decide to make a purchase.

Shaye Baker is a bass fishing content contributor for Field & Stream. He has more than a decade of experience covering professional bass fishing tournaments. Highlights Education Baker graduated from Auburn University with a bachelor’s degree in Spanish and International Trade. However, the education he nows relies on in his daily occupation came by way of working alongside many of the greatest tournament coverage professionals the industry has ever seen, learning to shoot, write, edit, and add commentary in the real world. Experience Baker spent his first few years after college simultaneously pursuing a professional bass fishing career, as well as a career in outdoor journalism in the bass fishing space. After mixed success on the semi-pro fishing trail, Baker decided that working in the industry for a guaranteed paycheck was better than fishing for a possible one. Baker’s clientele base gradually grew to the point he could transition to full-time content contribution for the likes of FLW Outoors, Bassmaster, Wired2Fis,h and several other local, regional, and national publications. He now enjoys working full-time in the industry, but has also returned to his roots, regularly participating in local tournaments with his father and friends. A Veteran Bowhunter Finds Two Bucks Locked Together Deep Into Public Land. Should He Shoot Them?

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 3, 2026 2:09 PM EST

Get everything from whitetail news and opinion pieces, to advanced hunting tactics and cutting-edge gear reviews.

Robert Denninger was in a tree saddle the afternoon of October 23 when he spotted a deer in a creek bed about 75 yards away. He’d accessed the Oklahoma public hunting area by kayak, paddling up a creek to a remote spot that he’d pinpointed the previous day.

“I decided to slide down quietly with my bow and get a better look at the deer in the creek,” Denninger tells Outdoor Life. “When I got close to the creek I spotted two big mature bucks fighting and they were locked up tight by their antlers.”

He knew he was witnessing an epic outdoor event and he shot video of the fight on his phone.

“The bucks were plowing into each other and into the creek bank,” says Denninger, a 42-year-old retiree from Dallas. “Mud was flying everywhere. My mind was racing – would attempting to harvest these bucks be a hunter’s gift or a violation of fair chase?”

It was clear the bucks were not able to separate their antlers. Denninger struggled with what to do and pondered if it would be ethical and legal to shoot the larger of the two bucks, which was a 12-pointer with a typical rack. He recalled a friend telling him a story of once finding a pair of locked-antler bucks dead in a pond. They had been killed and half eaten by coyotes.

Before deciding what to do, Denninger called a game warden.

“I spoke with a local [Oklahoma] Game Warden who assured me he didn’t have any on-call vet who could have come out to sedate the animals and attempt a safe unlocking,” Denninger says.

“The thought of letting ‘nature run its course’ and coming back in the following days to spook a pack of coyotes off two rotten carcasses or trying my best to put an ethical kill on these animals were what I believed my choices were,” he explains. “I decided to shoot the 12-pointer.”

His first arrow was deflected by brush. But his second arrow found the deer’s heart, and it quickly died.

“I watched to see if the eight point could free himself, but he only struggled more, barely able to move the weight of the harvested 12-pointer,” the bowhunter says.

Denninger had no way of freeing the deer, so he decided to shoot the second buck with his bow. Even when both deer were dead, it was impossible to unlock their antlers.

That’s when he called a couple younger buddies, who eventually arrived with a wheeled wagon.

“We tried to unlock their racks many times, and were unable to,” Denninger says. “We field dressed them. But it was a rugged haul to get the two bucks out, taking well over three hours to get them to a truck and out of the woods. It was almost midnight [by the time we were done].”

He called his Texas friend and taxidermist Dustin Moon and told him about the locked-antler bucks and his hunting tale.

“Dustin and his wife Stephanie of New Story Taxidermy drove five hours (on their sixth wedding anniversary) to where I was so they could see the bucks and help my deer processor get their antlers unlocked.”

Eventually they succeeded and the deer were separated. They will be mounted, though Denninger doesn’t know if he’ll have them in a locked-horn mount or separate mounts.

The 12-pointer is believed to be 7.5 years old and has a rough green score of 152 inches. It’s the biggest buck Denninger has taken during his dedicated bowhunting career. The 8-pointer is thought to be 5.5 years old and scores 132 inches. Both bucks have typical racks.

“I think what I did was the most ethical thing I could have done with my two Oklahoma buck tags, especially considering the alternative of having them suffer and die in their struggle, or have coyotes kill them.”

Bob McNally has been an outdoor writer since shortly after the earth’s crust cooled. He has written 12 outdoor books, more than 5,000 outdoor magazine stories (including many for Outdoor Life) and more newspaper outdoor columns and features than there are hairs on a grizzly bear.

As A Therapist, I Thought Caregiver Burnout Wouldn’t Come For Me. I Was Wrong.

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

The sandwich generation is sorely lacking in support.

Tuesday, 8:10 p.m. I’m helping my 87-year-old mother brush her teeth and change for bed. My 10-year-old calls from the room next door that he and his dad are done reading his latest Big Nate book. He asks (again) when I’m coming to his room to say goodnight. I try not to rush my mother, but I’m exhausted after a long day, and keenly aware of missing those precious minutes right before bedtime when you get the real deal from your child about everything that happened that day.

After months of juggling my work as a therapist with caring for my aging mother (who has been diagnosed with dementia), and being a present, engaged mom and wife, I had to admit that I was dropping some balls. Having all of this on my plate was clearly not working.

I knew I was not alone — I’ve been having this conversation with many friends and clients, also in their 40s or early 50s. In fact, 47% of caregivers under the age of 50 are part of the sandwich generation, those of us who are raising children and caring for aging parents at the same time. If we aren't talking about perimenopause with all its patches and pills, we’re talking about the health concerns of our parents and, potentially, our grandparents as well.

The burnout I was feeling falls particularly heavily on the shoulders of women. Even if you have siblings, most of the caregiving responsibility consistently falls on one person, and that person is typically a woman. Women shoulder the majority of caregiving responsibilities, often to the detriment of their careers. To make matters worse, some caregivers may have a complicated relationship with the parent who is now dependent on them.

Caring for your parent can be immensely rewarding and depressing. Sometimes I feel deeply honored to care for the person who dedicated her life to caring for me. Other times, my mom can’t remember why her beloved husband hasn’t come home yet — my father passed away several years ago. She really doesn’t enjoy having to be bathed by her child, for that matter.

As a mental health professional who has sat with clients going through this very thing, I expected to have more of the answers, to be able to cope and balance more than someone else due to my experience. But the truth is, nothing can truly prepare you for your induction into the sandwich generation.

No matter how we try to overcome the difficulty of caregiving as individuals, we must acknowledge that there’s minimal financial or systemic help for consistent care for the elderly living at home. Some Medicare programs can provide all your healthcare in one place, which is obviously better than driving all over town talking to doctors who aren’t talking to each other. But they often only offer last-minute daily help and, because of the high need in our country, you typically see a new caregiver each day. While better than nothing, it’s clearly not a great situation for a parent who is already confused in their day-to-day life. Some programs actually pay you, the caregiver, for your work, but that doesn’t give you back the missed moments with your own children or restore those career opportunities.

Eventually my husband and I realized that we needed help. Fortunately, we could afford to pay a nurse out of pocket, but the costs have been steep: tens of thousands of dollars annually to have carers for my mother seven days a week, and that’s with me still doing bedtime duty on the weekends. It’s simply not realistic or sustainable for most people.

And one of the challenges that we don’t often talk about is the guilt. Even if you are able to defy the odds and pay someone to come in to help, you still feel guilty about it and get caught up in the “shoulds.” I should be able to care for my parents and my children at the same time; people have been doing this forever with a lot less help. I should be grateful to be able to spend whatever time I have left with my parent. The important part missing from that is the fact that many of us don’t have villages in the way we used to — that nearby family network that would support each other during all the stages of life.

Our lack of a village came into focus for me recently when we adopted a baby into our family. She has brought us more joy than we could have imagined, and I see how much a community would change this experience for us. In my Mommy and Me classes, so many of the young mothers have discussed having their parents come and stay with them during the early weeks of having a newborn. What a stark contrast for me as a mother, in a different phase in my life, where I am taking care of my parent, rather than her taking care of me and my children.

This is a problem that is not going anywhere. Around the country and the world, people are making tough decisions every day — draining bank accounts, missing out on kids’ milestones, declining promotions and date nights with their partner — to take care of a loved one. The physical, financial, and emotional toll can cause extensive damage to the caregiver’s well-being in so many ways.

We also know what to do about this problem. Studies show that while caregiver burnout is a worldwide issue, it is less so in the countries where (surprise, surprise) there is a strong public healthcare system, flexible work environments for caregivers, and affordable private health insurance. These kinds of safeguards can be a key part of rebuilding a new kind of village and community support. The answer is there. The question is if we will take heed before we all burn out.

Dr. Patrice Le Goy is a licensed marriage and family therapist and international psychologist. She is also the research chair of Maternal Mental Health Now and the board chair of Open Paths Counseling Center, as well as the host of Like-Minded with Dr. Patrice, an award-winning mental health podcast.

Patrice lives in Los Angeles with her husband, two children, and their massive dog who is definitely part wolf. You can follow her on social media @drpatricelegoy.

Your Apple Watch has 2 new controversial listening features – but you have to opt in

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Siri Recap and Live Rewind are finally here, two months after Apple unveiled the always-listening Apple Watch features. Here's how they work.

Siri Recap and Live Rewind, Apple’s two controversial, always-listening Audio Intelligence features, are now available on the Apple Watch Series 12 and Apple Watch Ultra 4 with the iOS 27.3 developer beta. Both features are opt-in.

Apple unveiled Siri Recap and Live Rewind in September alongside its Series 12 and Ultra 4 smartwatches, but it waited to release the features until after the smartwatches launched. Siri Recap provides a high-level summary of meetings, while Live Rewind continuously buffers audio so the watch wearer can pull up a transcript of the last 15 seconds.

Also: Apple’s Audio Intelligence is priming us for its real AI wearable future

To try both features today, update your iPhone to the iOS 27.3 developer beta, and then update your Apple Watch.

Within the Siri app, users will find a new section called “Recaps,” where both the Siri Recaps and Live Rewinds live.

Also: Apple Watch 12 can listen in and recap your conversations now – genius or privacy nightmare?

Siri Recap isn’t listening for nitty-gritty details within a meeting or recording a transcript’s worth of text. It’s more geared toward a conversation’s key takeaways, like important dates, times, prices, and action items. A demo of the feature showed an off-the-cuff conversation between a parent and a child discussing college application strategies. Siri Recap took notes on the high school activities the student could pursue to include in their application, the topics they could write about in their essay, and the colleges they could consider applying to.

After a conversation, a user can save the meeting notes in the Siri app or export them to Notes or Journal. If they don’t save the conversation, it’s automatically deleted after seven days.

Apple designed the feature to exclude certain information from the Siri Recap. It includes a person’s name only if they introduce themselves or if their name is regularly brought up in conversation. Otherwise, it avoids identifying speakers. It also scrubs the audio of language that promotes self-harm, financial and authentication data, and personal identifiers.

You can enable or disable Recaps at any time through Control Center. You can create something akin to a Focus mode to automatically enable the feature once your Apple Watch detects you’ve reached your office or college lecture. Each morning, Siri Recap sends a notification to alert you that the feature is on. At the end of each day, you get a reminder to check your daily recaps in the Siri app.

The feature constantly assesses whether a conversation is relevant, optimizing its detection based on the conversation’s context, proximity, and length. It pauses summarization when a user is on a phone or FaceTime call, plays music, or uses earbuds. It won’t work when Bluetooth devices like AirPods are in use.

Double-press the Digital Crown to see a transcript of the past 15 seconds of conversation. The Apple Watch chimes and displays an animation to indicate it’s in use, then shows the transcript for 30 seconds.

Also: Is cellular on Apple Watch worth it? 4 ways people use it – and why I opt out

Live Rewind is built for those head-in-the-clouds moments when you miss a tidbit of information and can’t go back and ask what a speaker said. I think about how useful this could be in a college lecture or boardroom setting, where dozens of people’s conversations and questions prevent someone from asking a clarifying question about what was discussed.

The user can send the snippet to the Siri app to save it or ask about it (if they want to look up the definition of a mentioned term or get more information on the history of a mentioned topic, for example). If the user doesn’t act on it, the snippet is deleted. Any snippet you save is encrypted through iCloud. The audio buffer updates continuously, and unused audio is automatically deleted. It’s processed on the device and not sent to Apple.

To some, an always-on, always-listening smartwatch can sound like a privacy breach waiting to happen. That’s a fair concern, and Apple has taken steps to address privacy and security concerns with its Secure Exclave.

Think of the Secure Exclave as a private island on the S11 chip where the Apple Watch Series 12 and Ultra 4 process audio. A secure audio buffer processes the raw audio, and a secure pairing mechanism privately enables the connection between a paired Apple Watch and iPhone.

Also: The Apple Watch Ultra 4 just hit its lowest price ever ahead of Black Friday

For Siri Recap, the S11 chip listens to detect whether the watch wearer is part of a current conversation. Once detected, audio enters the Secure Exclave, where it’s encrypted and sent to the Secure Exclave on the iPhone. The iPhone turns that audio into condensed text, essentially editing the conversation to remove filler words or irrelevant information, and then it’s sent to private cloud compute. Private cloud compute creates a recap, delivered to your iPhone’s Siri app.

This process ensures that no one, including Apple, can access the audio data.

Don’t want your Apple Watch to listen to you? Siri Recap and Live Rewind are off by default, according to Apple.

If your Apple Watch prompts you to enable Audio Intelligence during setup, you can choose which features to turn on.

On its support page, Apple said you can “turn on Siri Recap in the Watch app on iPhone.” Once it’s enabled, you can “turn it on or off at any time from Control Center.” To enable Live Rewind, Apple said to “open the Watch app, tap Siri, then tap Live Rewind and turn on ‘Double Click Digital Crown.’”

I haven’t tested these controls myself. I’d expect you can follow the same steps to switch Live Rewind off, but I haven’t confirmed that yet.

Christina Bowlby Finds Inspiration Close to Home for Bentley Mills

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

For textile designer Christina Bowlby, inspiration can come from an unexpected place: the warm, heathered coats of her two Aussiedoodles, Birdie and Maggie. The dogs have become unlikely muses for Coming Home, a new carpet collection from Bentley Mills that coincides with Bowlby’s first year as the company’s senior director of design—and her return to California.

Christina Bowlby with her Aussiedoodles, Birdie and Maggie. Photography courtesy of Bentley Mills.

Bowlby and her two dogs relocated to Los Angeles nearly a year ago, trading Chattanooga, Tennessee, for the West Coast. It’s a homecoming for Bowlby, who grew up in California. Although she quickly fell in love with the South after a day trip and a ghost tour, she always imagined returning west.

Originally a film major at the Savannah College of Art and Design (SCAD), she discovered her interest in textiles almost by accident. “I took a fiber 101 class, and it changed my life,” she says. She entered the carpet industry upon graduation and never looked back.

“I thought Chattanooga was my forever home,” she recalls, but an opportunity at Bentley changed her plans. “The food, the culture, the people of LA—there is always something to do. The beach, mountains, of course Bentley; it has everything.”

Birdie and Maggie have settled into their new surroundings, too— their coats soon found their way into Bowlby’s design work. As she researched color trends for the new collection, she began noticing shades that reminded her of the dogs’ distinctive coloring.

Two of the collection’s four colorways draw directly from the Aussiedoodles. Birdie’s warm golden coat inspired Cactus Wren, named for a bird native to the American Southwest, while Maggie’s softer mix of colors informed Pure Magic, a nod to the dog’s full name.

The other two colorways, Arroyo Moon and Old Pasadena, reference the Southern California landscape. Collectively, the earthy neutral palette draws on sun-washed sands, stone, clay-infused taupes, and muted moss. Drawing on Bowlby’s experience in hospitality design, the collection favors warmth and tonal complexity over sharp contrast, with an emphasis on creating spaces that feel calm and comfortable.

The collection’s early mood boards reinforce that direction, pairing lived-in interiors and natural textures with images of coastal landscapes. Warm woods, soft furnishings, and muted colors establish a relaxed, residential sensibility that carries through to the finished carpets.

“The yarns for this collection—Helix® premium nylon 6,6 and Revolve® technologies from Universal Fibers—were such a good vehicle for translating [the color inspiration] because it has this heathered, wool-like quality,” Bowlby explains. “I was able to choose the specific hues and combine them in a way that looks natural. They were the perfect match for this collection.”

The heathered yarns allow multiple hues to mingle within a single surface, creating tonal variation reminiscent of natural fibers. This approach is particularly suited to Bowlby’s palette, where closely related shades of beige, taupe, gray, and brown create depth without relying on strong color contrasts.

The patterns introduce another layer of visual texture. Fragmented linear forms, overlapping markings, and irregular geometric elements create movement across the surface, while variations in density give the designs a sense of dimension. The result is a collection that feels visually layered without overwhelming its subdued palette.

The Coming Home palette is intended to feel enduring rather than tied to a particular moment. Bowlby credits this approach to her habit of noticing color in everyday surroundings, even when the source seems unremarkable. She recalls taking roughly 15 photographs of a stone wall to capture its variations for future design work. “It had the most beautiful range of warm neutrals I’ve seen in a while,” she says.

That attention to overlooked details connects Bowlby’s personal inspirations with her work at Bentley, where familiar surroundings can become the starting point for something new. “I share that ‘wink and a nod’ approach to design and life,” she says of Bentley.

As for Maggie and Birdie, their modeling careers may have peaked with their carpet-colorway debuts. For now, they’re back to hiking, beach runs, and making friends wherever they go. If you happen to spot them on Venice Beach, don’t be surprised if Birdie comes over to say hello.

From Drifting Lights to Crystal Grid, Preciosa’s extensive design portfolio brings the craft to customizable crystal.

Van Cleef &amp; Arpels Announces Support for Montecito’s Lotusland

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

The jewelry house heads to Santa Barbara with a fitting gift from a brand captivated by botanic motifs.

Above: The Rose Garden Terrace at Lotusland in Montecito, CA.

Ganna Walska was a lifelong collector. First, there were the husbands: six of them, from a Russian baron to a yoga instructor. Then came the jewelry: hundreds of carats of emeralds, diamonds, and sapphires. Finally, there were plants, her love of which ultimately led her to create a 37-acre botanical garden in Montecito, California.

Walska, an opera singer born Hanna Puacz in Poland in 1887, purchased the Cuesta Linda estate in 1941. She intended to both live there and house Tibetan monks, but after her final divorce (and after the monks failed to show), Walska devoted herself to collecting rare plants. She renamed the property Lotusland, for the Indian lotus in one of the ponds, and poured her fortune into the project. In 1971 she auctioned off many of her jewels to fund her gardens. One piece was the Oiseau clip from Van Cleef & Arpels, a special order depicting a bird in flight carrying a 96.62-carat yellow diamond known as the “Walska Briolette.”

Following her death, the property was opened to the public, and now Van Cleef is making a return. The maison has announced its support of Lotusland, focusing in particular on the Rose Garden Terrace. It’s a gift that honors both Walska’s love of the brand and the inspiration that Van Cleef has pulled from nature for a century, from countless floral variations to the butterfly motif. Jewels will once again endow Lotusland—just as Ganna would have wanted.

This story appears in the November 2026 issue of ELLE Decor.

Annie Goldsmith is the senior editor and digital lead at ELLE Decor, where she covers design, culture, style, and trends. She previously held positions at The Information, covering technology and culture, and Town & Country, writing about news, entertainment, and fashion. Her work has also appeared in Vogue, Rolling Stone, and the SF Standard.

Court won’t intervene in California ballot dispute

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Type to search articles, cases, and authors.

It’s Election Day in the United States. At SCOTUSblog, we’ll be watching not only for race results, but also for new voting-related legal battles. In recent weeks, the Supreme Court has addressed several emergency applications linked to the midterms, and the justices received – and decided – another one on Monday on alleged noncitizen voting in California.

On Monday, the Trump administration returned to the Supreme Court’s interim docket, asking the justices to intervene in its efforts to keep California from counting ballots cast by what it contends are noncitizen voters. Just hours later, the court denied the request. There were no noted dissents. For more on the dispute, see the On Site section below.

Also on Monday, the justices heard argument in two cases: Maxwell v. Dinis, on a federal inmate’s ability to use a habeas statute, 28 U.S.C. § 2241, to contest the Federal Bureau of Prisons’ refusal to accelerate his transfer to a halfway house or home confinement; and Younge v. Fulton Judicial Circuit District Attorney’s Office, Georgia, on whether an affirmative defense may be asserted as a basis for summary judgment if not presented earlier in a pleading.

On Monday afternoon, the court released its calendar for the January argument session, which will begin on Monday, Jan. 11. Learn about the session in the On Site section.

Today, the justices will hear argument in St. Mary Catholic Parish v. Roy, a religious discrimination challenge brought by Catholic preschools in Colorado over the state’s requirement that all schools participating in its universal preschool program must admit everyone – including LGBTQ children and children with LGBTQ parents.

Tomorrow, the justices will hear argument in Guerrero v. Johnson, on the limited circumstances in which a state prisoner can return to federal court for a second round of post-conviction review.

On Oct. 21, the Trump administration asked the justices to allow it to resume construction of its border wall in the Big Bend region of southwestern Texas. The request is now fully briefed, and the court’s decision could come at any time.

Kelsey Dallas is SCOTUSblog's managing editor and the primary author of the SCOTUStoday newsletter.
